## D: double up–dyno

### double up
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) a design of cowshed where two cows stand side-by-side to be milked.

>  Dairy farm - 20 A/S double up shed (40 cups.) <i>(Oct 17, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

### double velvet fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Leptopteris superba</i> ?Prince of Wales feathers

>  <b>POTTS</b> (And) <i>Leptopteris superba</i> double velvet fern <i>(1882,Out in the Open</i>)

### double(s) chart
 
 <i>n.</i> a chart showing horses in the two races on which a doubles bet may be placed; to collect both the chosen horses must win [(bookmakers') also doubles card]

>  Currently, 'Erb is earning three feeds a day by passing a double-chart around the assembled barflies. <i>(Jan 8, 1947,NZ Observer,16</i>)

>  Among the material were two doubles cards at 2/- for GBP5. <i>(Aug 24, 1939,Taranaki Herald,5</i>)

>  <b>BELTON</b> In addition to [day cards] the bookmakers have `doubles charts'. <i>(1939,Outside the Law,24</i>)

>  Another printed double chart was foundwhich was obviously issued by a big scalebookmaker. <i>(Aug 23, 1939,Taranaki Herald,4</i>)

>  He had seen men, on race days.. consulting double charts on the footpath. <i>(Aug 28, 1926,Press, The,8</i>)

### double-banded plover
 
 <i>n.</i> = banded dotterel

>  <i>Banded Dotterel Charadrius bicinctus</i>JARDINE & SELBY, 1827 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Pohowera, tuturiwhatu, double-banded plover.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,180</i>)

### double-banger
 
 <i>n.</i> something which is bi-partite; here, a lottery with two separate prizes to be won with the same ticket

>  Double-banger winner elusive [heading] Golden Kiwi lottery organisers may spend several thousands dollars searching for the winner of a [dollars]150,000 unclaimed prize in November's double banger Melbourne Cup sweepstake. <i>(Apr 10, 1986,Evening Post,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a large cracker which allegedly makes twice the bang of the ordinary double happy. [(children's) ca1930 J. Winchester (Wellington) -->ca1940 Havelock children]

>  Once a "double-banger" was thrown throughan open window, and its explosion causeda commotion in the crowd. <i>(Nov 6, 1928,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> There would be the bonfire.. and double-bangers dancing as they double-banged. <i>(1983,Springtime in Taranaki,78</i>) [NOTES: 1991 edition]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a cracker

>  <b>GRAY</b> `Giving you your bit is she, then? She'd be a double banger I bet. The quiet ones always are. ... Simon blushed ...`She's all right,' he said gruffly. `She's a good joker.' <i>(1987,Stepping Out,59</i>)

### double-bank
 
 <i>v.</i> of horse-traction, to hitch on a second team in difficult or steep places. [to double (up), orig. (so AND) of rowers either in pairs or two to an oar.]]

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> There the teams double-banked when they tackled the hill - ... <i>(1897,Station Ballads,44,1945 ed.</i>) [NOTES: `At the Foot of the Old Dunstan Track']



 
 <i>v.</i> carry a second person on horseback or bicycle

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The doctor came to the north bank and left his horse, and he and [Jimmy] the Needle double-banked across. The Needle was washed off and the doctor rode to the hut. He must have been `a bit on' to leave the other in the river. ... he was asked `Where's the Needle?' He replied `In Hell!' <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,178</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> As children they had walked along it to school, before they had a pony, on which they`double-banked'. <i>Ibid.</i> 52 They were ready and waiting when the three children returned from school, riding one behind the other on a fat Shetland pony. They still called it `double-banking' though young Nicholas had been added to the load at the beginning of the year. <i>(1948,Dusty Road,42,1949 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Ruefully he noticed a pal ... sitting on his well-groomed chestnut galloway. `Reg,' called Jim, `double bank me to the start, the blankey drag has gone'. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,178</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... and as the horse was too touchy to `double bank,' the shepherd had to leave the cook ... and head ... for another horse. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,105</i>)

>  double-bank vb. Austral. inf. to carry a second person on (a horse, bicycle, etc.). <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,333</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 224; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 144]]

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> My brother Jack made a scene when he discovered I was to double-bank on his horse. <i>(1987,Swagger on Our Doorstep,85</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SCANLON</b> `I offered Mrs. Swift a double-bank [on a horse], and she wouldn't come,' Whitton whispered in Trix's ear ... <i>(1948,Dusty Road,75,1949 ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to share a horse with another rider.

>  Some were on foot, some what is called 'double-banking.' <i>(Jun 2, 1883,Weekly News,6</i>)

>  Buggies in those days were almost unknown, and fully half the visitors arrived on horseback, many of the younger class 'double-banking.' <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,26</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> And as the horse was too touchy to 'double-bank,' the shepherd had to leave the cook .. and head .. for another horse. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,105</i>)

>  <b>HUTCHINSON</b> An old grey mare was the first pre-school teacher and, as each boy graduated to more spirited pony, the next youngest had to double-bank behind the saddle. <i>(1981,Hutchinsons of New Zealand, The,44</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> He bought them a horse and told them to double-bank to school with the other riders. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,75</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> carry a second person on (usu. the bar of) a bicycle; see also double, dub; double-dink [ca1920 W.H.B. Orsman (Havelock)]

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> As children they had walked along it to school, before they had a pony, on which they `double-banked'. <i>(1934,Winds of Heaven,42</i>)

>  Of course you soon learned to double-bank and take your girl-friend for a spin on a Saturdayafternoon. <i>(Sep 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,90</i>)

>  Of course you soon learned to double-bank and take your girl-friend for a spin on a Saturdayafternoon. <i>(Sep 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,90</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> In various transf. uses.a. To fight, two men against one.

>  And the growlers double-bank him/ In the way they alway [sic] fight. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,86</i>)

>  <b>PETERSON</b> One of my earliest recollections is of watching [?c1890s-1900s] ... one 5th November, while the boys let off their crackers.. One double banger seemed a dud. <i>(1981,Glasshouses,7</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> `I have to inform you ... that I engaged myself to two gentlemen last night and I don't want either of them. ...' ... `Two gentlemen. Double-banked. ...' <i>(1938,Promenade,134</i>)

### double-banked
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Two boys.. who were riding home fromschool "double-banked," collided with atelegraph pole; and one of them..sustained serious injuries to his head,which proved fatal. <i>(May 3, 1917,Stratford Evening Post,3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adv.</i> (Rural) two to a horse.

>  <b>JOHNSTONE-SMITH</b> We were riding double-banked when we met a Maori. <i>(1975,Battles, Buggies and Babies,69</i>)

### double-banking
 
 <i>n.</i> = doubling [three, perhaps all four, of the citations below belong with the verb to double-bank (TD)]

>  May Taylor and another girl were "double-banking" down a hill on their way toschool. <i>(Apr 11, 1917,Otago Witness,23</i>)

>  [Hdg] Double-banking on motor cycles. <i>(Apr 1, 1916,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  I saw numbers of children on their way tothe public school... Some of them were onfoot, some what is called "double-banking" on their pony. <i>(Jun 2, 1883,Auckland Weekly News,6</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> Times were harder still for many of their pupils, especially farmers' children from the backblocks, double-banking on a barebacked horse. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,29</i>)

### double-bar
 
 <i>v.</i> double-bank [ca1939 Havelock schoolboys]

### double-bottoming
 
 <i>n.</i> the theoretical ability to strike two layers of paydirt, one below the other in a claim. [gold mining]

>  Unless new discoveries - for double-bottoming still appears to be a myth - of which no [46] hint has as yet been given, are made ... the approach of winter will find Gabriel's Gully deserted ... <i>(Oct 16, 1861,NZ Goldfields,45,1976 ed.</i>)

### double-bunk
 
 <i>v.</i> to sleep two to a (Maori) bunk in a tramping hut. [(trampers') Usu. as vbl n double-bunking]

>  Huts ... Double bunk if necessary to permit all to have a bed. <i>(1984,Story of Nelson Lakes National Park, The,154</i>)

### double-bunking
 
 <i>n.</i> [(trampers') ca1945 at least H. Gretton - Trampers' Song in VUW Songbook There'll be no more double-bunking ... for me.]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> JOHN (Sings) ... No more double-bunking double-bunking, double-bunking,/ No more double-bunking, double-bunking for me. <i>(1982,Collected Plays,285</i>) [NOTES: (Mr O'Dwyer's Dancing Party)]

### double-decked
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stock trucks and loading ramps] having upper and lower levels.

>  Double-decked cattle loading ramps and drafting areas have also been incorporated into this [saleyard] site to increase efficiency. <i>(Nov 7, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)

### double-decker
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep with several years' growth of wool. (See [hermit; double-fleecer](../H#hermit;-double-fleecer))

>  We are all familiar with the 'double-decker' fleece. <i>(Oct 11, 1951,NZ Farmer,7</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The mob were started over [the ford] but the double-deckers, those with two years' wool, ran into bother ... <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,25</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shearing shed of two floors, usually with night pens beneath the shearing board.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. a double-decker capable of holding 2,000 woolly ewes and somewhat unusual in design, it is a striking building and a lovely shed. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,60</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> An eight-stand double-decker, it is a beauty and worth going to see. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,67</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a stock truck with both upper and lower tray or deck, often carrying both cattle and sheep.

>  <b>WATERS</b> It [the truck] was a double-decker and divided into 16 pens, each pen to hold six fat lambs or five adult sheep. <i>(1976,Mostly a Farmer,109</i>)

>  <b>WASWO</b> The modern livestock truck will carry 600 fat or 750 early lambs. A double decker can accommodate 35 big oxen. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,80</i>)

>  This was the first truck in New Zealand to carry a double-decker sheep crate in about June 1922. <i>(2000,Wool Grower,Autumn 28</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Some of the double-deckers [sc. trams] run on the long routes... All the provision that is made for smokers on double-decker cars is needed. <i>(Mar 30, 1912,NZ Free Lance,15</i>)

>  These trams are all what we call double deckers, and it is the fashion in Christchurch for the ladies to ride on the top instead of inside, so that on a summer's day these vehicles present a very gay and flower-like appearance <i>(Apr 24, 1890,Otago Witness</i>)

>  The double-decker must go. <i>(Mar 12, 1904,NZ Observer,2</i>)

>  Whatever the Company may think of the recommendations to abolish the double-decker cars, the proposal is one that finds considerable sympathy with the travelling public. <i>(Jan 16, 1904,NZ Observer,2</i>)

>  Another treat was the ride on a double-decker tram to Island Bay. <i>(Jun 2001,NZ Memories,No. 30/26</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> double fleecer [(farmers')]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The mob were started over, but the double deckers, [26] those with two years' wool, ran into bother on account of the weight of the wool, and started drifting downstream. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,25</i>)

>  <b>LETHBRIDGE</b> A hermit [sheep] must be distinguished from a `double-decker', which is simply a sheep that had missed the muster once and consequently had a double layer of wool. <i>(1971,Sunrise on the Hills,95</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Up in the mountains, young Roy Mytton so proudly succeeded in rounding up twenty-odd old hermits, defiant woolly sheep also known as `double deckers', which had been wily enough to dodge last season's muster. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,80</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> In the wool boom in 1951 an orchardist in Hamilton had five "double-decker" sheep in his orchard (they'd missed one shearing and had a double crop of wool)... Being double deckers each sheep clipped close to twenty pounds. <i>(1967,Return to Open Country,163</i>)

### double-dumped
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  ... the double-dumped scoured wool was given an extra passage through the willey. <i>(1949,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,30/170</i>)

### double-ender
 
 <i>n.</i> a double-ended dinghy, that is one with two `bows'. [ca1910 W.H.B. Orsman, Marlborough Sounds and elsewhere.]

### double-fleece
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>DICK</b> . We're shearing the double-fleece stragglers.' <i>(1964,High Country Family,70</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> Most of the sheep went on, except about fifty wild and double-fleece sheep that were put in a scrubyard. <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,58</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of a sheep] carrying twice the usual amount of wool.

>  <b>HALL</b> Got some double-fleece rams off The Mistake. <i>(Jul 7, 1868,Letter,243</i>) [NOTES: in Vance (1965) High Endeavour]

>  <b>MEEK</b> If I don't get these sheep shorn they will all be double-fleece. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,22</i>)

>  <b>DICK</b> We're shearing the double-fleece stragglers. <i>(1964,High Country Family,70</i>)

### double-fleeced
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  After a number of years, when sheep were mustered in from the back ranges, it was noticed that several would die in the yards ..., for they would require to be kept in for drafting on the following day. These dead sheep would be mostly sheep which had missed a shearing, and were double-fleeced, having very long wool. <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/278</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] having have missed one or more shearings.

>  Looking out for double-fleeced sheep. <i>(Nov 1869,Balfour Diaries,29</i>)

>  Double-fleeced sheep were the chief sufferers, the bird [kea] getting hold on the longer wool. <i>(Jan 1906,NZ Farmer,53</i>)

### double-fleecer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep that has missed at least one shearing. (See [double-decker](dou-dyn.md#double-decker))

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> We lived on wild pig, wild beef shot on Kaiwaka, and the fat wild sheep and double-fleecers that could be raked in from river cliffs ... <i>(1921,Tutira,221</i>) [NOTES: Tutira III (1999)]

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> If your dogs worked well, it was a good day, and if you came back with a glow of pride to tell how the little bitch caught a mob of "double-fleecers" down the back spur and fetched them right round onto the front face. <i>(1960,Tall Tussock,45</i>)

>  <b>VANCE</b> Waters snow-raked a mob of twenty double-fleecers out of the high basin of Separation Creek. <i>(1965,High Endeavour,189</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> If amongst them there are some "woollies" or "double-fleecers" which had escaped in previous musters the triumph is greater still. <i>(1972,Alone in a Mountain World,5</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> Double fleecers are more tricky, as they are likely to have had some real practice in deliberately escaping the muster. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,86</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> As we mustered the various blocks, all the double-fleecers had been kept and driven through the rivers. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,57</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> On the missing man's property 18 months later a musterer chased a double-fleecer under a rock that hung over a stream. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,130</i>)

### double-gate
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a drafting race with two gates so that the sheep can be run into any one of three check-pens.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> double-gate: referring to a drafting race having two gates so that the sheep can be run into any one of three check-pens. Very occasionally one may find a triple-gate race; and many saleyards boast, halfway along the race, a second pair of gates for another man. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,26</i>)

### double-headed Jack
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) a double-headed coin; aka double-header

>  <b>HYDE</b> ... Wallie Black ... was said to spin a penny or a double-headed Jack better than any other man in the N.Z.E.F. ... <i>Ibid.</i> 60 However, while he had his double-headed Jack the cupboard never went bare. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,59</i>)

### double-header
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A double-header is the term for catching two fish on different hooks at the same time. <i>(Jun 2, 1949,Southern Cross (Wgton)</i>) [NOTES: check year and page]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> `He began drinkin' double 'eaders. ... Then he goes on drinking these double 'eaders, but he don't get noisy, just ... glaring round the bar ...' <i>(1957,Strip Jack Naked,111</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `One for the road. But make mine a double. A double-header of rum.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,102</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Peter passed the rum to Horse ...`It's a double-header,' he said. `One for the road.' <i>(1985,Horse,25</i>) [NOTES: c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Name and the Game) 25]

>  <b>HARVEY</b> Fancy watching grown-ups standing around drinking those things like double-headers and spots and hang-overs Dad was always talking about. <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,37</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> `Whisky!' `Yes, double headers. A lemonade for Mr. Brindle.' <i>(1965,Rhetoric at the Red Dawn,194</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Of particular interest ... was the fact that the boat had a liquor licence. ... Singles soon turned to `double-headers' as we headed into the ... semi-darkness of the canyons. ... [The liquor] fired [the Maoris'] imagination ... Panic and fear is contagious - it was not long before several of the gang became `pannikin struck'. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,124</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a double-headed coin; see also double-headed jack, grey. [(gamblers') ca1941 St Patrick's College, Silverstream.]

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> Sometimes two pennies are filed down to half their thickness and then joined together. A double-tailer is called a 'grey'; a double-header a 'nob. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,163</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a two-day muster.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> double-header: a two-day muster. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,26</i>)

### double-hits
 
 <i>n.</i> [(child marble players')]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

### double-picking
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Sometimes we'd lift our [crayfish] pots twice in the same day - double-picking we called it. <i>(1998,On the Next Tide,91</i>)

### double-ridger
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a special cultivator used in soft or swampy areas.

>  .. with the introduction of double ridgers, this [ridging] is gradually being overcome ... <i>(Dec 1904,NZ Farmer,945</i>)

>  The seed (2lb of kale and half lb of turnip seed to the acre) and manure were planted through the double-ridger. <i>(May 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,380</i>)

### double-shear
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to shear twice in twelve months. (See [second-shear](sa-sho.md#second-shear))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Otui double-shore that year and Bob was there for practically the whole season. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,85</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. the practice has been to double-shear everything ... <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,156</i>)

### double-striper
 
 <i>n.</i> type of large sack marked with two longitudinal stripes

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> He would fill up about eight or ten massive wheat sacks we used to call 'double stripers' with crays and sew them along the top. He'd get 2/6 for a sack. That was back in the late 1920s through till about 1935. <i>(1998,On the Next Tide,14</i>)

### double-tailer
 
 <i>n.</i> (coin)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> Sometimes two pennies are filed down to half their thickness and then joined together. A double-tailer is called a 'grey'; a double-header a 'nob. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,163</i>)

### double-yolker
 
 <i>n.</i> a stupid person, buffoon; cf egg. [(prisoners')]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> I was very annoyed and went around referring to Kapua as a weak mug and a double-yolker. <i>Ibid.</i> 248 DOUBLE-YOLKER. (n) Stupid person, buffoon. See also EGG. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,197</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an egg with two yolks

>  Better still if it turned up on the plate as a perfectly poached double-yolker. <i>(May 2000,Cuisine,No. 80 : 7</i>) [NOTES: Commonly heard in the 1930s]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ewe gravid with twins.

>  a ewe known to be carrying twin lambs - a double-yolker. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

>  Dry humour runs thick through through this collection; for example .. double-yolker - a ewe carrying twins. <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion,C6</i>)

### doublebeak
 
 <i>n.</i> needlefish, <i>Scomberesox forsteri</i>

>  <b>MORELAND</b> NEEDLEFISH [<i>Scomberesox forsteri</i>] ... Other names are ocean piper, skipper, saury, and doublebeak. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,16</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> NEEDLEFISH ... Other names: <i>Scomberesox forsteri</i>; saury, skipper, double beak, ocean garfish, ocean piper. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,205</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,44</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] doublebeak 975 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### doubled-up
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of a milking shed] having two sets of cups per pair of bails.

>  <b>MCMEEKAN</b> .. there are two lines of walk-through sheds, the single and the doubled-up. The single has one set of cups for each pair of bails, while the doubled-up has two sets of cups per pair. <i>(1960,Grass to Milk,152</i>)

>  The ideal number of sets of cups for each milker in a doubled-up herringbone is six. <i>(Apr 11, 1968,NZ Farmer,23</i>)

### doubled-up shed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Milking.. is carried on in a 20-bail doubled-up shed in which 2 men handle 9 sets of cups each. <i>(Jan 1960,NZ Dairy Exporter,11</i>)

### doubler
 
 <i>n.</i> double n&sup5.. [(?obs.)]

>  <b>MONEY</b> Taking turn about at the duty of shouldering the stretchers, we reached the Wangangaro Redoubt early in the afternoon, and we were served out with a `doubler,' or two lots of grog in one. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,141</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an extra condenser for refining spirit; (cf Wilson's[??] biography). [(sly grog distillers')]

>  <b>OGILVIE</b> For good measure the distillate would be put through a `doubler' for further refinement and strengthening. At this stage a copper `worm' was used to lead the vapour to its condensation point. <i>(1971,Moonshine County,110</i>)

### doubling
 
 <i>n.</i> two people riding the same bicycle, one seated on the handlebars or crossbar [1999 citation belongs with verb to double]

>  Doubling is fun and we ride two and three abreast. <i>(Dec 1996,North & South,47</i>)

>  <b>STEAD</b> He remembers 'doubling' her on his bike down to Bridge Avenue for a swim, her black hair in his mouth, <i>(1999,Talking about O'Dwyer,54</i>)

>  <b>FOOTE</b> They tried doubling. As anyone who has experienced it will know, doubling for any distance is mighty hard on the posterior of the one on the bar. <i>(2000,Bread and Water,26</i>)

### doubtful
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stock ] with quality that can not be guaranteed.

>  All cows and heifers near profit caused keen competition, whilst backward and doubtful calves were hard to quit. <i>(Aug 7, 1900,Hawera Star,3</i>)

### dough roaster
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cook on a station or farm.

>  <b>STACEY</b> The packmen left and were replaced by 'no-hoper dough roasters' which did little to alleviate the problem so they were replaced ... <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,78</i>)

>  <b>PETER</b> dough-roaster: cook. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,4</i>)

>  station cook: dough-roaster [dough-roaster] <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### doughboy
 
 <i>n.</i> a plain flour and water mixture, occas. with suet or sugar added, boiled, steamed or fried.

>  <b>SCOTT</b> Ned Ryan and Pat McPhee.. Sat down to Christmas dinner of pork, doughboys and billy-made tea. <i>(1900,Tales of the Colonial Turf,230</i>)

>  In it there are pork bones and watercress, doughboys and potatoes - a treat for the bros who will pay $7 each to eat it with a plastic fork off a polystyrene plate. <i>(Sep 12, 1998,Dominion,15</i>)

>  Spread an old newspaper on the floor andon it place a steaming pot of puha,kamo-kamo, riwai, brisket-on-the-bone anddumplings what we call: doughboys. <i>(Sep 7, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  The title Doughboy comes from thetraditional Maori boiler, a pot of puha,pork bones, potatoes and a lump of wetdough, broken into little round balls,thrown in. These little dumplings arecalled doughboys (Mutumutu), and by somecruel of happy fate, depending on how youlook at it, this has become one Maorislang name for gays. <i>(Jan 24, 1996,Capital Times,6</i>)

>  Dough boys, "sinkers," or "water boys" are prepared in exactly the same manner as damper, except that the dough need not be so stiff. <i>(1905,'Ukneadit' Tried Recipes,105</i>)

>  <b>TUWHARE</b> She would make doughboys to go with the kamo-kamo. <i>(1972,Sapwood & Milk,15</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Doughboys, that's what they're called, but we called them buggers afloat. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,89</i>)

>  "Maori language has been my bread and butter - or my boil up and dough boys - for the last four or five years," he says. <i>(Jul 14, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F7</i>)

>  Nor do you get much more Victorian (or rather Elizabethan) than the miti-miti or dough-boy dumpling which comes with the Paua Bar's boil-up, along with the usual meaty pork bones, some nicely cooked kumara and potato, a little of the cooking juices, and puha - or in this case watercress, lightly boiled in deference to modern tastes. <i>(Aug 10, 2000,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  <b>BURNETT</b> As for myself after a glorious feast last night of potatoes, bacon, doughboys, and sweetenedcoffee, as I sat up in my sleeping-bag, ... <i>(Mar 1860,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1860 March Burnett Diary in 1948 Von Haast Julius Von Haast 87]

>  <b>WALKER</b> Fortunately we had brought a bucket ..., so we could make doughboys and cook some bacon on our knives. <i>(Jan 4, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> Sometimes the flour is made into dough-boys - that is, the flour is made into dough and then rolled into balls about the size of your fist. <i>(Jan 22, 1869,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1869 Jan 22 Miller Captain Robert Strang (1964) 8]

>  <b>MONEY</b> We had a billy of `skilligolee' to warm us up while the ... dough-boys ... were being got under weigh. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,29</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> ... the black cook always had a good supply of meat and `dough-boys' <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,52</i>)

>  Fried chops and `dough boys', the latter being simply flour mixed with water, and fried ... <i>(1888,Rock Colonists,7</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> .. we could only afford two small `dough-boys' - or suet dumplings - for each meal <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,139</i>)

>  One might have scraped gum and cooked `doughboys' among less interesting surroundings than those of the diggers' camp. <i>(Mar 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,IX/429</i>)

>  <b>KENWAY</b> flapjack and a doughboy (dumpling) ... <i>(1928,Pioneering in Poverty Bay,7</i>)

>  Doughboy: A dumpling made of flour, sugar and suet, boiled. It was a favourite dish out mustering. (C.) <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>EVANS</b> They bought rice and a little flour to make doughboys ... <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,30</i>)

>  I was always eating ... And I loved doughboys, which are small balls of flour, usually eaten with puwha. ... <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,86</i>) [NOTES: 1970 Ihimaera in 1977 NZ Listener Short Stories 86]

>  <b>TUWHARE</b> my favourite niece said she would make/ dough-boys to go with the kamo-kamo, the meat/ and the puha ... [sic] <i>(1972,Sapwood & Milk,15</i>)

>  dumplings containing raisins, cooked in camp oven. see Buggers Afloat <i>(Jan 17, 1980,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 1980 Jan 17 2YA Christine Hunt Stones From Takaka 12:45]

>  Then, I'd roll out my threadbare Whaariki/ - to help you remember to take your boots/ off - spread an old newspaper on the floor/ and on it place a steaming pot of puha,/ kamo-kamo, riwai, brisket-on-the-bone and/ dumplings what we call: doughboys. <i>(Sep 7, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>) [NOTES: (Hone Tuwhare)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> doughboy dumpling of flour, sugar and suet, a favourite when out mustering; in America it is flour and rice-based. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,39</i>)

>  All excess members of the poultry yard were were eaten, ... or the older ones converted into a midweek stew with ... perhaps herb flavoured `dough boys' on top. <i>(Oct 1988,North & South,44</i>)

>  <b>GEE</b> We ... run home through the twilit streets into the kitchen - doughboy and stew. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,83</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> Doughboys are dumpling-like globules of flour and baking powder, milk and water, dropped into stews and cooked. Neither damper nor doughboys feature greatly on modern menus. <i>(1988,Hills, The,73</i>)

>  Like many Maori, the Tamiheres called these flour and suet dumplings "doughboys". Another Irish culinary inheritance, the doughboys were used to supplement the meat and vegetables and make the food go further. <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Dominion Post,F6</i>)

### doughnut
 
 <i>n.</i> spinning move in a (boy racer's) vehicle

>  [Hdg] "Doughnut' driver disqualified. A young man whose car hit a girl while he was doing a "doughnut" has been heavily fined and banned from driving. <i>(Aug 12, 1998,Dominion,8</i>)

>  A teenager was seen yahooing, doing doughnuts and fishtailing the jetski he was riding minutes before he hit and killed a 16-year-old girl in a kayak. <i>(Jun 9, 1998,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  He began doing donuts (tight circles). He was just being a hoon... [He] revved up again for more donuts, this time in the shallows. <i>(Nov 4, 1997,Dominion,7</i>)

>  As [the car] moves slowly through a series of screeching circles the acrid odour of burning rubber fills the air. In drag-racing parlance this is a "donut" and judging from the hoots of the spectators it is a good one. <i>(Nov 30, 1997,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> doughnut round-patterned wheelies done in car or on motorbike. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,27</i>)

>  Showmanship and artistic merit come into it; these guys consider donuts - spinning round in a tight circle - to be an art form. <i>(Oct 31, 2000,Dominion,7</i>)

### doughnut economy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It can be seen..from the country's newspapers running regular soul-searching articles on the cure for the "doughnut economy"... "The idea that there is a doughnut effect in the economy, that something is missing: that sort of question is abroad fairly fundamentally." <i>(Sep 27, 1998,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

### doughnutting
 
 <i>n.</i> see citation

>  There was also the blatant "doughnutting" by National backbenchers, notably Wayne Mapp: the phenomenon whereby unknown MPs flock to sit in the seats around well-known ones who are speaking on an important bill, in the hopes of getting themselves on telly. <i>(Dec 12, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

### doughy
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of cocksfoot grass seed] with a quality indicating ripeness.

>  <b>COULSON</b> If it [the cocksfoot grass seed] appeared doughy, it was ready for cutting. <i>(1979,Golden Harvest,35</i>)

### Douglas fir
 
 <i>n.</i> the timber from the Douglas fir grown locally; in North America usu. called Oregon (pine); also called Oregon pine.

### Douglas score
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a score awarded to deer antlers, using the Douglas scoring system.

>  <b>ADDENBROOKE</b> It was a nice even twelve, Douglas Score 295. <i>(1992,Home from the Hill,158</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Antlers that added up to 329 3/4 points on the famed Douglas Score system. <i>(1996,Great Hunting Yarns,181</i>)

### dourigan
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DAVIN</b> When Martin came back from seeing her off, the family were all sitting round the fire. `She was a nice little dourigan, I thought,' his father said. ... `But what on earth is a dourigan?' Joe asked. ... `It's the word we used back home for a little potato,' his father said; but his father's smile was only teasing and there were much worse words in his Irish vocabulary. <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,134</i>)

### dove petrel
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also dove prion](../A#also-dove-prion))

>  The sea bird known as the prion turtur or dove petrel, was found yesterday in a paddock at Opin, beyond Kennedy's. <i>(Jul 3, 1883,Wanganui Herald,3</i>)

>  This is the beak of the broad-billed dove petrel (<i>Prion vittatus</i>), one of the three species of whale birds. <i>(Mar 17, 1909,Otago Witness,86</i>)

>  He appends the following list of birds to be found on the Little Barrier in support of his statement .. dove-petrel, blue penguin. <i>(Nov 16, 1892,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  Perhaps the most memorable sight on Stephens Island was the dove petrels. <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,166</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> <i>Prion turtur</i> - Dove Petrel - Titi Wainui. <i>(1914,Mutton Birds & Other Birds,201</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Pekeha ... Pachyptila vittata, dove prion</i>, a species of petrel. <i>Totorore, ... 2. Pachyptila desolata Antarctic prion (dove prion). </i> <i>ibid.</i> 497 <i>Whiroia, ...Pachyptila desolata, dove prion (dove petrel or whale-bird). = totorore.</i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,276</i>)

>  A dove petrel.. was found at Taihape last week... The whale birds, as they are known by sailors, are always very thin and emaciated when blown ashore. <i>(Aug 1, 1918,Wanganui Chronicle,4</i>)

>  PRION TURTUR, <i>Sol</i>. - Dove Petrel. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/128</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> PRION TURTUR. (DOVE PETREL.) ... <i>Native name{s}</i>.- Whiroia [cf Williams 407 <i>Whiroia ... Pachyptla desolata, dove prion (dove petrel or whale-bird). = totorore</i>.] {and Totorore}. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,309,1888 2nd edn 2/209 ed.</i>)

>  Dove petrels are not the only residents of the island. <i>(Mar 23, 1938,Auckland Provincial Gazette,42</i>)

>  It is the broad-billed dove petrel <i>Prion vittatus</i>, common round our coasts, but breeding in the Chatham Islands. <i>(Jan 6, 1909,Otago Witness,85</i>)

### dove prion
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also dove petrel](../A#also-dove-petrel))

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Pekeha[a] ... Pachyptila vittata, dove prion</i>, a species of petrel. <i>Ibid.</i> 497 <i>Whiroia, ...Pachyptila desolata, dove prion (dove petrel or whale-bird). = totorore. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,276</i>)

### dove's foot
 
 <i>n.</i> an introduced geranium-weed [from the shape of the leaf]; soft geranium, soft crane's bill, wild geranium, also called crane's bill, wild geranium.

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> -->1926 [1967] Hilgendorf [and Calder] Weeds 113DOVESFOOT (<i>Geranium molle</i>), or soft geranium or wild geranium or soft cranes bill, is common in all soils and situations, and has been considered a native. It is a diffuse much-branched annual or perennial, softly hairy in all it parts. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,113,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>dove's foot [s] Geranium molle</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,22</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>dove's foot [s] Geranium molle</i> L. <i>(1980,Weeds of Crops,40</i>)

### dover
 
 <i>n.</i> a knife; (pl.) eating tools; often in the run of the dover(s) `as much food as one can eat' `all necessary food'; cf musterers the run of the dover; ? connected with Dover stove in high country tramping or mustering huts; ? =necessary food. -->cf Pascoe Land Uplifted High 100 - [(obs., farming)]

>  Dover: [1898 Morris Austral-English] gives the meaning as a clasp knife. George McMillan, who came out in 1858, told me that when he arrived it was the custom for hands to find theirown knife, fork, spoon, plate, and pannikin, which were all called dovers. A man would offer work for `a pound a week and the run of my dovers'. The expression died out in Canterbury before 1860. <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Once, in lamenting inevitable changes, a runholder exclaimed, `I wish I were back in a maimai with a plate of bark and a dover!' The saying then [ca1870] in engaging a hand was `so much a week and the run of the dover.' The dover was a Dover knife, and the `run of the dover' was as as much food as one could eat. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,10</i>)

>  He said `I tell you what, Johnson, these big houses are a curse instead of a blessing. I wish I was back in a `maimai' with a plate of bark and a dover!' The saying then in regard to wages was `So much the week and the run of the dover.' (The `dover' was a DoverKnife and the `run of the dover' was as much food as you liked to eat) <i>(1989,Pioneer Recollections,1/93</i>) [NOTES: -->19?? Beattie Pioneer Recollections 1/93 (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>KERSE</b> This `big house' [built of slabs in 1860] ... was very draughty and McNab at a time when he was suffering from a bronchial ailment, was heard to remark ..., `I tell you what, Johnson, these big houses are a curse. I wish I were back in the maimai with a plate of bark and a run of the dover'. <i>(1984,Knapdale Run, The,18</i>) [NOTES: [a1890 in] 1984 Knapdale Run 18 [Alexander McNab: had Australian experience]]

>  dover n. a clasp knife. [fr. Dover, the name of the maker] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  dover: An old brand of hand shears, and soon used as another name for the shears themselves, hence such terms as `the run of your dover' and flashing your dover'. [This needs checking. He may have go this from Wilkes.] <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/23</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an eating utensil.

>  <b>MCNAB</b> I tell you Johnston, these big houses are a curse. I wish I were back in a maimai, with a plate of bark and a Dover. <i>(1858,BLANK ENTRY,5</i>) [NOTES: in Wilson & Fraser (1936) Book of the Pioneers]

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> dovers: eating utensils. According to Acland (Early Canterbury Runs) it was customary in the very early years for the employees to supply their own, but the practice, and with it the expression, died out about 1860. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,27</i>)

### Dover stove
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The stove was going good, that old cast-iron Dover there that was secondhand when I got it.. ages ago. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,127</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> We snuggled into the hut on Goat Pass and built up the scrub fire in a Dover stoveto warm our bones and sleeping bags. <i>(1952,Land Uplifted High,150</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> My pay as a cadet was so small ... [that] I induced the management to provide me with a `bach' fitted up with a Dover stove for cooking and warmth. <i>(1951,Confessions,56</i>)

### dovie
 
 <i>n.</i> [mining]

>  At times, some defect in the tunnelling leads to the reappearance of a "dovie". <i>(Mar 23, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,42</i>)

### Down
 
 <i>n.</i> a Southdown (sheep). [(farming)]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> My experience of the Downs is that they are best suited for dry country. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,97</i>)

### down
 
 <i>n.</i> have (get) a down on, to have a set on; grudge against; be prejudiced against; (See [also derry](../A#also-derry))

>  But if someone has a 'down' on the rich as well as people of a different colour or culture, then I guess that is adding fuel to the fire. <i>(Oct 26, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> Go on in the old fashion,/ And ne'er improve the town;/ And still on all new comers/ Keep up a fearful `down;' <i>(1862,Dunedin Songster,No.1/2</i>) [NOTES: The Old Identity]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> I've got no `down' on Travers,/ For he's a clever chap; 'Twas but in self-defence I/ Gave him a gentle rap; <i>(1862,Canterbury Songster,10</i>)

>  <b>BRADSHAW</b> Sun and wear have had too much of a `down' on that suit for much colour to survive <i>(1883,New Zealand As It Is,3</i>)

>  He has a decided `down' on Trade Unionism. <i>(Nov 10, 1890,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  The lad stated that Taubman had ordered him to stand there till the night-watchman came, because Taubman `had a down on him.' <i>(Sep 22, 1906,NZ Truth,66/6/1</i>)

>  <b>SATUDHOLME</b> My father had a great down on light-coloured beasts. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,118</i>)

>  down n. from adv. prejudice against, hostility to; have a down on, be prejudiced against. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> down, in phr. have a down on a grudge, hostile or at least poor opinion of someone; in 1862 Charles Thatcher said he had `no down on Travers'. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,39</i>)



 
 <i>adv.</i> indicating direction of travel or locality reached, in relation to a starting point.

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> In the afternoon assembled the committee; when it was determined the Herald should return to the Colony for stores, as none had come down exceptg. flour ... <i>(Mar 16, 1827,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,45,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> I brought down from Sydney [to Nelson] thorough bred Merino rams ... <i>(Oct 22, 1847,Handbook for New Zealand, The,249</i>)



 
 <i>adv.</i> go/get down sc. from NZ to Chathams (cf Aust to NZ : go down : bring up) ?old whalers' usage ?=go west.

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> One thing that was rather bad in my early years down there was the fact that there was no branch of the Post Office Savings Bank attached to the Post Office. <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,148</i>)

>  Still in Ch.Ch. looking for a ship. Mrs Briscoe of the Islands is here and very anxious to get down. The Cleopatra is the one expected to go down but she is bar bound in the Grey and may stay so for a month. <i>(Aug 1, 1876,Chudleigh Diary,250</i>)



 
 <i>adv.</i>

>  <b>FITTON</b> .. there are the drays ... for carrying down the wool after the shearing season, to the ports for shipment. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),220</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to pinch, steal; to appropriate [1940 at least: cits needed.]



 
<b>2</b> <i>adv.</i>

>  John & Seal out shooting down the River and to old Whare swamp, killed a couple of swamp hens - <i>(Jan 24, 1857,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,66</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Had a bit of a run-in with a pongo MP down the Berka last night. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,209</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> `Where you been all this time?' his mother asked. `Down the library,' he said. ... `I bet he's been tarting,' Joy said. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,32,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... who led raids on the apple orchards down the South Belt. <i>Ibid.</i> 140 Down the back the lunkos [sc the hoons, a crudely behaved pair] were playing a crooner complaining to listeners [141] that somebody was `breaking my heart in two', a sentiment I suspected was wasted on them. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,10</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> I was down Whangarei port loading fertiliser. Dust everywhere. <i>Ibid.</i> 159 Young womendown the back of the bus were [160] talking about guys putting the hard word on them. <i>(1985,G'day Country,35</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Or when we were playing around having a fight down the back, someone would say, `Go away, nigger.' ... Then there'd be a fight. Same thing when the towneyites - thekids from up Kaikoura - came down here. <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five,98</i>) [NOTES: (Marama, Kaikoura)]

>  Suppose a friend says to me, `You know that drongo who was on the chain with me at the works last summer? Just a little joker he was, remember? A real serious ankle-biter. Hailed from somewhere down the Wire Wrapper. <i>(Jan 1989,Pacific Way,10</i>) [NOTES: (Sharp New Zildish)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>adv.</i>

>  Down the Sounds. [title] <i>(1922,Journal of Katherine Mansfield,304</i>) [NOTES: (1954 Murry)]

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> If we are ever together down the Pelorus Sounds come off with me for a whole day - Will you? -and let's just remember. <i>(1922,Journal of Katherine Mansfield</i>) [NOTES: 1922 in 1954 Journal pp. 309-311 in 1974 Gordon Undiscovered Country 240; also quoting 1928 Letters II, 143]

### down country
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) rural area close to town.

>  Down country, used (chiefly by people in the hills) to describe the localities near town or on the plains. <i>(Sep 30, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area to the south.

>  Down country they have had a quite disastrous lambing. <i>(Nov 13, 1975,NZ Farmer,51</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adv.</i> (Rural) in the direction of town, from the high country or back blocks.

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> A dozen or more horses with their pack-saddles empty .. were returning down country. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,135</i>)

>  The wool clip is now being sent down country (says the Dunstan 'Times.') <i>(Feb 1906,NZ Farmer,138</i>)

>  <b>JONES</b> At first we only had young cattle which we bought down country ... <i>(1933,Autobiography of an Early Settler,41</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Kennedy, whom rumour had it was courting, had gone down country 'on business' for a week ... <i>(1949,High Country Days,86</i>)

>  <b>VANCE</b> The fast transport which we take for granted today makes us forget that in earlier times, the station hand who wanted to go to another station, or down-country, had to 'swag it.' <i>(1965,High Endeavour,67</i>)

>  <b>PORTER</b> We left spick and span, for the run down country. <i>(1970,Under the Nor'West Arch,39</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the area farmed in the plains and foothills of the South Island mountain ranges.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> It happened that Barney had a pup which he had bred himself, and had got someone 'down country' to rear it for him. <i>(1960,Tall Tussock,46</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> down country: perhaps the term only really belongs to the South Island, where it means the plains, and the foothills or front country. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,28</i>)

### down cow
 
 <i>n.</i> [Used also USA] (See [downer](dou-dyn.md#downer),[downer cow](dou-dyn.md#downer-cow))

>  When is a down cow not a down cow? <i>(Aug 22, 2005,NZ Farmers' Weekly,3</i>)

>  Your staff member was late starting, you have had two down cows, one looking terminal, and the somatic cell count already looks dodgy. <i>(May 30, 2005,NZ Farmers' Weekly,5</i>)

### down horn
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a downward-turning horn of cattle.

>  LOST: A bullock .. he is not branded, has down horns, and a ring in his nose. <i>(Jul 29, 1857,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

### down horns
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  LOST. A Bullock, ... he is not branded, has down horns, and a ring in his nose. <i>(Jul 29, 1857,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

### down south
 
<b>2</b> <i>adv.</i> (in) the trouser pocket(s).

>  'Enessy sticks 'is lighted bumper down south into 'is overcoat pocket. <i>(1916,Anzac Book, The,47</i>) [NOTES: [?Aust. in AND]]

>  He dreamt he'd got his fourteen `peg' / And buttoned it `Down South'. 3. snow(ing) down south [W3], see snow 2. <i>(May 16, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,137</i>)

### down South
 
 <i>adv.</i> in modern usage applied to any place considerably to the south of the speaker, esp. the south of the South Island. Contrast up North.

>  <b>LADY BARKER</b> A Journey `Down South' [sc. from Canterbury, to Otago.] [heading] <i>(May 1867,Station Life in NZ,137,1874 ed.</i>)

>  .. particularly in that money-making go-ahead province Otago. Surely it may be accepted as a strong argument ... that such sharp business men as those `down South', have readily seen ... the advantages to be gained by the half-holiday. <i>(May 21, 1872,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: 1872 May 21 Evening Post in 1963 Oct 3 Evening Post 16]

>  <b>BEATON</b> GINNY: When I was a kid. Down South. <i>(1984,Outside In,106</i>) [NOTES: [the S.I.]]

>  <b>LADY BARKER</b> All the first class pasture land `down South' as [Otago] was called had been taken up long before. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,93</i>) [NOTES: (Canterbury, to Otago)]

>  <b>G M WILSON</b> Our first visit [at Wellington] was to a chemist and also to an artist where we saw some views he had taken of the lakes, we had seen down south, and there were two of Manapori, ... <i>(Feb 1873,Diary,176</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Feb G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 176]

>  <b>TROLLOPE</b> `A railway for you gentlemen down South!' [sc. South Island] says a northern member. `Certainly, - but on condition that we have one here, up North.' <i>(1874,New Zealand (various authors/titles),117</i>)

>  ... he [a Wellington man] had married this woman down South [sc. Canterbury-Otago] <i>(Apr 17, 1880,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> `Very cold this morning, Mahon.' `Very Sir: there's been a heavy fall of snow somewhere down-south.' <i>(May 22, 1881,Waikato Journals,175,1982 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [?lower N.I.]]

>  <b>BRADSHAW</b> The rush recently has been down South [to Otago]. <i>(1883,New Zealand As It Is,340</i>)

>  Fought the grim fight with Otago, .../ Last year's doings down South ... <i>(1888,College Rhymes,15,1923 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [sc OTAGO]]

>  <b>PASCOE</b> `Oh! yes, down south [sc. on the west coast, SI], they have all drifted into Maori habits `plenty more days tomollow' etc. ...' <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,54</i>) [NOTES: [about Douglas's lethargy]c1894 Roberts to Harper in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 54]

>  <b>HARPER</b> On one occasion he was `down South', below Gillespies [Beach], and, with five others, was trying to shoot a check-shirt bird close to the hut of another digger, ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,147</i>) [NOTES: [ref. point central W Coast]]

>  <b>BELL</b> ... I remember now he was blamed for firing the tussocks ... on Grassdale run, down south, one night when there was a big Nor'wester blowing. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,18</i>) [NOTES: [in the South Island]]

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> `What part of the country do you come from?' `Down South' `Taranaki or Hawkes Bay way?' <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,18</i>) [NOTES: [A Northland man speaks]]

>  Down South [in South Island], now that the dentists have given up advertising ... <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth,1/6/?</i>)

>  Down South he is carrying on his pranks. <i>(Oct 21, 1905,NZ Truth,18/1/8</i>) [NOTES: (in SI)]

>  Insurance agents among the wild and whiskerous `backbone' down south have lively experiences, sure pop. <i>(Jul 14, 1906,NZ Truth,56/1/7</i>) [NOTES: [S.I.]]

>  Hon. Tam. Mackenzie surely cannot be to a sure of his Taieri seat down south, and constituencies have a habit of biffing out turn-coats, because numerous countrynewspapers are hinting at Tam's desertion of his old love. <i>(Sep 3, 1910,NZ Truth,271/1/8</i>) [NOTES: [Southland or Otago]]

>  <b>MANDER</b> Already he saw the cream factories which men down south were dreaming as part of every township. <i>(1925,Allen Adair,48,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> The `Count' [Potocki de Montalk] used to conduct a milk-round down South in New Zealand. <i>(1934,Journalese,46</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Down south five Invercargill schools ... proudly ... claimed him as an ex-pupil. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,180</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> `Reminds me of the time down south when I was watchman at a brewery,' Simmons said. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,21,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> I happened to meet a very old South otago friend there [in Christchurch]. ... She said `... you know there are no friends like the old ones down South.' <i>(1943,Pure Gold,89</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> Once she went for a holiday down South [from Canterbury to Otago] where snowberries and penny oranges grow on the hills. <i>(1951,Lagoon, The,12,1961 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (The Secret)]

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Down south, where Bill and Nobby come from, you can ride along a ledge and throw your cigarette butt into a lake thousands of feet below. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,21</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Sam got the cowman-gardener's beardie to train for him `like we handle 'en down South.' <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,74</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> He came from down south somewhere. <i>(1965,Outcasts,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the south of the SI [also attrib.]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Lofty, in happy mood, and unfailing in his praise of his native province [Southland], delighted young Wallace with tall stories of `down South.' <i>(1949,High Country Days,69</i>)

>  Bush whisky makes the boozer think the world is upside down. After three glasses of a brand retailed by a down South publican ... <i>(Apr 7, 1906,NZ Truth,42/1/3</i>)

### down the chute
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>GEE</b> Then, advancing through it, almost subaural, a chatter.. our lives together. It grew into a roar. It rattled down a chute and fell from hearing, and I was empty and must start again. <i>(1992,Going West,219</i>)

>  If I go west, a lot of jobs would go downthe chute. <i>(May 30, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),32</i>)

### down the dunny
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Save them, but for heaven's sake, don't <i>not</i> spend them or the economy'll be down the dunny. <i>(Jul 1, 1998,Dominion,1</i>)

>  Raised in rural Palmerston North, Walton didn't think too much about faith until his late teens, when his life seemed to be going down the dunny. <i>(Dec 26, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  The fact that a few hundred thousand dollars of public funds have been flushed down the dunny in the meantime might be forgiven if we could be reassured that the arrogance and cynicism behind the editorial judgments on <i>McCormick Rips</i> will not be repeated. <i>(Jul 8, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  Malcolm Walker was The Examinercartoonist before it went down the dunny. <i>(Feb 1993,Metro,12</i>)

### down the gurgler
 
 <i>phr.</i> washed away, beaten, ruined [OED online: A person or thing that gurgles; spec. in <b>Austral. slang</b>, a plughole, drain; freq. in fig. phrases down the gurgler, etc. (cf. DRAIN n. 1 e).]

>  Indeed, a slight hiccup in [his] career proves that an unblemished expert on business collapses can inadvertently find himself going down the financial gurgler. <i>(Jan 14, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  [Westbrook Haines a NZ kiwifruit salesperson] Haines had previously worked for the New Zealand Dairy Board where he says he watched the Australian dairy industry go `down the gurgler' while the New Zealand industry went ahead in leaps and bounds because of its marketing skills. <i>(Jun 3, 1986,Bulletin, The (Sydney),32</i>)

>  (Scott cartoon caption) This country is really going down the gurgler, mate. Even the sheep are emigrating. <i>(Jul 7, 1988,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Pessimists ... interpret the polls to mean that the Government is politically down the gurgler. <i>(Nov 13, 1988,Sunday Star (Auckland),A15</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> gurgler plughole, often phr. down the gurgler ruined or lost; eg `There goes another bright idea down the gurgler.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,53</i>)

>  Employment Minister Annette King opened Parliament's general debate with the accusation that National would be deservedly `down the gurgler' because of its sexist attitude to women. <i>(Aug 17, 1989,Dominion,2</i>)

>  As far as I can see the country is just going to go down the gurgler because all the kids are using foul language now. <i>(Aug 3, 2002,Dominion,A25</i>)

>  <b>MARRINER</b> Doing things my way, plead guilty to <i>just</i> the reefer, and I'll personally see all the violence charges go down the self-defence gurgler. <i>(2001,Stonedogs,330</i>)

>  [Hdg] Plug pulled on trans-Tasman meet as $67,000 goes down the gurgler. <i>(Dec 23, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B6</i>)

>  We'd had a wonderful marriage for 50 years and it was going down the gurgler. <i>(Nov 10, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  ...it would be nothing like the stoush that would result if we allowed the marine education centre to go down the gurgler. <i>(Feb 12, 2004,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  I flew over and found a place called Cranks that was going down the gurgler. <i>(Nov 2005,North & South,90</i>)

>  <b>McGill</b> Or was Peat going to win and life as he loved and cherished it go right down the damned gurgler? <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,29</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The high standards set for thisnewspaper.. have gone down the gurglerand the tubes double quick. <i>(1992,Fast Times at Greenhill High,72</i>)

>  The only organisation he was in favour of backing was surf lifesaving. "Where does it end?" he asked. Where indeed? Down the gurgler because of the insularity of one small council out in the wop-wops, it seems. <i>(May 31, 2006,NZ Herald,A2</i>)

>  19-- TV1 commentator at the end of the second Aust-NZ one-day match, which NZ won `Australia is down the googler again and we are two up in the series.' HNZD2 DOWN THE GURGLER: 1. =wastepipe of sink [check] <i>(2006,Dominion Post</i>) [NOTES: 2006: not date of citation; date of entry.]



 
<b>2</b> <i>phr.</i> of, esp. beer, down the throat. [Occas. pron @/googler/]

### down the tarmac
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) on the plains [a hill country term].

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> down the tarmac: down the mudflats (ie the Plains); the opposite of up the burnt spur or up the gully. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,28</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Gully, up the: Opposite to down the tarmac. But it also suggests a propensity for avoiding visits to the towns, cities etc. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,37</i>)

### down the track
 
 <i>phr.</i> in the future

>  [Employers' advocate] We're down the track on that one [negotiated settlement]. <i>(Nov 23, 1987,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Morning Report National Radio]

>  [Political union] is something our successors down the track - a long way down the track - may want to look at. <i>(Nov 22, 1987,TV1</i>) [NOTES: 1987 Nov 22 Lange and Hawke on TV]

>  <b>David Geary</b> 'You see,' said the Mayor...'what you don't realise is we're not just thinking about tomorrow. We're thinking twenty-five years down the track.' <i>(2008,Six Pack 3, The,126</i>)



 
 <i>phr.</i> finished; go down the track: to be sacked.

>  <b>THOMSON</b> We usually find that a poor worker makes a poor mate; and if we find any that don't pull their weight on the open range, there is no enthusiasm about being mates with them. Usually theyare not long in going down the track. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,21</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> `The cook's in bed, and when he recovers he'll be going down the track. Can you cope?' <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,109</i>)

>  [During a round-table discussion on farming a stock and station agent says] In five years' time ... if these conditions continue I put it to you farming will be down the track. <i>(Mar 23, 1986,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>)

### down time
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering and other rural seasonal work] a period when there is no work.

>  <b>KERR</b> Mustering work is on-again off-again, so I, like a lot of contemporaries, would live by the gun in down times, like the off-season when I was at Nokomai. There was six weeks of down time around February at Glenaray, just as there was at Nokomai. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,35</i>)

>  Agworkers: Daggers/crutchers - good money with regular work. Can supply down-time work as well. [down-time] <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Dominion,33</i>)

### Down Under
 
 <i>n.</i> [(?whalers')]

>  <b>HOLMES</b> The time of migration of sperm whales around `Chatham waters' (`Down Under' as it was called by the whalers) was November to April each year. <i>(1984,Chatham Islands,20</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Australia and New Zealand; now used of NZ (with Australia) by other than New Zealanders though pre-WW1 usage suggests a local acceptance. [also adjective]

>  Indeed, <i>down under</i> is first recorded as a noun here rather than in Australia (in 1905, ten years before the first Australian use, according to the <i>Australian National Dictionary (AND)</i>. <i>(Aug 2000,NZWords,4 : 1</i>)

>  The young man from "down under" headed later for Detroit. <i>(Jul 18, 1928,NZ Free Lance,9</i>)

>  What started as a four-week jaunt downunder has become a full-on battle for bragging rights at Mount Maunganui over the next three days as German world champions Jonas Reckermann and Julius Brink take on the New Zealand Open. <i>(Jan 20, 2011,Bay of Plenty Times,A16</i>)

>  <b>ANZAC</b> Anyway, the men from Down Under were real glad to see them. <i>(1916,On the Anzac Trail,72</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> In those days Antipodes meant more than `down under.' The term imaginatively suggested the edge of the world. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,10,1949 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> [A US writer] The little Dominion of New Zealand is the more or less happy land popularly known as `Down Under.' ... It is doubtful whether the origin of the term `Down Under' will ever be known. It first came into general use during the World War of 1914-18, when the New Zealanders who fought in the ill-starred Gallipoli campaign were referred to as `the men from Down Under', but that was not the start of it. This faraway land, it seems, was known as `Down Under' in some parts of England and Scotland before the birth of the present century. The first World War merely [put] the expression into wider usage. An Auckland artist, Mr Trevor Lloyd,made it the subject of one of his cartoons at the very beginning of that great upheaval, and at least one English war correspondent used the term when writing on Gallipoli. After the year 1918 the expression fell into disuse, but during the second World War it again became popular, and I think we must thank the Americans for its revival. ... In England it is also known as the Antipodes. <i>(1945,If You'd Care to Know,7</i>)

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> He also uses expressions like `down under', which implies implies that there's something wrong with the Southern Hemisphere, and `Australasia', which implies a close connection between New Zealand and her bullying barbaric neighbour, ... <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,18</i>)

>  down under inf. Australia and New Zealand <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,145</i>) [NOTES: [Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 336; NZPOD 225]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> Down Under New Zealand and Australia, as perceived from the Northern Hemisphere. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,39</i>)

>  `Down Under' boys on Furlough. <i>(1916,NZEF Chronicles,199</i>)

>  <b>ANZAC</b> .. trained for service with the `Down Under' contingents ... <i>(1916,On the Anzac Trail,2</i>)

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> ... a Down Under book for overseas friends and relations. <i>(1945,If You'd Care to Know,6</i>)

>  As well, her Downunder Mr Wonderful had to be warm, caring, considerate ... a man of strict Christian principles. <i>(Nov 8, 1987,Sunday News,21</i>)

>  "Down under" boys on furlough are intown, Discharged from hospital, repairedand braced. <i>(Dec 27, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/9 : 199</i>)

>  Let them pause, and hear when nights arestill The other girl who coo-ees from"down under". <i>(Dec 27, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/9 : 199</i>)

>  With that I tell her how much we're looking forward to her visit Downunder. <i>(Apr 23, 2008,Express (Gay & Lesbian Express),13</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>adv.</i>

>  <b>FROUDE</b> We were to bid adieu to the `Australasian' ... She had carried us safely down under. <i>(1886,Oceana,92</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Two big maps, one of the North Island, one of the South, with Stewart Island tacked on down under, showed up on one of the bare walls ... <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,163</i>)

### down-country
 
 <i>n.</i> The flat part of a country (as opposed to hilly regions). The noun (and aj and av phrases) is used mainly to distinguish the lower or flat country (often the plains) from the high-country (South Island) or hill country (NI); it also often connotes `thecoast', `the town', as distinguished from the back country (see also front country), as often New Zealand's flat land is coastal or towards the coast. The usage is US and`colonial'.

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> In the down-country. [title: sc Christchurch] - <i>(1904,Sons o' Men</i>)

>  Acland quoting 1872 Apr 4 Wakatip Mail Down Country. See COUNTRY <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the flat country on a farm as opposed to the high or hill country.

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> At Waimate we ran Cross-bred sheep on the down-country and Merinos or Half-breds on the hills. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,97</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> There's a subtle difference between back-country men and those enslaved to industry and commerce. ... `The blokes up here think with their hearts,' said one of my mates one night after a session in a country pub with a crowd of cullers, musterers and shearers. `Down country they only think with their heads.' <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,20</i>) [NOTES: [?n in the dc; ?av equiv. up here] ']



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of foot-hills, rolling and flat land] closer to settlement than the high country or back country.

>  It will be the duty of down-country farmers to get the shearing over as soon as it can be managed, in order that the men go up country. <i>(Nov 1917,NZ Farmer,1226</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> The new comb will also find full use in down-country flocks for the out-of-season shearing of breeding ewes, young sheep and rams. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,81</i>)

>  <b>VANCE</b> The stock and station agency tried to put down-country sheep on Rollesby. <i>(1965,High Endeavour,208</i>)

>  The down-country cockies like nothing better than a nice line of blacks and will pay accordingly. <i>(Sep 10, 1970,NZ Farmer,35</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> One may detect in those who live up-country a slight hint of envy which is cloaked under a pose of smugness and scorn for down-country dwellers and which needs to be kept hidden because the down-country men do arrive at the annual ewe fairs to spend lots of money! <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,28</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> Down-country sheep often look a bit pink after shearing because they're closely shorn, but in the high country a bit more wool has to be left on them. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,21</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adv.</i> towards or on the flat land near the coast; towards or near the town (esp. send (animals etc.) down country).

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> A dozen or more with their pack-saddles empty ... were returning down country. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,135</i>)

>  <b>BARRY</b> We were mustering the sheep and getting ready to start down-country.<i>Ibid.</i> 263 I sent herdown-country [sc. to Dunedin] in a coach. <i>(1879,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,20</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Once or twice he had gone down-country with an empty dray ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,133</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Another fire, started up about Greenvale in 1858, came `lolliping' down-country over the Merino Downs country, finally dying out about Waipahi. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,25</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> After being at Hogburn the best part of a year he decided to leave before the worst of the winter came on and so he returned down-country. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,3,1956 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [Hogburn =Naseby]]

>  When an inhabitant of the Mackenzie goes beyond Burke's Pass, he talks of going `down country.' The phrase implies a moral as well as a physical descent. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,122</i>) [NOTES: (from Korero, AEWS magazine) 122 (`Mackenzie Country')]

>  Many [high-country men] are born `down-country' as we say <i>(1951,High Country,42</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> However, I saddle up and set out `down country' for Pukaki. <i>Ibid.</i> 21 He dule?) took a job down country, finished with the back country for good. Six months later he was back again ... He'd got footrot down on the `mud flats' and had to come back to the hard country. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,20</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The deer hides from the same parties were brought here by packhorses for a final check, then stored until transported down country to skin buyers or agents. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,66</i>)

>  And Christmas as Tarndale was better held drunk than sober, because down country all our people were hanging up stockings and being kissed under the mistletoe and getting plenty of mail and good things, while we slept on wire netting and tried not to think of home. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,20</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> ... as I lay in my bunk doing a bit of thinking I came to theconclusion going by the way I had evaded Macdonald, that it was high time I headed down country and started associating with people before I went crackers. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,59</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> In November, when Bluey finished at the station, he headed down country for his sister-in-law's place. <i>(1970,Tat,91</i>)

>  <b>PINNEY</b> Because of its position Benmore had to sell stock almost always down-country. <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,25</i>)

>  He was puzzled why any kea would be found so far down-country [near Hokonui] <i>(Jul 29, 1986,Southland Times,19</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>adj.</i> esp. South Island, of or pertaining to the flatcountry away from the mountains or hills

>  <b>HISLOP</b> ... `Shiner' ... had called with his usual thirst at one of the down-country hotels. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,126</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> Late snows that came down into lambing paddocks, noisy rituals of dipping, crutching, shearing; the final down-country journey of the wool-bales. <i>(1945,Died in the Wool,13</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> It was a down-country drover returning from an assignment to the west coast ... <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,188,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> How many down-country paddock farmers would be happy with an equal result? <i>(1969,Molesworth,192</i>)

>  [sic] While the tussle was going on I managed to get in touch with a down-country drover whose dogs could manage these sheep. (Canterbury) <i>(1978,Woolgatherers,112</i>)

### down-creek
 
<b>1</b> <i>adv.</i> (Rural) in a direction towards the mouth of a creek.

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> .. each morning she continued her journey on the cream-launch all the way down-creek to the high school in the town ... <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,22</i>)

### down-string
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [stock] to move downhill in a single file.

>  <b>MCKEE WRIGHT</b> .. and the hoggets down-stringing through clover ... <i>(1897,Station Ballads,13</i>)

### down-table
 
 <i>adj.</i> of Cabinet Ministers, placed away from the head of the cabinet table; cf down market.

>  Last week I noted how many down-table Cabinet members, and even MPs had lost the ability to drive themselves, as, indeed, has a substantial proportion of the military officer-class. <i>(Apr 29, 1987,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>)

### down-trou
 
 <i>n.</i> a lowering of trousers (also as verb) [Spears "Slang & Euphemism" gives "trou" as US 1900.]

>  Prime aim: avoid the down-trou. <i>(Jan 14, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D3</i>)

>  <b>BROSNAHAN</b> Children home and the party went on/ Still keeping pretty clean / Though one of the fellows did a down trou' / After I left the scene. <i>(1998,Glenaray 1898-1998,80</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> down trou male (usually) party trick of standing on the table, and dropping trousers, shared with certain Brits, as author witnessed when several All Blacks and Lions rugby players downtrou-ed in a Wellington restaurant in 1983. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,39</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> Yay, so there is a God - one who had covered up my indiscretions and sent my sons home before I had done my down trou. <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,86</i>)

### down/downers
 
 <i>n.</i> [(whalers')]

>  <b>GRADY</b> Down, downers - The time a submerged or diving whale stays underwater. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,226</i>) [NOTES: (Cook Strait Whalers' glossary)]

### downcast
 
 <i>n.</i> [(mining)]

>  Under present conditions the miner from the moment he sets foot upon the cage or steps inside the downcast in reality takes his life out of his own hands. <i>(Apr 20, 1911,Maoriland Worker,8/2/3</i>)

### downcast shaft
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McARA319</b> Downcast shafts: Those shafts into which the air flowed from the surface, as opposed to upcast shafts which discharged the warm spent air. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary)</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary of Waihi Mining Terms)]

### downed
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of hunting stock] killed or injured.

>  <b>PORTER</b> It was good to put my freezing hands into the warm gut of a downed deer. <i>(1970,Under the Nor'West Arch,62</i>)

### downer
 
 <i>n.</i> OED: fig. A depressing person or experience; a failure; a downward trend, esp. inbusiness or the economy. Cf. UPPER n.2 2. orig. U.S.

>  It was a kind of a downer after the way I had been going. I'd been so stoked with my game. <i>(Feb 22, 2002,Evening Post,25</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [down n3.](../D#down-n3.))



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sick cow or sheep.

>  Since the drainage has been done there have been fewer downer cows and fewer milk fever losses. <i>(May 13, 1982,NZ Farmer,28</i>)

>  I wonder how many farmers have tended 'downer' cows during storms not realising that they may be seeing an aftermath of facial eczema ... <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,80</i>)

>  I have often told the story of the downer cow in a storm in 1973 ... <i>(Jun 1994,NZ Dairy Exporter,94</i>)

>  In spring there are inevitably the odd downer cows that have difficulty calving and cannot get up. [downer cow] <i>(Sep 2001,Growing Today,64</i>)

### downer cow
 
 <i>n.</i> [Also used in USA.] (See [downer](dou-dyn.md#downer),[down cow](dou-dyn.md#down-cow))

>  .. one Waikato farmer who last week found the rules have changed when it comes to getting downer cows picked up for pet food. <i>(Aug 22, 2005,NZ Farmers' Weekly,3</i>)

### downland
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the lower, flatter and foothill land in proximity to alpine and high country.

>  <b>BROSNAHAN</b> With the development of the downland country came the need for better utilisation of the improved pastures. <i>(1998,Glenaray 1898-1998,71</i>)

>  Mr Hewson was brought up on a family farm near St Andrews in South Canterbury, and later farmed the downland in partnership with his brother. <i>(Sep 26, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### downpipe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Downpipe. Length of non-flexible [sic] pipe through which power is transmitted to the machineshearer's hand-piece. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)

### downtown
 
 <i>adj.</i> to and around the central shopping and entertainment area of a town [Jim writes: NSOD tags this as <i>chiefly NAmer</i>, Collins as <i>US, Canadian & NZ</i>. All OED citations are North American or refer to North America, except one from Australia in 1969. A <i>New Scientist</i> article in 1966 found it necessary to translate the word for English readers. However, I have recently heard and read it in British TV programmes and publications with reference e.g to Adelaide and Santiago. Is it used of British cities and since when? It has been common in NZ at least since the 70s esp but not solely in relation to Auckland and Wellington.]

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> It certainly wasn't the night for a leisurely stroll down town or to be loitering in shop doorways, and yet a surprising number of the city's population were doing just that. <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,79</i>)

>  [Hdg] Discover Downtown. Downtown Wellington combines four richly different experiences, all within walking distance from each other. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,Evening Post,29</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> I went about my daily tasks at home - milking the cows, often sharing the ride with Bruddie, taking my "turn" at riding Dad's bike downtown for the messages. <i>(1982,To the Is-Land,220</i>)

>  Downtown is walking distance from everywhere, and the harbour's so close that it's the city's front yard. <i>(Nov 23, 2000,Dominion,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an amphetamine derivative

### downy
 
 <i>adj.</i> as a distinguishing epithet in plant names (See [brome](../B#brome),[hakea](h-hea.md#hakea),[ironheart](../I#ironheart),[rata](ra-red.md#rata))

### downy country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] the farmed downland adjacent to the South Island high country.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> It had little flat land, and not even much 'downy' country like the other side of Forest Creek. <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,180</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> One of the troubles of this kind of 'downy' country is that there are no defined routes for sheep. <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,186</i>)

>  He prefers cattle to sheep on his downy country during droughts. <i>(Jul 8, 1971,NZ Farmer,32</i>)

### doze
 
 <i>n.</i> [(forestry)]

>  DOZE. Decay or incipient decay in timber. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

### doze off
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to use a bulldozer blade to level or flatten land, erections, scrub or trees.

>  How many extra acres would you have if you could smooth off that gully .. clear out that fence row..move those rocks .. doze off or pull out brush and trees? <i>(Mar 1967,NZ Journal of Agriculture,68</i>)

### dozie
 
 <i>adj.</i> of land where sheep lose condition; of sheep so affected with a deficiency disease; see also dopie.

>  Dozie: Country where sheep lose condition and do not thrive - go dopie, in fact - is called dopie. I have heard shepherds speak of d[ozie] sheep also. It is very likely the Scots for dopie. (C.) <i>(Oct 7, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of poor country] where sheep do not thrive.

>  <b>ACLAND</b> dozie: country where sheep lose condition and do not thrive - go dopie, in fact it is called d. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,370</i>)

### DPAG
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Dunedin Public Art Gallery

>  At the time, the Dunedin Public Art Gallery (DPAG) had no idea that the Hotere works were heading to Gore...Pitts argues that the DPAG does have an "active" programme of local artists. <i>(Nov 15, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

### DPB
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Domestic Purposes Benefit [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  Mothers with young families who are forced into taking up the DPB get an enormous amount of flak. <i>(Nov 1999,North & South,75</i>)

>  Most were in their 20s and some were on the DPB, the rest were on the dole. <i>(Oct 1997,North & South,76</i>)

>  Dr Cathy Wyllie, a social researcher, did a major survey of women on the DPB in 1980. <i>(Mar 2, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  The dpb was introduced in 1973 to help women leave violent relationships and to allow unmarried mothers to keep their children instead of giving them up for adoption. <i>(Jun 27, 2001,Dominion,10</i>)
