>  There is no evidence.. that teenagers get pregnant for the DPB; there are more men than single teenage mothers on the DPB. <i>(Mar 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  It's the pits being on the DPB but if it's the only way you can get income to feed your kids you do it, eh? <i>(1995,Mana,No. 10 : 31</i>)

>  <b>OLIVER</b> She's a good little mother is kanga/ scraping / along on the d p b/ no mr about the place just roo/ <i>(1982,Poor Richard,18</i>)

>  ... they feel neglected, especially those on long-term benefits like DPB. ... [fn] Domestic Purposes Benefit <i>(Feb 1984,Public Service Journal,10</i>)

>  Studies to date offer no evidence that the DPB encourages women to get pregnant, ... A woman struggling to get off the DPB and into the work force ... might as well give up and go home. <i>(Apr 26, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  Despite the fact that most of these women qualify for the DPB, many choose to work instead. <i>(Jul 1986,Metro,130</i>)

>  After dissolution of marriage by separation agreement there was the DPB. <i>(May 1987,Metro</i>) [NOTES: Roger `Auckland Life']

>  Clergyman opposes cancelling of dpb [headline] <i>(May 19, 1987,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Just once I would like to read an article with a positive approach to `solo mums'. ... I am on the DPB ... <i>(Feb 1988,North & South,34</i>)

>  The [Collins English] dictionary does not, however, contain entries for DPB, EFTPOS, GST, CNG, first fifteen, street kids, May holidays, and it is still too early for orange roughy, night shelter, and Rogernomics, twink and lotto (New Zealand-style) ... <i>(Feb 27, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>) [NOTES: (G. Kennedy)]

>  <b>MACRAE</b> It was a cheap loan but while she was on the DPB, more than enough to cope with. <i>(1988,Awful Childhoods,24</i>)

>  We thought bracken beds might be a good way of stopping DPBs procreating, but it was pointed out that they would have been gathering bracken from the roadside, and not paying transit New Zealand for it. <i>(Dec 28, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),124</i>)

>  She thinks the domestic purposes benefit (dpb) should keep up with the cost of living, but believes some people waste their benefit money... <i>(May 22, 2004,Dominion Post,E1</i>)

>  These days he's a solo father on the DPB. <i>(Aug 1991,Metro,60</i>)

>  "I'm getting $141 a week and they're trying to get me to pay $102 a week liable parent because my ex is still on the dpb." <i>(Dec 19, 1991,Dominion,9</i>)

>  In the early 1980s the National Government conducted an inquiry into abuse of the DPB.... Currently, almost one New Zealand child in eight is being raised on the DPB. Even so, the DPB is not a ticket of life-long dependence on the state. <i>(Feb 13, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

### DPMC
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Department of Prime Minister and Cabinet

>  The Department of Prime Minister and Cabinet (DPMC) has, at times, managed to ace the other two because its boss is <i>the</i> boss. Under this government, the Treasury has had everything confiscated but its calculators, and the DPMC is less a department than a secretariat to a very determined PM. <i>(May 5, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

### drabbie
 
 <i>n.</i> a drab, colourless person

>  FED UP. [a restaurant] 244 Ponsonby Rd. BYO. No bookings. Small, crowded with drabbies. Menu long overdue for a change, but still good value. <i>(Jul 1986,Metro,20</i>)

### dracaena
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Dracaenas and their other relationscordylines (cabbage trees) can be used togreat effect. <i>(Oct 1992,Metro,28</i>)

### drack
 
 <i>adj.</i> awful or ugly

>  <b>McGILL</b> drac(k) awful or ugly, as in Dracula. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,39</i>)

### draco
 
 <i>n.</i> short for <i>Dracophyllum</i> spp.

>  <b>HULME</b> We could walk past the peat bogs. But if you went off the planks you got quickly lost in the draco... [sic] <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,167</i>)

### dracophyllum
 
 <i>n.</i> genus of trees and shrubs

>  So lonely below the tupare and dracophyllum. It is inhabited by ghosts. <i>(Jan 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  Time out at the hot pools of Hanmer then.. climbing away from the beech forest into a West Coast garden of Dracophyllum, mountain holly, flax and harebells, descending into the Taramakau Valley. <i>(Mar 17, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C5</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> As we went on, the land became more and more barren and level, till it became a mere moor, without a shrub, and almost without vegetation; a few bushes of the miserable-looking Dracophyllum being all that occurred to break the monotony of the plain. <i>Ibid.</i> 100 I here first saw the great Dracophyllum; it formed a small tree about six inches in diameter, and twenty feet high; it is one of the most curious plants in the world; the leaves grow in tufts at the ends of the branches, just as in the dragon tree (Dracaena), and are the same shape, but in this species they are elegantly reflexed, like the feathers of a soldier's plume; they are a foot long and an inch broad at the base. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,53,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The forest [near Rotorua] was mostly tawai (Leiospermum racemosum), miro, and hinau; the Dracophyllum, which often grows to the size of a small tree, and the reddish leaves of which render it a very remarkable and beautiful shrub, was ... to be met with. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/399</i>)

>  We followed deer tracks zigzagging up through snow totara and <i>Dracophyllum</i>, then plugged up through soft snow to Brodrick Pass (5,308ft.), taking nearly two hours from camp. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,49</i>)

### draft
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Ibid.</i> 162 The climax came to a head, when on a draft of stud ewes paddocked handy and pushed in on the late afternoon, to make a handy mob before Saturday half-day gave all a welcome respite from duty. `... My extra choice store lambs are [pound]1 per head'. That evening a Mr. Wright ... asked casually if an offer could be procured of 800 to 1000 lambs for immediate fattening. `Yes,' said Jim, `I know of a draft,' roughly describing same. ...' `I leave to-morrow for Fielding [sic] to attend a sheep sale there, and may pick up some suitable fattening lambs.' <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,49</i>)

>  He obtained the good average of 49 guineas for his draft. <i>(Nov 22, 1937,King Country Chronicle,5</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> We had a small draft of sale ewes that year. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,88</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a group of animals separated from a main mob for a special purpose.

>  draft n. a body of cattle, etc., separated from a herd; ... <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  draft v.tr. to separate or sort cattle. n. the separated body of cattle. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),165</i>)

>  draft ... 7. Austral. & N.Z. a group of livestock separated from the rest of the herd or flock. ... 12. Austral. & N.Z. a. to select (cattle or sheep) from a herd or flock. b. to select (farm stock) for sale. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,336</i>) [NOTES: [See NZPOD 226, DRAFTING; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 146]]



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> All hands drafting at woolshed - <i>(Jan 3, 1867,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,91 ed.</i>)

>  <b>T.C. KIERNAN</b> C.H.S. and T.S. drafting and putting shorn sheep out to the paddocks. <i>(Feb 27, 1879,Diary,91</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Feb 27 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie Smith Tutira 127]



 
 <i>v.</i> to remove certain animals from a group by separating them or by driving them up a race and removing them through a drafting gate.

>  <b>DUNCAN</b> First we had to draft [the sheep] off and boat them across the arm of the lake. <i>(1888,Wakatipians,64</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Arriving at Tarndale, we drafted off the woollies and sent them to Molesworth to be shorn. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,37</i>)

>  Draft off: Removing certain animals from a group. This can be done by driving the animals out of a group (cut off or drift off) or driving them up a race and removing them through a special gate (drafting gate). <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,5</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to sort (farm animals) into particular groups; to separate a large mob into smaller groups.

>  <b>BOWEN</b> With the race and gates shown sheep can be drafted six ways. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,123</i>)

>  The sheep drafted in through a race from the yards into pens running down the centre of the shed, were taken from these pens by the shearers. <i>(Sep 23, 1924,Otago Witness,54</i>)

>  finished drafting wethers which were put accross [sic] Wedderburn ... Number including hogs about 1540 <i>(Nov 11, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  ... got a small mob into the yards 19 of which we selected and it took us the rest of the day to draft them ... <i>(Apr 11, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,32,1950 ed.</i>)

>  The armies of sheep were only being drafted (that is sorted) for sale. <i>(Mar 28, 1867,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/237,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> Dagging the hoggets, or drafting the rams. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,9</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The sheep ... are ... placed in smaller ... enclosures adjoining the wool-sheds, from which are drafted, from time [243] to time, a number sufficient to keep the pens ... full. <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,242</i>)

>  Draft: (i) To divide stock into classes, either in a yard or (cattle) by cutting out (q.v.). In the old days sheep were hand drafted, i.e. lifted over a rail or manhandled through agate. (Butler A First Year in Canterbury Settlement],p.135) About 1864 it was found possible to run sheep one at a time through a drafting race and move a single gate as they came, so drafting into two lots. See double gate. Cattle are usually now drafted in a stock-yard (q.v.). See also shed. (G.P. W.) (ii) A number of stock sent for sale, e.g. `The annual Stonyhurst draft of four-year-old ewes will be offered.' When the draft is actually offered it is usually spoken of as a line. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>CHERRILL</b> Sometimes she would draft a mob to the stock yards, but ... prices ran low. <i>(1950,Story of a NZ Sheepfarm,113</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> Presently Larry was summoned to help draft the sheep. <i>(1953,Breakfast at Six,34</i>)

>  draft ... v.i. to sort cattle, etc., into different groups. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  draft v.t. to separate or sort cattle; n. separated body of cattle. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Drafting sheep: The process of dividing a mixed mob into sexes or ages or any other required types. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,253</i>)

>  When witness had finished "dagging," hedrafted the sheep. <i>(Oct 24, 1914,Stratford Evening Post,2</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to sort or separate stock, especially sheep, for the purposes of sale, breeding, pasture management or treatment.

>  Finished drafting wethers ... <i>(Nov 11, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> Dagging the hoggets or drafting the rams. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,9</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Mustered the sheep out of the house paddock, put them in the yards and drafted them. <i>(Oct 8, 1914,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> A few more minutes and Bill would have been there to help him; now he had had a box, and the sheep would all have to be drafted. <i>(1954,On the Hill,26</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Draft off or drafting: Removing certain animals from a group. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,5</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an animal or group of animals that is separated from others in the process of drafting for sale, treatment or culling.

>  Sent a draft to Isolated Hill by upper road. <i>(Dec 12, 1856,St Leonard's Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Brought Pahau draft up from Pukeroa and we drafted off all shorn sheep. <i>(Nov 25, 1915,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> There was endless labour in paring the hoofs of the limping brutes, in running the lame drafts through the arsenic troughs. <i>(1921,Tutira,157</i>) [NOTES: [1999]]

>  <b>WILSON & FRASER</b> .. after the boiling-down works opened at Woodlands, large drafts were sent there every year. <i>(1936,Book of the Pioneers, The,30</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The coastal flats of Homewood and the adjoining properties are great fattening country, producing big drafts of fat stock. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,48</i>)

>  <b>WATERS</b> Put the rams out early, lamb early, get your fat lamb drafts away early ... <i>(1976,Mostly a Farmer,78</i>)

>  The last draft killed at Affco averaged 367 kg and 92% were in the P1 or top grade. <i>(Sep 4, 1997,NZ Farmer,13</i>)

### draft off
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to select or separate animals within a group or mob.

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Drafted off the steers from the heifers. <i>(Nov 23, 1915,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Drafted off the tops. <i>(Apr 6, 1931,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  Contrary to common practice, I don't draft my lambs off before shearing. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,501</i>)

>  If rams remain with the ewes for more than 4 weeks, they should be raddled on the brisket so that the late-lambing ewes can be drafted off. <i>(Mar 1955,NZ Journal of Agriculture,264</i>)

>  These [twinners] are all drafted off and held in separate paddocks. <i>(Apr 25, 1968,NZ Farmer,51</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> When we had mustered all we could catch, the lambs were drafted off their mothers and turned out ... <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,231</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> shed: (ii) (v) to draft off sheep or cattle in the open paddock. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,71</i>)

>  The top 100 calves are drafted off at weaning ... <i>(May 19, 1993,NZ Farmer,19</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> All the sheep had to be mustered on the station and the sale lambs drafted off. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,65</i>)

### draft out
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to select or separate stock within a mob or group.

>  Draft out the ewes and bring them to the scrub pen. <i>(1847,Pharazyn Diary,147</i>)

>  Drafted out 1200 sheep. <i>(Mar 18, 1863,Haldon Station Diary,249</i>) [NOTES: in Vance (1965) High Endeavour]

>  <b>BALFOUR</b> Drafted out the run sheep. <i>(Mar 6, 1873,Balfour Diaries</i>)

>  <b>BALFOUR</b> Drafting out the run sheep from the Leicesters and the rams. <i>(Mar 7, 1873,Balfour Diaries</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> .. after next shearing we'll have a good mob of sheep to draft out and stock it. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,94</i>)

>  Drafted out strangers and ewes and lambs. <i>(Feb 16, 1888,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

>  Some shepherds make a practice of drafting out the ewes as they lamb and put them on to better feed. <i>(Sep 1, 1925,NZ Farmer,1329</i>)

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> As lambs that are thriving can carry a heavy worm burden without being significantly affected it is essential to draft out the unthrifty and 'tail-enders'... <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,131</i>)

>  <b>FOSTER & WRIGHT</b> There the 'dirties' - the sheep in need of dagging - and the rams are drafted out. <i>(1983,Stockman Country,142</i>)

>  Draft out and milk cows with clinical mastitis in a separate mob, after the main milking herd. <i>(Oct 1999,NZ Dairy Exporter,51</i>)

>  Watch for shy feeders and draft out when necessary. [draft off] <i>(Feb 28, 2002,Fencepost.com,Expert farmer Livestock</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Templars sheep came in the morning - Drafted out most of Newton's - <i>(Feb 18, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: [cf cut out also used]]

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> Draft out rams as flock is dipped and keep them in a separate flock. <i>(1949,Sheep and Sheepmen of Canterbury,46</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Duppa in 1949 Crawford Sheep and Sheepmen of Canterbury 46]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> You will see fit to draft out all the lambs that are ready for weaning. <i>Ibid.</i> 154 Should you be drafting out sheep or taking your rams out, let the sheep ... be let into yards D and E. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,x 153</i>)

>  <b>T.C. KIERNAN</b> Drafted out all woolly sheep and took them to the shed. <i>(Feb 27, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Feb 27 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie Smith Tutira 127]

### draft up
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to draft.

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Drafted up small poor wethers. <i>(Feb 9, 1931,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Drafted up most from No 3 and 4. <i>(Feb 17, 1931,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Brought sheep in, picked out the tops, then drafted up some odds and ends. <i>(Feb 26, 1931,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

### drafted
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  It being too late to get down the river tonight I only crossed the drafted cattle over one stream and took them on one mile. <i>(May 5, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,181,1950 ed.</i>)

### drafter
 
 <i>n.</i> one who drafts stock.

>  Draft'.er (dr&adot.ft'.&etilde.r), n. One who drafts; esp., one who separates sheep into flocks in a drafting yard. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2021</i>)

>  drafter n. man engaged in drafting cattle. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the person who operates a drafting gate or selects stock.

>  .. the carcase was bruised through the drafter having pulled the wool of the lambs. <i>(Mar 1931,NZ Farmer,221</i>)

>  Drafters, like Alex Guy, are experts in their field and use their ability and experience to ensure maximum returns. <i>(Nov 27, 1975,NZ Farmer,97</i>)

>  At the height of their working season, drafters are intensely busy. <i>(Sep 12, 1985,NZ Farmer,27</i>)

>  The drafter couldn't believe that we hadn't touched them apart from docking them. <i>(Jun 15, 1994,NZ Farmer,21</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> The drafter's arms work the gate as though computerised. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,81</i>)

>  A number still use a stock agent. If you are using an agent you should check the consistency and accuracy of your drafter against the killing sheets. <i>(Dec 21, 2001,Fencepost.com,Expert farmer Livestock</i>)

>  They were drafted commercially, and the drafter didn't know if he was taking a lamb from a little ewe or a big one. <i>(2002,Wool Grower,Spring 21</i>)

### drafting
 
 <i>n.</i> the process of separating stock from a main mob

>  Finished drafting and when we came to take the rams out ... we found 40 of them had nocked down a rail ... <i>(Jan 9, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,117,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PEACHE</b> Think some got out as made out there were 14,000 in hand after drafting. <i>(Jan 21, 1883,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1883 Jan 21 Peache Jnl in 1970 Gray Quiet with the Hills 63]

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> drafting, culling, cutting and tailing [etc] <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,9</i>)

>  .. whenever the mobs were mustered for ...shearing or drafting, these `cranky' [that is a genetically shortwinded race of sheep of Strath Taieri] (as they came to be called) were left behind. <i>(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/173</i>)

>  On a cattle-run the tasks of mustering and drafting the stock and branding the youngsters are very heavy work, needing the display of considerable powers of rough- riding among thehorsemen; while on a sheep-run the lambing and shearing seasons tax every power of the station-hands. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,157</i>)

>  After a number of years, when sheep were mustered in from the back ranges, it was noticed that several would die in the yards ..., for they would require to be kept in for drafting on the following day. These dead sheep would be mostly sheep which had missed a shearing, and were double-fleeced, having very long wool. <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/278</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> The yards were small and inconvenient, and of a temporary character, and the work of drafting was difficult. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,79</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> A new hand ... is wise investing in a good huntaway or a heading dog. He can practice [sic] on the `killers' i.e. the sheep kept handy for station use, or better still, a good noisy yard dog, as allstation hands are liable to be called upon to assist in drafting, etc. <i>(1913,Country Work,33</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> One who was present has told me of a cattle drafting on the Beaumont Run, above Wrey's Bush. ... what with cutting-out the beasts wanted, rounding-up the remainder, dodging wild charging brutes, ... there was some ... exciting work done that day. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,44</i>)

>  drafting n. NZ & Austral. drawing off or selecting livestock for particular purpose; drafting gate swing-gate at end of drafting race; drafting pen pen to hold selected animals; drafting race narrow alley down which animals are driven for drafting. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,226</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Four-way drafting is a feature of the design. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,125</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the dividing of farm stock into separate mobs for sale, breeding or other purposes.

>  <b>WATERS</b> He would arrive at a farm in his gig, tie the horse up to a fence and get on with the drafting. <i>(1976,Mostly a Farmer,38</i>)

>  <b>WATERS</b> Be it understood the drafting of fat stock for the overseas market can not be done by the man in the street. <i>(1976,Mostly a Farmer,39</i>)

>  <b>DREAVER</b> When the shearers knocked off, there would still be drafting and shedding up for the musterers and we would all get home for a very late tea. <i>(1994,Catlins River Wisp Run,57</i>)

### drafting gate
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BOWEN</b> The sheep are also more easily seen by the man on the drafting gate when walking up hill. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,119</i>)

>  In drafting one sheep witness thought wasaccused's ran into his lot and witnesscould not catch it at the drafting gate. <i>(Oct 24, 1914,Stratford Evening Post,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a two-way gate in a drafting-race for separating farm animals into special groups.

>  <b>RIVES</b> After dinner we draughted out strangers and woolly ones and a devilish hard job I had standing at one of the draughting gates counting out the woolly ones. <i>(Feb 26, 1875,Jottings on the Spot,7</i>)

>  Drafting gates 3ft. wide. <i>(Sep 20, 1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,220</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> In later years we had stockyards at different parts of the Station where the drafting was done. The cattle were driven up a narrow race, with double gates at the further end, which were worked by a man standing on a platform. These yards were specially built for handling wild cattle, and had narrow gaps between the hinge of the drafting gate and post, through which a man could escape from a charging beast. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,136,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> He could hardly tell his own buggy horses ... and would never take the drafting gate ... <i>(1946,Early Canterbury Runs,153</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> DRAFTING: The main feature of sheepyards is the drafting race. This race, perhaps eight yards in length, is only wide enough for sheep to pass through in single file; at the end of the race are two swinging gates which can be manipulated to turn the sheep into different yards, and so separate them into different `lines' or mobs. <i>(1949,High Country Days,194</i>)

>  drafting-gate A gate used in separating cattle and sheep into classes or herds. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),131,8th ed.</i>)

>  drafting gate n. gate used to separate sheep or cattle <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> The boss was there working the drafting gate - two-tooths to the left-hand gate, six-tooths to the right. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,1</i>)

>  Draft: Removing certain animals from a group. This can be done by driving the animals out of a group (cut off or drift off) or driving them up a race and removing them through a special gate (drafting gate). -->to remove certain animals from a group driving the animals out of a group (cut off or drift off) or driving them up a race and removing them through a special gate (drafting gate). <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,5</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) one of a pair of gates in a drafting race that is used to separate stock into two or three mobs.

>  <b>CAIRD</b> .. men have to go in on foot to force the animals through drafting gates ... <i>(1874,Notes on Sheepfarming in NZ,23</i>)

>  Drafting gates 3 ft wide. <i>(Sep 20, 1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,229</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> As the man at the drafting gates is practically indispensable, the man filling the crush pen is largely responsible for keeping an even flow of sheep going up the race. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,75</i>)

>  <b>MALLOCH</b> For 65 years he raddled lambs for that firm and even in his 80s he could be seen clutching the drafting gate at Waikaia Plains. <i>(1992,From the Tops,112</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Yards are full, race is full, gates are latched .. except the little one by the drafting gates! <i>(1996,Trouble with Stock Agents, The,40</i>)

### drafting pen
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MULGAN</b> There were stables and ... a huge wool-shed and drafting pens. <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,314</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> This drafting race divides sheep into the separate lots required, and these are then handled by the drafting pens, check pens, and holding yards, which should correspond in size to the receiving yards. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,120</i>)

>  <b>WALLIS</b> There a baler was a brochure number, a header a price. Somewhere outside ... there were salesmen talking to farmers, ... sitting astride the drafting pens discussing dips and shearing machines and drug dispensers ... <i>(1963,Point of Origin,18</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pen in a stock yard that is adjacent to the drafting race.

>  In larger sets of yards one of more of these drafting pens communicate via a gate ... <i>(Jul 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,7</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> This drafting race divides sheep into the separate lots required, and these are then handled by the drafting pens, check pens and holding yards, which should correspond in size to the receiving yards. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,120</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLL</b> There should be as many drafting pens as there are ways of drafting. The check pens should be adjacent to the drafting pens and should equal them in number. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,334</i>)

### drafting race
 
 <i>n.</i> a race for drafting stock into smaller mobs. See cit 1966.

>  <b>Geary</b> A drench salesman had sworn he'd once seen Craven break alkathene across the rear of one hyena-looking huntaway that wouldn't stay out of the drafting race. <i>(2003,Man of the People, A,132</i>)

>  <b>W.H.S. ROBERTS</b> At Macfarlane's the men saddled fresh horses and rode in some cattle which we had a great bother to yard, as everything was new ... The yard was divided into three - one large and two small - but as there was no crush pen, or drafting race, we had to head rope those requiring branding. <i>(Dec 18, 1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Dec 18 W.H.S. Roberts diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 43-44]

>  <b>PERRY</b> [The sheep yards] should consist of receiving yards at each end, forcing pen, and drafting race. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,16</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> This drafting race divides sheep into the separate lots required, and these are then handled by the drafting pens, check pens, and holding yards, which should correspond in size to thereceiving yards. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,120</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> `Teviot' had a magnificent stone and steel woolshed [built 1880] ... with two complete drafting races, much wool storage room, two ancient screw presses, and stands for 26 blade shearers, for this was the era before the Ferrier Woolpress, ... <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,17</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> DRAFTING: The main feature of sheepyards is the drafting race. This race, perhaps eight feet in length, is only wide enough for sheep to pass through in single file; at the end of the race are two swinging gates which can be manipulated to turn the sheep into different yards, and so separate them into different `lines' or mobs. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,186</i>)

>  At times foot-rot troughs are at the bottom of the drafting race and covered over.. when not in use. <i>(1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,229</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a long alley through which stock are driven for separation for sale, culling or for processes of dipping, troughing etc.

>  <b>PERRY</b> They [sheep yards] should consist of receiving yards at each end, forcing pen and drafting race. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,16</i>)

>  Now begins the long struggle at the drafting race. <i>(Mar 23, 1967,NZ Farmer,33</i>)

>  All the drafting races were in yards that have seen many years service. <i>(Sep 12, 1985,NZ Farmer,27</i>)

### drafting stick
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a waddy or pointer, used in drafting yards.

>  Those modifications [to a farm bike] include carriers back and front, and holders for shepherd's crooks, a drafting stick etc. <i>(Jun 7, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,25</i>)

### drafting yard
 
 <i>n.</i> an enclosed yard into or out of which stock are drafted; also pl., the collection of pens, yards, races used esp. for handling stock.

>  <b>ALLOM</b> `I will conclude this part of the subject with a few words about stockyards. They are generally made of stout posts and rails; the corner posts ought to be much stronger than the rest, as they often have to bear the weight of a heavy bullock in branding and other operations. The yard is generally divided into three portions - viz., a large yard for the whole herd, a drafting yard, and a milking yard, at one end of which is placed the milking shed, cow bails, and calf house, the whole being so arranged that a calf can be let out to suck its mother on one side while she is being milked on the other.' <i>(1853,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 123 (Wairarapa)]

>  Drafting yard and cow bails. <i>(1849,Notes on NZ,26</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> ... use a hurdle-yard for shearing ... have your permanent drafting-yard elsewhere. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,90</i>)

>  <b>J.G.WILSON</b> Donald and Mick went over to the Bush at Sandy's to cut posts for the Drafting yard. <i>(Apr 29, 1874,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1874 Apr 29 J.G.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 186]

>  <b>T.C. KIERNAN</b> T.C.K. went to Tutira to mend gates at drafting-yard. <i>(Feb 19, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Feb 19 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie Smith Tutira 126]

>  <b>PERRY</b> Sheep Yards. - The erection of drafting yards is one of the first improvements to be attended to by the young sheep farmer. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,16</i>)

>  <b>DARE</b> We had to move the whole lot to the drafting yards. <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,167</i>)

>  drafting-yard A yard or enclosure for drafting cattle. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),131,8th ed.</i>)

>  drafting-yard n. yard divided into compartments for drafting sheep or cattle into groups. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Barbara ... dawdled across the horse paddock by the homestead heading for the dusty, noisy, drafting yards. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,1</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a stockyard and race used for sorting stock into separate groups.

>  Hours spent in the drafting yards mean, often, unpolished floors. <i>(Oct 29, 1927,NZ Dairy Exporter,36</i>)

>  These two pictures show two completely different approaches to the problem of providing cattle drafting yards. <i>(Sep 14, 1961,NZ Farmer,7</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The drafting yards on the ground floor are all of dressed timber. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,76</i>)

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> .. when a difficult muster is completed and the sheep are in the drafting yards and holding paddock ... <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,113</i>)

>  <b>FERNADEZ</b> Barbara and Kathie dawdled across the horse paddock by the homestead heading for the dusty, noisy, drafting yards. <i>(1975,Tussock Fever,1</i>)

### drag
 
 <i>v.</i> to hold others up in an activity

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Drag the chain. To be at the rear in a race or in a game (of, e.g., cribbage): New Zealand: C. 20. Niall Alexander, letter of Oct. 22, 1939, `The ploughman's term to designate his slow horse that does not keep its chains tight'. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1035</i>) [NOTES: 1939 in 1949 Partridge Dict Slang Addenda 1035]

>  <b>PROF. W. CAMERON</b> A trampers' term applied to those who go very slowly (uphill). <i>(1951,Personal Communication</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> See cit. Not otherwise recorded.

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Drag. v. ... -3. v.i. and v.t., to take a portion of the stakes in a gambling game as a reserve for future play: Australian and New Zealand: C. 20. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,240</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to search thoroughly [[?Is this merely an extended metaphor thus OMIT???]]

>  See him [sc. a shepherd] drag a gully full of tomos and patches of scrub and guarantee ... you wouldn't find one straggler. <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,127,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton `Saving the Breed')]



 
 <i>n.</i> the pull with a whale in tow back to the mother ship. [whalers']

>  <b>Capt. Joy</b> Second Mate had his boat robbed ... of a bottle of rum, it being customary to carry it here so that in case of a hard drag to give it to the boats crew ... <i>(May 6, 1836,Log</i>) [NOTES: 1836 May 6 Capt. Joy Mary Mitchell log in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 439]



 
 <i>n.</i> a drag-saw. [(sawmillers')] (See [also drag-tooth](../A#also-drag-tooth))

>  <b>MANDER</b> By day the whole bay vibrated with the whistle and screech of the circular saws, the tear of the break downs, the rasp of the drags, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 198 They watched the progress of the log from the booms up the skids to the side of the breakdown platform... Halved and quartered, it was then levered ... on to greased rollers and rushed towards the big circulars, which turned it into flitches. Then the small circulars, the drag, and the goose completed its metamorphosis into the regulation strips ... <i>(1920,Story of a NZ River, The,159,1974 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a three-month gaol sentence.

>  According to a well-known Christchurch demon, the slang of three months in thieves' argot is `a drag'. `Critic' thought a 'tec was the `drag' when he pinched 'em. <i>(Aug 1, 1908,NZ Truth,163/1/5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [dragstone](dou-dyn.md#dragstone))



 
 <i>n.</i> in full drag ball [quartzmining]

>  These tailings are treated in berdans with extra quicksilver andground up. The berdans now in use at the Thames ... are generally five feet in diameter ... At one time a couple of rotating balls were considered sufficient for the amount of crushing required; now, the general practice is to have a loose ball as well as a stationary one attached to a chain,and it is called a drag ball; this drag does more work than a loose ball ... for the drag grinds the bowl as much as the tailings. <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/179</i>) [NOTES: (Goodall Thames Gold-fields)]

### drag hunt
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a horseback hunt where the quarry is not live game, but a sack or sheepskin which has been impregnated with anise or a similar strong smell, dragged over the course.

>  <b>CHINN</b> A Drag Hunt Club was formed. The quarry in this type of hunt was not live game, but a scent was laid down before hand by a sack or sheepskin, impregnated with some strong smell such as anise, being dragged over the course.' <i>(1963,Packtrack to Highway,42</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Arthur Barker at Winchester and W Postlethwaite at Raukapuka had packs of beagles too, and the type of hunting indulged in then was the 'drag-hunt' when a bag doused with aniseed was dragged along the course to be followed by the hounds. <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,396</i>)

>  <b>HAYES</b> Jasper Nicols was one-time Master of the Drag Hunt, which was a picnic outing for those who could not ride but still came in buggies to watch. <i>(1978,From the Toss of a Coin,90</i>)

### drag off
 
 <i>v.</i> [Not recorded elsewhere: but OMIT???]]

>  A tearful Putaruru Mongrel Mob leader admitted in the Napier High Court yesterday that he wastrying to `drag off' another car when his car smashed head-on into an oncoming station wagon and killed a man, his wife and two of their three children. <i>(Jul 15, 1987,Dominion,3</i>)

### drag one's hook
 
 <i>phr.</i> to leave a place

>  <b>CRUMP</b> I think it's time we dragged our hook, Sam. It's about time a man had a little holiday. I can feel the itchy feet coming on. <i>(1962,One of Us,105</i>)

### drag rake
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an implement used to clear drains etc.

>  <b>STACEY</b> The creeks and streams had not been cleared for years, and it was backbreaking job cutting the weeds into manageable sizes and pulling them out to the bank with a drag rake. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,39</i>)

### drag the chain
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [shearing] to work slowly.

>  Drag the chain, to. To be the slowest shearer in a shed. <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> From the New Zealand shearing sheds came those effective expressions to drag the chain and swing the gate, phrases applied to the slowest and fastest shearer in a shed respectively. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,39</i>)



 
 <i>phr.</i> to be slow or behindhand in any group or competitive activity. [(mainly shearers and drinkers)]

>  Drag the chain, to.-To be the slowest shearer in a shed. <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>BAKER</b> From the New Zealand shearing sheds came those effective expressions to drag the chain and swing the gate, phrases applied to the slowest and the fastest shearer in the shed respectively. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,39</i>)

>  Drag the chain: To be the slowest shearer in the shed. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,366</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> To drag the chain is a shearer's term meaning `to be a slow worker'. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,122</i>)

>  dragging the chain: A shearer is said to do this when he lags behind the rest because he is slow, lazy or inexperienced. The origin is from bullock driving where it is said that the rear bullock attached by a chain to the wagon, `drags the chain' if it slows down. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/24</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> But stop dragging the chain. Drink up and have one on me. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,45</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Stop dragging the chain supplies a need where the etiquette of drinking requires rapidconsumption of liquor: it means `hurry up with your drinks so that the next round can begin'. <i>Ibid.</i> 131 To drink more slowly than the group is `to drag the chain'. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,122</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Shagging about like an old moll at a christening - Dragging the chain - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,164</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Shagging about like an old moll at a christening - Dragging the chain - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,164</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> dragging the chain holding up a group's activities, such as a drinking school; originally the slowest shearer in the shed; eg `Larry's dragging the chain again on keeping his berm mowed.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,39</i>)

>  There's old timers we knew, and some who shot throughA hard case, drag the chain, done a bunk,As busy as a blow fly, and bugalugs - from the boo-aiThe rouseabout, as drunk as a skunk. <i>(Dec 2004,NZ Memories,p18</i>)

### drag tooth
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAINES</b> Cross-cut saws have what is called a peg-tooth and cut both ways. It is a great improvement to sharpen every fifth tooth, counting from the centre to the ends, as a drag tooth, that is, a tooth filed square across and with a considerable hook, this arrangement tends to keep the cut clear of sawdust. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,173</i>)

### drag-tooth saw
 
 <i>n.</i> a drag saw [-] (See [also drag n](../A#also-drag-n))

>  ... crosscuts known as drag-tooth saws. <i>(1951,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: [ca1914] 1951 H.S. McCarroll The Days of the Kauri Bushmen [Radio Talk 2: TS]]

### dragger
 
 <i>n.</i> [(Post Office technical)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Some of his best days had been spent working as a postal assistant ... He had been a spreader in the mailroom and a dragger on the landing. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,85</i>)

### dragline
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ...fully 20 cars start up and rumble out of the parking lot... The dragline is in operation. The convoy rolls south down the motorway, keeping in touch with each other in the thin traffic by flashing their hazard lights. <i>(Jan 26, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

### dragon
 
 <i>n.</i>

### dragon fruit
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Dragon fruit: Otherwise known as pitaya and grown in the Bay of Plenty, this fruit is usually exported but you can find its distinctive shape in chic fruit and vegetable shops. <i>(Jun 16, 2002,Sunday Star Times,D1</i>)

### dragon-palm
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> In spite of this warning I climbed down a steep bank to the cave, but found no bones, only a beautiful group of dragon-palms - a rare coincidence. The leaves of these Dracaenae - called by the Maoris tohi-palms - possess flexible fibres out of which the natives weave their waterproof rain-mats (pureke). <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,176,1933 ed.</i>)

### dragon-tree
 
 <i>n.</i> cabbage-tree, ti [??occas. nikau].

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We met with a great many Cabbage-palmtrees, [Cordyline australis] as they were commonly called by our Sailors, but upon Examination we found them to be a kind of Dragon tree (Dracaena Linn): for we met with the fruit and flowers of them. <i>Ibid.</i> May 13 2/277[?nikau???] ... an infinite variety of high trees & shrubbery [in Dusky Bay], among which theNew-Zeeland Dragon-tree (Dracaena antarctica) [ed. fn Cordyline australis] is very remarkable; (NB Our Sailors called them the Cabbage-palm, but it is different; though the middle most leaves may be eaten & taste almost like sweet fresh Almonds). <i>(May 7, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/270</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> [??=nikau] These trees, however, were not the true cabbage-palms, nor did they belong at all to the class of palms, ... They were properly speaking a new species of dragon-trees, with broad leaves (dracaena australis) of which the central shoot when quite tender, tastes something like an almond's kernel, with a little of the flavour of cabbage. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/176</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> A species of Dragon-tree, the central shoot of which partook at once both of the flavour of cabbage and of an almond kernel. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/247 [=??nikau, though prob. Cordyline cabbage as Andersen says: Nicholas ref. to Forster 1/176 above] They [Cook's people] found here also ... a new species of dragon-tree, the central shoot of which partook at once both of the flavour of cabbage and of an almond kernel. [reference Forster 1/176] <i>(1817,Voyage Round the World,2/245</i>) [NOTES: [page no and cit. need rechecking]]

>  <i>(1841,NZ Journal</i>) [NOTES: (Andersen) [=TI]]

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> I here first saw the great Dracophyllum; it formed a small tree about six inches in diameter, and twenty feet high; it is one of the most curious plants in the world; the leaves grow in tufts at the ends of the branches, just as in the dragon tree (Dracaena), and are the same shape, but in this species they are elegantly reflexed, like the feathers of a soldier's plume; they are a foot long and an inch broad at the base. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,100,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Ti - sweet root of the dragon-tree <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/387</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The tropical-looking dragon-trees (dracaena), called ti by the natives, and the karaka-tree, grow up the steep sides of the hills ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/9 [ti] Onwards, through gently undulating fern-land, interspersed with bushes of phormium tenax and an occasional dragon-tree, we againfound belts of forest-land. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/28 A grove of dragon-trees (Ti). <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/240</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> .. the sides of the [Wellington] hills ... are almost tropical in their character, from the abundance of the ti, or dragon trees, with which they are covered. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),92</i>)

>  `When this name was given the tree was Dracoena australis.' <i>(Jan 1856,Edinburgh New Philosophical Journal,(NS)</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Jan Tancred Notes on Natural History Canterbury]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Cordyline</i> belongs to the sub-family Dracoenoideae, and is therefore related to the dragon-trees. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,9</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HARPER</b> In places [in the Otira district] a stunted mountain birch grows freely, and a curious Dracena, or dragon tree, with a seed that resembles a pineapple, of deep red colour. <i>(Jul 20, 1868,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,130,1914 ed.</i>)

### dragonet
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>DRAGONET Foetorepus phasis (Callionymus phasis, Yerutius phasis)</i> ... [285] They ... give off a very strong smell when captured, giving rise to an alternative common name of stinkfish. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,284,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] dragonet 1989 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  A stranger from the deep arrived at the Canterbury Museum yesterday - a rare dragonet fish caught recently in 90 fathoms of water by the crew of the Lyttelton based trawler Ida Marion. ... The fish appears to be confined to waters off the east coast of the South Island and southern Australia. <i>(Sep 10, 1987,Press, The,2</i>)

### dragonfish
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [starry dragon](spa-sys.md#starry-dragon))

 <i>n.</i>

### drags
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Tom Pollard and the members of his company became the guests of the officers of the contingent, driving out to the camp in drags. <i>(Jan 18, 1900,Otago Witness,57</i>)

### dragstone
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This faulting has effected a considerable `drag' of the vein to the eastward ... <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.4 (NS) 114</i>) [NOTES: (Fraser and Adams, Coromandel)]

>  This north-north-east fault effects a vertical displacement ... and its `drag' has caused the conglomerates to assume the steep dip of 70 [degrees] to the east-north-east.] -->[?Definition:check geol.] <i>(1934,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,16/125</i>) [NOTES: (Branch and Dagger)]

>  Buhman further casts doubt on the so-called `dragstone,' theposition of which influenced prospecting for the lost portion, and doubts its value as an index to the direction of movement; he mentions that drag quartz diminished when followed up the fault-plane and along the strike of the fault, whereas the amount of dragstone should have increased as the lost portion of the ore-body was approached. <i>(1938,NZ Geol. Memoir,No.4/74</i>) [NOTES: (Modriniak and Marsden, Reefton)]

>  <b>No.42 (NS) 53</b> ... i.e., that the continuation of the lode was relatively upthrown south-west of the fault, as appeared to be suggested by the up-turning of the lode against the fault and drag-stone. ... The channel may be offset, but dragstone should indicate in which direction to seek. <i>(1948,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin</i>) [NOTES: (Gage, Reefton)]

### drain weed
 
 <i>n.</i> starwort.

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> STARWORT (Callitriche verna [spp.]), sometimes called drain weed or even duckweed, is not uncommon in still and slow-running water. ... [It is native to New Zealand.] <i>(1928,Weeds of New Zealand,129,1967 ed.</i>)

### Drainage Board
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  Mr Bell by some means has got the Drainage Board to do all the draining through his property as somehow his land is left out of any drainage board. <i>(Nov 28, 1909,Chudleigh Diary,450,1950 ed.</i>)

### draining
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  For nearly six weeks I have worked hard from daylight to dark, stumping, draining and scrub-cutting. <i>(Jul 11, 1928,NZ Free Lance,7</i>)

### draining pen
 
 <i>n.</i> a pen in which animals emerging from a dip can shed surplus liquid for draining back into the dip.

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> At the end of the dip are two draining-pens, used alternately. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,88,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Dad was `crutch' man and also responsible for timing each dip full of sheep before opening the gate and letting them out into the draining pens. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,48</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pen where sheep are held after dipping, so that drainage is returned to the dip or bath.

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> At the end of the dip are two draining-pens, used alternately. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,88</i>) [NOTES: (1935)]

>  <b>BRABAZON</b> John boarded draining-pen, filling dip ... <i>(1908,Brabazon Diaries,14</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The draining pen will be made on the highest ground, the floor at the lower end, namely at the gangway ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,22</i>)

>  The sheep have to be directed to the draining pen. <i>(Dec 1, 1928,NZ Farmer,1481</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> The yards consist of two essential parts; a) crush pens leading to the dip; and b) drainage pens leading from the dip. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,358</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Dad was crutch man and also responsible for timing each dip full of sheep before opening the gate and letting them into the draining pens. <i>(1991,Off the Sheep's Back,48</i>)

### drainlayer
 
 <i>n.</i> [The common term in NZ - see Yellow Pages where "drainlayers" is a heading]

### drake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> DARNEL (<i>Lolium temulentum</i>), sometimes called drake, is an annual a good deal like Italian rye in appearance, not uncommon [occasionally found] in cultivated fields and waste places in both islands. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,47,1967 ed.</i>)

### drapes
 
 <i>n.</i> curtains [OED - chiefly US]

### drapetes
 
 <i>n.</i> an alpine plant, <i>Relleria dieffenbachii</i>

>  The common drapetes, <i>Relleria dieffenbachii</i>, a plant commonly found in alpine fellfields from the Coromandel mountains to Stewart Island, is named in his honour. <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/49</i>)

### draught(ing)
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [draft(ing)](dou-dyn.md#draft(ing)))

### draw
 
 <i>v.</i> in absol use, of a harpoon, to draw out. [(whalers')]

>  1 P.M. saw a whale ... Mate got fast but his Iron drew - <i>(Jul 16, 1836,Piraki Log,39</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(Northland bushmen's)] (See [also sliver](../A#also-sliver),[stringer](spa-sys.md#stringer))

>  <b>ROY GILBERD</b> [The fibres of wood in a tree just about to fall] do not part all in one plane and `stringers' or `drawers' are drawn out of the barrel, sometimes several feet long. Sometimes men on contract, trying to save timber, place their saw-cut too low and some of the wood-fibres break off up in the barrel. These are `stringers' while they are breaking, and `slivers' or `drawers' when they are left upright in the sloven. <i>(1950,Letter</i>) [NOTES: (Northland)]

>  <b>REED</b> A `draw' was a serious defect, but was not due to any fault in the living tree. When a tree fell before it was quite severed, it sometimes, in the act of falling, wrenched or `drew' out a portion of the butt, leaving great jagged, fang-like splinters adhering to the stump. The damage was, of course, always to the heart wood ... [<i>Ibid.</i> 156 [Edward Thompson] ... very often, before the saw got within a foot or so of the front scarf, the tree would start to fall gradually, and the uncut timber between the saw and the front scarf would have to break, and seldom cleanly; the heart, being more brittle than the sap, would break here and there, several feet above where thesawcut would have been standing straight. As the tree fell, these pieces, still attached to the stump, would draw out of the tree, and being almost heart-wood constituted the chief defect we call `draws'. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,151</i>) [NOTES: [?c1900]]

### draw a marble
 
 <i>phr.</i> win an opportunity

>  Phil Moore's introduction hints at a possible larger gathering next year, but in Hawkes Bay, so even if I'm lucky enough to draw a marble for it, I have still missed a chance to look at those crucial Gisborne areas on the ground in company with some geologists whose competence and judgement I respect and admire. <i>(Jan 1983,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,59/68</i>)

### dray
 
 <i>n.</i> In NZ of two main types,(1) a high-platformed two-wheeled shafted single-horse-drawn cart with sides, often a `spring dray'; and (2) in early settlement, a bullock-dray-->, a usu. four-wheeled pole-wagon without sides (occas. draycart--> (OED2 1719) as in:

>  <b>WAKFIELD</b> ... except for the conveyance of heavy goods, for which dray-carts drawn by bullocks are chiefly used, locomotion [in Wellington] is much easier on foot.] See also pack-dray. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,97</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to transport by dray.

>  <b>C W RICHMOND</b> We found him away 'draying' Mr Moore's wool. <i>(Oct 21, 1857,BLANK ENTRY,318</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers I]

>  <b>FULLER</b> Such tracks .. for draying down the squatters' produce. <i>(1959,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,149</i>)

>  Drayed down goi on R ridge. <i>(Jul 16, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  .. peat sheds, from where peat dust was drayed on the paddocks ... <i>(1862,BLANK ENTRY,92</i>) [NOTES: in BEATTIE (1947) Early Runholding]

>  Wilson draying scrub to Clark. Chaff cutting and draying stones to dam. <i>(Apr 29, 1867,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> My house..is being cut out in Christchurch and will be drayed to our station next month. <i>(1869,Station Life in NZ,39</i>) [NOTES: Station Life (1874)]

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> There is mention in the station diary of a contract to 'dray out' the wool for the coming season. [dray in, dray down] <i>(1959,Guthrie-Smith of Tutira,35</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [[fn 1971 Smithyman p.210 fn4] Drays. Used after the colonial fashion, for a heavy two-wheeled cart.]

>  <b>ALEC WHISKER</b> One of the Drays broke down about one mile from the camp with shot and rum on it. <i>(1846,Memorandum Book,21</i>)

>  A ford has been opened for drays opposite the seventh mile post on the North road, and a culvert and bank have been placed over the Kaikainui creek. <i>(Jul 1, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  Two horses were then attached to the dray ... <i>(Jul 1863,Invercargill Times</i>) [NOTES: 1863 July Invercargill Times in 1971 Watt Centenary of Invercargill 14 (Griffiths Collection)]

>  Runs have to be stocked in 12 months [which] sometimes expires about shearing time. ...[sic] many up country runs in Nelson are inaccessible ...[sic] for drays ... I have ...[sic] agreed ... to consider stock as having been on the run in time immediately after shearing. <i>(Jul 3, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/54,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  <b>LYTTELTON</b> ... but drays are much used, open in front and behind, and like an improved costermonger's barrow. <i>(1868,Two Lectures,31</i>)

>  <b>McKEE WRIGHT</b> And the fork tines flash as the sheaves are turned on the frame of the one-horse dray; <i>(1900,Wisps of Tussock,58</i>) [NOTES: (`In the Moonlight') in Centennial Treasury of Otago Verse]

>  dray n. a two-wheeled cart. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  dray ... NZ & Austral. two-wheeled cart (bullock-dray). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,228</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 147]]

>  <b>BURR</b> Mr Jensen has another dray in hand of asimilar kind for a settler in Carnarvon. <i>(1995,Mosquitoes & Sawdust,73</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from Manawatu Times 15 Nov. 1882]

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> He kept talking to the man forking offthe dray. <i>(1977,Gus Tomlins,73</i>) [NOTES: Original edition 1938]

>  <b>GORDON</b> The dairy farmers.. came to town inhorse-drawn milk waggons, or by horse andgig, or by bullock drays. <i>(1956,Back-Blocks Baby-Doctor,133</i>) [NOTES: 3rd edition]

>  <b>McLAGAN</b> While not riding horseback we went aboutin a farm dray. <i>(1965,North & South,23</i>)

>  <b>BRADLEY</b> [They] were all in the heavy carryingbusiness with horses and drays. <i>(1982,Great Northern Wairoa, The,158</i>)

>  <b>NOTT</b> I would harness up the old horse into thedray and swing the cans of cream into thedray to go off down to the wharf to putthem on the mail launch which came intwice a week to deliver the mail andstores and collect cream. <i>(1944,Sound Living,12</i>)

>  When we got to the top of the range, we took off the wheels, parked them on the dray, put on runners, and made a sledge of the turnout to pull it down to the flats. <i>(Jan 25, 1934,Feilding Star,4</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  [Caption] A photograph cherished by many of the Stanley descendants is one of the fanily in the bullock dray taken with a background of buildings in Cathedral Square, Christchurch, in the 1860s. <i>(Apr 2004,NZ Memories,No. 47/44</i>)

>  He is a new chum only landed that day. He was getting on the dray to ride just behind the off polar and of course got cicked. <i>(Dec 16, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,114,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Met Boys and dray coming up. <i>(Feb 6, 1850,Torlesse Papers,129,1958 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [?bullock dray]]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a dray with a tilt over it.

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> He borrowed Thomas Thomson's big tilted dray and drove to his old home. <i>(1956,Pioneer Recollections,5</i>) [NOTES: 1947-1948 Beattie Pioneer Recollections (1956) 5]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> [Cits needed. Used in Havelock. CurlyHawkins had one.]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> dray drawn by two horses in single file

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> TANDEM DRAY: Drawn by two horses in single file. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,189</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 189]



 
<b>4</b> <i>v.</i> convey on a dray.

>  We found him away `draying' Mr Moore's wool. <i>(Oct 20, 1857,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/318</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  I have arranged with Johnny Jones that he shall have a large shepherd's house ... and woolshed - to be drayed up so as to have no waste timber. <i>(1858,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/423</i>)

>  J Hepburn came and said drays were at steep hill and that Hood had drayed down Hopkinsons wool to Waikowaite spit ... <i>(Feb 26, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  Drayed stones and pitted potatoes <i>(Aug 14, 1862,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> Such tracks may become available ... for draying down the squatters' produce. <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,149</i>)

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> ... and [we] dray any wool. <i>(1949,Sheep and Sheepmen of Canterbury,47</i>) [NOTES: 1860 September Duppa in 1949 Crawford Sheep and Sheepmen 47]

>  ... they met another [boat] on the Waimea Plains being drayed up. <i>(Jan 10, 1863,Otago Witness</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>LADY BARKER</b> My house ... is being cut out in Christchurch, and will be drayed to our station next month. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,39,?1869  1874 ed.</i>)

>  dray ... NZ & Austral. two-wheeled cart (bullock-dray) <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,228</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 147]]

### dray-cloth
 
 <i>n.</i> tarpaulin [(obs.)]

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> We stretched the dray-cloth, or tarpaulin, full-spread over the loaded dray, pinning it to the ground all round, spreading heaps of wool-bales which it had brought, underneath the wheels tolie on. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,72</i>)

### dray-pole
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> ... the driver had succeeded in freeing an axe which was ... lashed to the guard-irons, with the intention of cutting the dray-pole, and so letting the team escape. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,169</i>)

### dray-road
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rough track that accommodates a horse and dray.

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The proprietors constructed a dray-road up the steep side. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,II 159</i>)

>  ... to give Messrs Illston and Holgerson a dray and pack road for their sections would be as follows:- Six chains of additional side cutting near Holgerson's whare ... <i>(Apr 17, 1880,Hawera Star,03</i>)

>  If from the first they had access to their holdings by a dray road ... <i>(Jan 30, 1905,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> It also, in olden days before the advent of a dray-road, played an important part in station activities. <i>(1921,Tutira,188</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> It [the grass] has survived changes necessitated by the widening of the pack-trail into a bridle-track, and that again into a dray-road. <i>(1921,Tutira,285</i>)

### dray-rope
 
 <i>n.</i> a rope used to secure the load on a dray

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> After picking up our fourteen pigs, - and with the assistance of a couple of dray-ropes piling them on the sledge as securely as dead pigs may be piled, - we started for home ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,155</i>)

### dray-track
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) [also dray-road]

>  <b>FULLER</b> Improvements to a Run ... [consist of] partial cuttings for a dray-track when required to be made. <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,149</i>)

>  .. a practicable dray-track ... a good bridle-track. <i>(1862,AJHR,E-9-24</i>)

>  <b>MURRAY</b> When such bridges, culverts and crossings ... as cannot be dispensed with are made, the track which is often on the line of an old bridle-path, becomes a `dray track.' <i>(1866,Description Province Southland,19</i>)

>  Mr Acland and I went to the bush, the dray track is nearly impassable. The empty dray with 8 bulocks [sic] went in up to the axle for 20 yards together. <i>(Jul 30, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,97,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> ... some old Australian diggers, engaged in cutting a dray track ..., discovered the precious metal in sufficient quantity to create a fever of expectation and a `rush.' <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,198</i>)

>  Communication with the interior of Otago, in the 'fifties ... was slow and difficult; and it was not until 1860 that money was forthcoming to make a dray track from West Taieri to Black's station . <i>(1931,Old Coaching Days,19</i>)

>  dray ... NZ & Austral. two-wheeled cart (bullock-dray). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,228</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 147]]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rudimentary path that accommodates a wheeled cart or dray.

>  .. floods do not do much harm; suffice it is to say that tons of soil must be annually removed from the dray-tracks and water-tables of the roads to gullies and lower levels where the power of the current becomes spent. <i>(Jul 15, 1898,Clutha Leader,6</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> A protection is elsewhere granted to the squatter in entitling him to compensation for the improvements he has made upon his Run - that is to say for buildings, fences, partial cuttings for the dray-track where required to be made ... <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,149</i>)

>  Went up Stony Creek to look for dray track and place for hut. <i>(Mar 1, 1865,Haldon Station Diary,253</i>) [NOTES: in Vance (1965) High Endeavour]

>  He has not lost his appetite for land, but the bridle track, much less the dray track, was not there ... [dray track] <i>(Nov 10, 1903,Hawera Star,2</i>)

### dray-wheel
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> With a load each way [on the bullock-dray] it was a fortnight's work to go to the coast and return ... - and the dray-wheels at night for shelter. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,166</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> `Madcap' was a dark bay horse ... with one very bad habit, that of ... trying to jam his rider's legs against the nearest object; whether that might be a passing dray-wheel ... <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,207</i>)

### draying
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Stock owners have been enabled to complete their draying operations with ease. <i>(May 13, 1857,Lyttelton Times,8</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Then follows the draying of the wool to port, and the bullocks come in for their full share of work. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,x/159</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... most stations have a bush near to the homestead, or greater facilities for draying than we possess. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,62,1870 ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the transporting of wool, farm animals and other produce by dray.

>  <b>FULLER</b> He may add to his income by draying, if he possesses a team, for others in the vicinity ... <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,135</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> .. he may save that money [of a station close to town] in a few years by the reduced expense of draying ... <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,154</i>)

### drayload
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The polers, that is the bullocks attached to the pole of the dray and accustomed to bear the weight of the dray-load on their necks, are always the quietest. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,234</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BARKER</b> It was preceded by two dray-loads of small rough-hewn stone piles, which are first let into the ground six or eight feet apart: the foundation joists rest on these, so as just to keep the flooring from touching the earth. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,49,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Round Preston's run [c1860 matagouri] had been killed by by fires ... leaving blackened sticks which could be gathered by drayloads and carted away for firewood. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,120</i>)

### drayman
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BARKER</b> [We have to] hire a drayman who possesses a team of bullocks and a dray of his own, to fetch it to us: ... <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,62,1870 ed.</i>) [NOTES: CHECK 1867]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> In Shag Valley draymen and others can get charred timber on the hills ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,129</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> In Shag Valley draymen and others can get charred timber on the hills ... <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,68</i>)

### dreaded lurgy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  STRONG winds and a heavy swell at the weekend helped dissipate huge stretches of mud-like growth which has drastically reduced Taranaki fishing catches. Nicknamed grey mud or the dreaded lurgy, the growth has stretched from Mokau, 70 kilometres northwest of Waitara, to Cape Egmont. <i>(Nov 20, 1984,Dominion,4</i>)

### dredgable
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>EVANS</b> This is a valley about nineteen miles in length and containing over 5,000 acres of dredgable land. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,43</i>) [NOTES: 1906 Mining Handbook 276 in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 43]

### dredge
 
 <i>n.</i> In NZ usu. associated with gold rather than oysters.



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to take out, find with difficulty; to extract in a slovenly fashion, or with difficulty.

>  <b>CRUMP</b> The stack of firewood stood waist-high beside the chopping block. There was little enough to see ... I'd sat on the wood block, dredged out the letter Jim Reed had given me, and worked the flap open with a knife. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,14</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  It didn't take long for gold-hungry miners to figure that there would be plenty of the precious meral buried in the bed of the Clutha, and a primnitive spoon dredge was built to attempt recovery as early as 1864. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 65/49</i>)

>  [Caption] Gold was what [Seddon] principally had in mind., as, no doubt, would the operators of this early "spoon" dredge. <i>(Apr 1997,NZ Geographic,No. 34/59</i>)

### dredge  oyster
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The commercial fishery for dredge oysters, <i>Ostrea sinuata</i>, is confined to Foveaux Strait. <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,48</i>)

>  The dredge oyster, Bluff oyster or Foveaux Strait oyster is by far the most popular in New Zealand. <i>(May 1985,Metro,47/28</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Dredge oyster Tiostrea lutaria</i> ... Alternatively Bluff, Foveaux Strait, or Stewart Island oyster. Native to New Zealand, with a similar species in Chile. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,166</i>)

### dredge paddock
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  When a fall of earth takes place in the paddock the dredge is working, the ladder has to be lifted clear of the fallen mass, and then lowered again for the buckets to get their loads. <i>(Jul 8, 1897,Otago Witness,20</i>)

>  <b>EASTWOOD</b> A prospecting tunnel from Aynsley's old sluicing claim on the Grey River ran under the dredge paddock. <i>(1982,Brief History of Gold Mining, A,86</i>)

>  The starboard bow line slackened during the heavy flood, causing the dredge to get athwart the current, when she filled and sank in her own paddock, which contained about 12ft of water. <i>(Dec 4, 1897,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

### dredge pond
 
 <i>n.</i> the pool, constantly being formed as the dredge moves forward and filled with tailings behind, on which a gold-dredge floats

>  <b>EVANS</b> `Lines' here refers to ropes, and the punt for carrying the coal across the dredge pond to the dredge. <i>Ibid.</i>80 A Mr. Lusk was drowned in one of the United dredge ponds ... <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,38</i>)

### dredgie
 
 <i>n.</i> a dredgeworker.

>  <b>PEARSON</b> But, bugger me, these bloody miners and dredgies come along and spoil it. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,275</i>)

### dredging
 
 <i>n.</i> gold-dredging

>  <b>BLYTH</b> <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11 [sic]</i>)

### dredging claim
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka dredging-claim; see wet claim, beach claim, et al]

>  Crossing the Beaumont Bridge the road follows the west bank of the Clutha, passing numerousdredging-claims. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,59</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> Meeting a miner on the road, he asked him if he knew the site of such-and-such a dredging claim. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,120</i>)

### dregs
 
 <i>n.</i> beer slops

>  The word "dregs" refers to the drippings from the taps in a bar and to the overflow from the glasses as they are filled. These dregs are sometimes called "swill".. or "slops". When they are mixed with beer for sale the resultant liquid is colloquially known as "Wompo". The term has been in use among barmen for as long as one experienced licensee could recollect - from twenty to thirty years.., and it is known to the man in the street. <i>Ibid.</i> 115 The resultant "Wompo" was then served to the public. <i>(1946,AJHR,H-38/113</i>)

### drench
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Advt] Every dairy farmer risks avoidable loss if he neglects to drench both before and after calving. <i>(Jan 31, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,15</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> The next time they drove past the light they'd leave it there, whether it was a box of staples, a gallon of drench, or Enid's old gumboots. <i>(1955,Moaville Magic,69</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>HILL</b> This figure is not an escapee from some biochemical battleground. It's Merv Toohill, off to drench his yearlings. <i>(1988,More from Moaville,19</i>)

### dress
 
 <i>v.</i> prepare (flax)

>  He desired us to state explicitly the desirability of all flax-millers dressing flax thoroughly. <i>(1898,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,VI/239</i>)

### dress shorts
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [walk shorts](../W#walk-shorts))

>  Can we, he inquires, wear dress shorts to work? <i>(Mar 19, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A3</i>)

### dressed to the indie nines
 
 <i>phr.</i> ref. to Indian pop culture

>  Rob is immaculately dressed to the indie nines, wearing gleaming white sneakers upon his feet. <i>(Nov 2005,Tearaway,12</i>)

### dresser
 
 <i>n.</i> As well as referring to a kitchen sideboard, the word is used for a nbedroom chest of drawers, with or without mirror. [Jim Cameron writes : OED, NSOD and Collin tag this use as <i>NAmer.</i> It seems to be well-established in NZ.]

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> He read for a while in bed.. put the marker in the book, closed it and put it on the dresser. <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,135</i>) [NOTES: check quote]

>  Nothing in it but two single beds, an outdated dresser with knobs. <i>(1960,Tangahano,251</i>) [NOTES: Check quote]

### dressing-shed
 
 <i>n.</i> a changing-room or building at beaches, swimming-baths, etc. Also called bathing shed, changing shed. [ca1940 at least Havelock `bathing shed']

>  <b>IHIMAERA & LONG (ed.)</b> In the dressing sheds afterwards, Tua cut Joe's hair. <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,131</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> True he had come up through all the grades, progressing from foxing in the riverbed to the knotholes in the girls' dressing sheds at the baths <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,47</i>)

>  <b>COWLEY</b> As they changed in the dressing sheds, Julie-Ann said ... <i>(1971,Man of Straw,110</i>)

### drift
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The gold is obtained partly as alluvial gold from deposits of gold drift (`wash-dirt') of the miner. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),101</i>)

### drift off
 
 <i>v.</i> to remove certain animals by driving them out of a the main group. See also drift 1. Compare draft off. [(farming)]

>  Draft off: Removing certain animals from a group. This can be done by driving the animals out of a group (cut off or drift off) or driving them up a race and removing them through a special gate (drafting gate). <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,5</i>)

### drift-gold
 
 <i>n.</i> gold found in gravel or sand drift.

>  `There is a nuggety or mammillated appearance about drift-gold which reef-gold does not present. <i>(1890,TrNZI,22/403</i>)

### drifting
 
 <i>n.</i> [boy-racing term]

>  Drifting means hitting, say, a 50kph corner at about double that and swinging round the curve like a rally car on gravel. <i>(Jan 26, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> working a claim by driving tunnels (See [also drift n1](../A#also-drift-n1))

>  <b>MAY</b> DRIFTING: working a claim by driving tunnels. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,526,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> DRIFTING: working a claim by driving tunnels. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,526,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> The term driving generally referred to tunnelling in any direction but in some circumstances, where reefs were present, it meant tunnelling along the line of the reef or parallel to it. The term drifting was used more specifically for driving on a reef, while the term crosscutting referredto tunnelling across the reef up to an angle of say forty-five degrees; however it was common to talk of `driving a crosscut'. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi,184</i>)

### drink
 
 <i>n.</i> [?Watchmakers' ?kerosine or oil; ? a clean.]

>  <b>HISLOP</b> ... I got busy on a Peep-o-Day alarm clock ... Very nice business I may quite easily have walked away from had that alarm clock not required a drink. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,84</i>)

### drink with the flies
 
 <i>phr.</i> to drink alone [cf. Jimmy Woodser]

>  <b>Picard</b> `What's yours? Ain't 'aving any, right oh, I'll have a Jimmy Wood's with the flies. <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,12</i>)

>  The self-respect that was in him has departed, and knowing not when he will get another, he drinks deep of what he lays his hands on, and deteriorates into a Jimmy Woodser and absorbs hisliquid with the flies, and sheds any small remnant of rectitude that he might have had about his clothes. <i>(Jul 25, 1908,NZ Truth,162/4/6</i>)

>  What was `Arry's lotion? Did he have `a handle' of beer every time, or just a drop o' Scotch? And was he on his ace, drinking with the flies, or did he shout for the boys like a toff? <i>(May 29, 1909,NZ Truth,205/7/3</i>)

>  He apparently thinks that he has but to dangle his bait and the fish will bite but `Dr.' Chapman should remember that a fish hooked does not necessarily mean caught, and that many of hislast converts may have been `drinking with the flies'. [N.B. Paragraph NOT re alcohol] <i>(Jan 18, 1913,NZ Truth,395/4/5</i>)

>  When last seen, the wowser-bird was fluttering off to have one with the flies. <i>(Sep 2, 1918,Quick March,1</i>)

>  With the flies, to drink.-To drink by oneself. I don't think that this phrase is peculiar to sheds and stations, although that is where I have most often heard it used. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss. additions]

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `That's about it. Have you got a drink?' `Sure there's a drink. You're welcome. I was just thinking of having one with the flies.' <i>(1959,No Remittance,194</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> He drank with the flies for twenty minutes and smoked a couple of cigarettes. The faces of the others in the bar did not interest him. <i>(1974,Maori Woman,166</i>)

### drink-boating
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Authority director Russel Kilvington said "drink-boating" was as serious a problem as drink-driving. <i>(Jan 8, 1997,Evening Post,3</i>)

### drinking funnel
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DONNE</b> Drinking funnel for tohunga when tapu. [caption] <i>(1927,Maori Past and Present,76</i>)

### drinking game
 
 <i>n.</i> a game or contest for drinkers involving some feat, the non-peformance of which involves finishing the drink in hand and starting the feat again.

>  Included in Hodges's study is the truly macho battle of alcoholic consumption - the drinking game. ... There'll be some inane feat of manliness to perform, such as a lewd tongue-twister, ora physical show of strength, and he who loses has to down his glass in a gulp. In Otago alone, Hodges found 42 varieties of the theme, with names such as `One Fat Hen and a Couple of Ducks' or the `Hokonui Swindle'. <i>(Oct 19, 1986,NZ Times,3</i>)

### drinking horn
 
 <i>n.</i> NZ Universities drinking contest, (? orig. with a horn as prize and a wooden spoon as booby prize), held during University Tournaments. -->ca1945 at least in Wellington

>  DRINKING HORN Mon 23rd Starts 12.15. <i>(Apr 23, 1979,Salient,9</i>)

>  The Drinking Horn is the big event of the year and included is the famous "Naked Skull" and "Beer and Cold Pie" race. <i>(Mar 1992,Metro,105</i>)

>  Vic's shooting team also won the Shooting Drinking Horn for the Newts Trophy. <i>(Apr 7, 1970,Salient,7</i>)

>  Not forgetting the celebrated "drinking horn". <i>(Sep 6, 1947,Sports Post,9</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The old bar has gone where we practised for the Drinking Horn contest [at university]. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,41</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> Gaping mouths, swallowing everything ... and spewing it up over the exam paper. Like a drinking-horn, all you had to do was to get it down soon enough and keep it down long enough. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,8</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He poured his way to selection for the Drinking Horn team. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,80</i>) [NOTES: [Canterbury University]]

>  The Drinking Horn, held later that afternoon, was compered by Ross Blanch, OUSA President-elect. The 8 oz Individual Sculls were again won by Philip McDonald, of the Southlanders' Society. ... The jug sculls, a simple matter of either being able to or not being able to were won by a Canterbury drinker. The University of Canterbury Student's Association had sent down a team for this event and the boat races. <i>(Jun 3, 1986,Campus News,2</i>)

>  A nameless person on whether Peter Fenton, UCSA President-elect is `politically unsound.' - `He must be: he organised this year's drinking horn.' <i>(Sep 8, 1986,Campus News,17</i>)

### drip
