 <i>n.</i> usu. pl., slices of fried bread. [ca1934 children's coll: Otago: Alistair Campbell]

### drippery
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [The stories] are inspiring, too, though Sefton spares readers the slavish drippery that runs through many New Zealand sports books. <i>(Oct 23, 2004,Dominion Post,E11</i>)

### dripping pen
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pen adjacent to a sheep dip, where sheep are stood to drip/drain and drainage returns to the dip. (See [draining pen](dou-dyn.md#draining-pen))

>  <b>SCOTT</b> .. instead of sliding comfortably down a shoot [sic] into their sixty seconds' bathe and walking leisurely upstairs to the 'dripping pen' the sheep were jammed into a small yard. <i>(1922,Reminiscences of a New Chum in Otago,18</i>)

### drive
 
 <i>v.</i> to fall a group of trees whose trunks have been partially cut through, and so placed that the impetus of falling a chosen one will set off a `chain reaction'. See drive n2.. [(bushmen's)]

>  <b>BELL</b> Harry had just given the last cut of the axe to a big rimu, which, after a far premonitory cracks as it began to move, fell, driving before it in its line of fall a dozen or so of the smaller trees, which had been `scarfed' or cut partly through in readiness, and skilfully, so that each, when struck, might again in its turn strike and bring down another. The noise of a fall or drive of this kind is like thunder - loud, prolonged, reverberating. <i>Ibid..</i> 94 Harry had planned a big drive with a combined rimu and rata ... had `scarfed' all the smaller trees in front of it down to the already felled bush, and expected, when it was brought down, that it would send to earth the whole of these, driving one upon another far beyond the reach of its own length ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,83</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The three [15] brothers spent the first few weeks clearing, learning the art of chopping, scarfing, driving and working a face. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,14</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [machine shearing] to push through the wool with a deliberate stroke.

>  <b>BOWEN</b> This results in the wool often pulling the skin out, which makes it sometimes difficult to 'drive' with a full comb without marking the sheep. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,133</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BOWEN</b> ... the Corriedale produces a big weight of wool, and yet has the tender, loose skin of the Merino. This results in the wool often pulling the skin out, which makes it sometimes difficult to `drive' with a full comb without marking the sheep. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,133</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the falling of a group of trees by partially cutting through their trunks and falling a specially chosen tree so that the impetus of its fall sets off a `chain reaction'. [(bushmen's)]

>  <b>BELL</b> Harry had just given the last cut of the axe to a big rimu, which, after a far premonitory cracks as it began to move, fell, driving before it in its line of fall a dozen or so of the smaller trees, which had been `scarfed' or cut partly through in readiness, and skilfully, so that each, when struck, might again in its turn strike and bring down another. The noise of a fall or drive of this kind is like thunder - loud, prolonged, reverberating. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,83</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> [drive n and v.t. described as scarfing trees in a line (on a rise), then falling the back one to push the rest down in a line.] <i>(1911,In Maoriland Bush,61</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> Even the most experienced men sometimes get caught by a falling tree or branch, or clearing a `drive' that has missed. <i>(1913,Country Work,31</i>)

>  <b>KENWAY</b> Selecting a certain group of trees he will `belly scarf' and `back scarf' the lot ... If this `drive' as it is termed, has been well-managed the whole lot will came down together. <i>(1947,Quondam Quaker,53</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... from early morning till dark the axe would resound and the crash of falling trees, or drive of trees, would be the only sound ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,240,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Jack learned about scarfing, backing, limbing, ... toms, strops, drives, triggers, ... <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,42</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> The bushmen developed an expert knowledge of driving, or directing one tree against another, or a body of trees, which swept a great weight in one irresistible drive of crashing and thunderous timber. <i>(1952,Faring South,179</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He had seen as many as twenty-four men in a single [professional bushfalling] gang and they worked `drives' of up to five acres and more [in the King Country]. <i>(1972,Wake of The Axe,117</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> It was quite a common practice for experienced bushmen, to have a `drive'. If you are chopping up a `face' and there is a large tree behind you further up the face, you select that as the `key'. To save time ... you do not waste time by chopping down each tree individually, but just `scarf' and partly chop each tree. When all is ready and you have worked back to the key, you scarf it verycarefully. ... If everything goes according to plan it will `drive' the one below, which will in turn drive the next, and so on ... <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,22</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> A `drive' was in progress; as the men do the underscrubbing they note the large trees and their distance from each other. They scarf each one so that it will fall on the next. Then, after all the smaller timber has been brought down, they fell the first tree which falls on the second and so starts a whole sequence of crashes. The line need not be straight. More often, it zig-zags from right to left. Bushmen can do wonders by means of an ingenious drive. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  [quartz] Taking the claims as they came, the first ... consisted of a drive of about a hundred feet into a prominent looking `spur'. <i>(Oct 27, 1867,Trip to the Thames Gold-fields,27,1978 ed.</i>)

>  If he [sc. a mining manager] goes out in a buggy for a day hecharges it to the company as `putting in a fresh drive' <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...,31</i>) [NOTES: (Thatcher on Mining Companies)]

>  <b>BAINES</b> Our reef, or leader, measuring only four inches in thickness, a drive for a considerable distance following the course of the gold-bearing quartz had to be carried out, ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,262</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... the work was both difficult and dangerous, being a drive or tunnel made into the side of the hill, ... blasting the rocks that were too big ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,80</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> In London the swells in the park drive all round, But the diggers drive here with a pick underground; And the props that they use, surely everyone knows Ain't the same used in London for hanging up clothes. The women use fans* there, the men use them here, Though to cockneys that statement may sound very queer, And the fellows that frequently hang round hotels, Out here are called loafers, at home are called swells. <i>Ibid.</i> [p 89 fn] * A fan is a machine for pumping air down the shafts. <i>(1875,NZ Songster,No.3/88</i>) [NOTES: London & the Diggings]

>  <b>LAPHAM</b> There was a deserted `drive' up on the spur. <i>(1880,We Four,18</i>)

>  But Steel had already disappeared down the winze. ... The whole roof of the drive fell in. <i>(Nov 1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,107</i>)

>  Mr Coombs took me down [the Waihi mine] and ... we went into every drive 900 ft. down. <i>(May 16, 1907,Chudleigh Diary,441,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PURNELL</b> Then rushed the [coal] miners from their hollow `drives'. <i>(1912,Modern Arthur, The,17</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> Drive: A horizontal tunnel. <i>(1947,Bright Fine Gold,200</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> (coalmining) ... drives were broken off at right angles to [the dip heading] at distances of two chains apart ; thus forming pillars on either side of the dip, two chains square. These drives were called main levels. The bords were driven about two chains off the dip, and the `stenton' would be driven from the lower level to the one above. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,169</i>)

>  <b>BLYTH</b> Drive: A horizontal drift, or tunnel, in a mine. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> DRIVE: a horizontal excavation. As a verb (`driving') to make a horizontal excavation. `Drive' and `tunnel' were used interchangeably, but properly one tunnelled into a lead or reef and then commenced to `drive' along it. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,526,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McCARTHY</b> Paddy made bigger money than most of the miners, taking on contracting work in driving the levels, or drives. These drives were the roadways where we trucked the stone from the stopes. To get down to drive each level ... <i>(1973,Listen...!,54</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> Drift or drive: A tunnel usually driven parallel with the reef. a passage or tunnel excavated horizontally (cf v4) <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),319</i>)

>  <b>NOLAN</b> Drive: same as drift (above). Incorrect, but sanctioned by common usage. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,112</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Rained all last night, ... made a shorter drive to the [Arowhenua] bush. <i>(Nov 25, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,154,1950 ed.</i>)

>  ... I finde I shall clear about 73[pounds] out of my two drives 50[pounds] out of Mr Harris and the rest from Mr Acland. <i>(Dec 2, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,155,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to tunnel (horizontally) through country or ground. Also drive out. Contrast sink (v) (vertically). [goldmining]

>  Went down several more feet with our pit. One of the digers [sic] helped us and he came to the conclusion we should have to paddock it which is to work it like a quarry, large at the top instead of driveing levells [sic]. This man and his mates afterwards worked it in this way ... Theygot colour. <i>(Jun 3, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,41,1950 ed.</i>)

>  The washdirt was `paddocked' or `driven' out, and carried or carted to where there was water. <i>(1906,Mining Handbook,204</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> Immediately north of the [Waikaka] township a rich layer in the fault-involved older quartz drifts, ... was worked from the surface ... This layer is locally known as the `back lead'. On or near it a shaft was sunk ... but was not utilised for driving-out. ... Somewhere about 1880 ... others drove an inclined drift from point to the southward of the back lead for a distance of 254 feet ... Then they drove horizontally for a short distance and intersected an auriferous ... layer of quartz wash, about 10 feet wide, known as the `front lead'. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,4</i>) [NOTES: 1914 P.G. Morgan report Geological Survey Branch 147 in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 4]

>  No. 3 adit ... has been driven 750 ft. in a formation of crushed and sheeted country traversed by numerous stringers and veinlets of quartz. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/14</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> DRIVE: a horizontal excavation. As a verb (`driving') to make a horizontal excavation. `Drive' and `tunnel' were used interchangeably, but properly one tunnelled into a lead or reef and then commenced to `drive' along it. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,526,1962 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1973 see n3]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Messrs. Nelson Brothers, of the meat-freezing works at Tomoana, kindly offered to receive and paddock any black lambs forwarded ... to their works until such time as a drive of black lambsshould be collected together. <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/197</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(axeman's) Compare punch @v. [nf discts.] =?]

>  Axe-men's contest [showing chops] ? the stroke which leads up to severing the block. <i>(1983,TV1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [[Can't find this at drive: check let.]]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Charlie, ever on the lookout for making fun of me, said that it was just as well that Dick and he were the only two in the vicinity, as a stranger would think I was a deer barking and let drive at me from the bush, ... <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,43</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a mass of floating logs driven downstream. See v1. [(Northland kauri bushmen's)]

>  Where [? When] there are sufficient logs for a "drive," the dam is "tripped" during a"fresh" or flood. <i>(Mar 9, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,33</i>)

>  By means fair and foul the logs were assembled in creeks to await a big flood called a 'drive' that would float them out to tidal water. <i>(Apr 2003,NZ Memories,No. 41/50</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> When all was completed I had one hundred logs rolled into the stream below, and thirty above the dam. I then fixed upon a day and an hour to have a `drive,' and mustered all the men I could. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,176</i>)

>  Often it is necessary to construct costly dams to impound the water in the upper parts of the creek, in order that it may be suddenly liberated ... so that the entire assemblage [332] of [kauri] logs may be `driven' to the booms. In dry seasons the logs may remain in the bed of the creek for months, and, occasionally, after a successful `drive', the harvest of logs is carried out tosea and lost, through the booms breaking from the immense pressure behind them. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,331</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> ... [the booms] often enclose an area of several acres, which after a successful `drive,' is crowded with thousands of logs. <i>(1889,Forest Flora,151</i>)

>  [The dam] looked well for a good `drive,' and the boss gave the word to `trip' it and let her go as soon as the logs were straightened up. <i>(1906,Red Funnel,2/16</i>)

>  During a `drive' the logs, driven by the rush of the waters, scour out the bottoms of the creeks, and create artificial floods, by which logs, dragged to the creeks, may be transported. <i>(1913,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.16 (NS) 27</i>) [NOTES: (Henderson and Bartrum, Aroha and Hauraki)]

>  <b>REED</b> Other men, if available, would at the same time be preparing the creek bed for the `drive.' <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,221</i>)

>  The release of water and logs by tripping the dam was known as a drive. <i>(Sep 1991,Historic Places,28</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a horizontal mine tunnel

>  Drive : A horizontal tunnel on the reef <i>(Sep 1999,Ohinemuri Regional History Journal,43 : 14</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the shifting of a mob of sheep by driving, mainly from the point of view of the animals driven (`a drove'), but also from that of the action of driving (`a short drive').

>  I should like to get a drive or two before going to the diggins it would give one more pocket money. <i>(May 7, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,131,1950 ed.</i>)

>  [Mr Harris] asked me to bring a mob of sheep from Moorhouses ... for him, ... Have taken the drive and leave Mt. Peel on Thursday morning. <i>(Nov 7, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,152,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> The [sheep] drive ... continued with varying fortune for three weeks. ... We drove steadily by day, steering down [157] the plains due South, and camping at night as best we could; .. <i>Ibid.</i> 204 On the drive, or on the run, or in the yards,- ... they [sc cattle] just ... continuedwild ... and at shortest notice, dangerous. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,56</i>)

>  <i>(Feb 7, 1877,Chudleigh Diary,254,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> There was a fair muster of sheep in the [sale-]yards so we did not think we should have much trouble in getting a drive. <i>(1897,How I Stole 10,000 Sheep,28</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> There were sufficient [sheep] to make a `drive' out to the back. <i>(1949,High Country Days,55</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> For some years he was on the road for [the Richards] collecting cattle they had purchased, and was also on drives to Appleby. <i>(1973,Big Country,5,1977 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to drove

>  ... I mention these impediments more particularly, to shew the utter impossibility of ever driving stock or taking a horse coastwise to Canterbury, ... <i>(Mar 8, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> As a rule, to drive a mob of sheep for any distance, takes not less than four hands ... <i>Ibid.</i>It needs incessant care to prevent stragglers breaking away from the head and sides of the mob, and the rear of the flock, made up of the weaker sheep, requires an equally constant lookout. The horses are led or ridden, as the ground allows; ... but in driving through unknown country, the halts are made very much where the explorers find themselves at sundown. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,45</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Drive: In very rough bluffy country it is sometimes possible to muster, or `drive,' deer into some spot where they will be cornered or trapped, and a big kill can be made. <i>(1949,High Country Days,195,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> of stock, poss. more idiomatic in early NZ use than drove cf also driver/drover; driving.

>  `Reached sheep about two o'clock, - rounded them up and began driving at once. Reached ourproposed camping-ground about sundown. <i>(Apr 4, 1860,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Apr 4 notebook in 1874 Kennaway Crusts 111]

>  Breakfast before daylight, cutting out till 4 o'clock when we drove five miles along the sea shore, jamed [sic] the cattle in a bite and pitched our tent, boiled some of my cocoa <i>(Apr 16, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,32,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Sheep race before it [sc. southerly burster] as hard as they can go helter-skelter, ... There is no shelter on the plains, and, unless stopped by the shepherds, they will drive from one river to the next. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,viii/124</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a concentrated 'push' to destroy large colonies of rabbits or hares.

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> We were on a hare drive one day ... <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,22</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Ash Faigan also mentioned the 'drives' which were once a method of rabbit destruction. One such drive in the Waipiata district yielded over 4,000. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,93</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to execute so that the burn spreads progressively through the whole area. [?Or is this OED2 19 `Prosecute vigorously'?]

>  <b>RIDGEWAY</b> The burning off should be driven as late in the season as it is safe to do so. <i>(1860,Voices from Auckland,73</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> of a sheepdog (opposite of `pulling'!)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Clyde [a Kelpie] isn't an unqualified success at `driving' yet; but by the time we have finished shearing he will know as much as the next dog. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,48,1935 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 48]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to float (timber) down a river usually by releasing a dam. Also drive out. [(Northland kauri bushmen's)]

>  <b>BAINES</b> The `stand of kauri' I had acquired ...was estimated to contain upwards of a million feet of timber, capable of being `driven out' by placing a flood-dam in a suitable position ... too great a body of water filling the creek at one time for a `drive.' <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,170</i>)

>  The only other practical method I can propose for the preservation of timber is to `drive' the logs down to deep-water at once. <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/368</i>) [NOTES: (Brown)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> ... the sluices of the dams are opened, and the logs, which are often piled high in the bed of the stream, are `driven' to the booms by the volume of water suddenly liberated. <i>(1889,Forest Flora,151</i>)

>  Often it is necessary to construct costly dams to impound the water in the upper parts of the creek, in order that it may be suddenly liberated ... so that the entire assemblage [332] of [kauri] logs may be `driven' to the booms. In dry seasons the logs may remain in the bed of the creek for months, and, occasionally, after a successful `drive', the harvest of logs is carried out to sea and lost, through the booms breaking from the immense pressure behind them. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,331</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> Here [the kauri logs] lie until a `fresh' drives them down the creek or harbour where the `boom' lies waiting. <i>(1906,Plants of NZ,63</i>)

>  The logs were transported to the mill in the case of the Waitanui by a wooden-railed tramway; in the case of the Tamaki they were `driven.' <i>(1913,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.16 (NS) 19</i>) [NOTES: (Henderson and Bartrum, Aroha and Hauraki)]

>  It was a great dam and drove 10,000,000 feet of timber out. <i>(1951,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: [ca1914] 1951 H.S. McCarroll The Days of the Kauri Bushmen [Radio Talk 4: [TS]]]

### drive the pigs home
 
 <i>phr.</i> to snore

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>drive the pigs home</i> to snore; in England the phrase is: 'drive the pigs to the market.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,39</i>)

### drive-on
 
 <i>adj.</i> easy access by car [Jim writes : NSOD entry refers only to ship, a use that I suspect is uncommon in NZ. The principal NZ use is as a qualifier of <i>section</i> - drive-on section. Also drive-on access.]

>  Sunny, drive on section, with a babbling brook and mature trees. <i>Ibid.</i> 3 It has drive on with a double carport and extra parking. <i>(Sep 19, 2000,Independent Herald (Wellington),Suppl. : 2</i>)

>  You could be moving into a brand new, architecturally designed home for a very reasonable price if you've got your sights set on a home that:*is on a flat section * has drive-on access and garaging* is close to the city and beach. <i>(Sep 16, 2000,Dominion,18</i>)

>  Sunny, drive on section, with a babbling brook and mature trees. <i>Ibid.</i> 3 It has drive on with a double carport and extra parking. <i>(Sep 19, 2000,Independent Herald (Wellington),Suppl. : 2</i>)

### driver
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  In addition to these factors, other positive earnings drivers include lower churn rates (the precentage of residential subscribers over the last 12 mnonth period who terminated their subscriptions), an appreciating NZ dollar.. increased pay-per view revenue and the recent launch of its e-mail and interactive service. <i>(Feb 6, 2002,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  One driver for buying 3G is to make sure you've got insurance. <i>(Jul 15, 2000,Dominion,14</i>)

>  [They] believe that Telstra-Saturn's business drivers - details of the company's time, cost and resources - were added during the group's deliberations... The terms of reference included the business drivers and we accepted the terms on that basis. <i>(Apr 14, 2001,Dominion,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a sheep or cattle-driver, a drover. cf drive @n and v&sup4.,

>  <b>ELWELL</b> ... drivers [=drovers] <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,94</i>)

>  Drover: One who drives stock for a living. In Canterbury they were usually spoken of as sheep- or cattle-drivers until about 1900. <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a leather strap on the handle of a pair of shears, made to fit over the back of a shearer's hand.

>  Driver:A leather strap on the handle of a pair of shears, which fits over the back of the shearer's hand. (C.) <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> The shearers buy their shears from the station and then proceed to cut them and bend them, and embellish them with `drivers,' `knockers,' `jockeys' and `cockspurs' until their makers would hardly recognise them. <i>(1951,High Country,26</i>)

>  driver ... NZ & Austral. leather strap on handle of shears to prevent the shearer's hand from slipping on to the blades. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,229</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Driver. Leather or lampwick strap on the handle of a pair of shears. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)

>  driver: A leather strap on hand shears. This fits firmly round the handle and over the back of the shearer's hand, thus allowing more drive to be given to a blow while preventing the hands from slipping over the blades. Sometimes this is also called the `monkey'. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/24</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]



 
 <i>n.</i> [[Poss. OMIT???]]

>  The driver takes the trucks through the mine races, and deposits them in lay-bys, places similar to ... railway sidings. Here the trucks are received by the hangers-on, boys who attach the trucks to an endless rope. <i>(1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,IV/614</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [[check that this is not a miscopyng of drive].]

>  <b>BAINES</b> The former [sc a natural fresh] is not to be depended upon, too great a body of water filling the creek at a time for `a driver', whilst at another there would hardly be any flood at all. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,170</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a tree cut to push or `drive' others down

>  <b>LYON</b> Again, a tough old rata or hinau often had a tall pine felled and lodged in its branches, with the result that the weight of the driver and the lower tree were too much for the supporting one, and a few cautious axe strokes completed the job. <i>(1952,Faring South,178</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [Poss. OMIT??]

>  <b>GRADY</b> Driver - The man who drove a high-speed launch type of whale-chaser as used by the Peranos in a whale-hunt. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,226</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a leather flange attached to a sickle-hook or reap-hook for grass-cutting, to prevent injuries to cocksfooters' wrists.

>  <b>COULSON</b> Some men attached a leather flange known as a 'driver' to the handle to facilitate cutting and ease the swelling of wrists from constant movement. <i>(1979,Golden Harvest,38</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hand-fastening strap on handshears.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> The shearers buy their shears from the station and then proceed to cut them and bend them and embellish them with 'drivers', 'knockers', 'jockeys' and 'cockspurs' until their makers would hardly recognise them. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,26</i>)

>  A driver can be fitted, using a strip of leather or lamp wick. <i>(Dec 23, 1976,NZ Farmer,21</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> driver: a strap, usually of cotton lampwick, bound on to a pair of hand shears to fit round the back of the hand. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,27</i>)

### driver licence
 
 <i>n.</i> a state-issued authorisation to drive a vehicle [The British term is <i>driving licence</i>, which seems to be the one in general popular use in NZ.]

### driving
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> [(Northland (kauri) bushman's)]

>  You have been in that district, about the creeks where the driving of the logs takes place? <i>(1896,AJHR,I.-4A/20</i>)

>  ... the `driving' of large kauri-logs in the creeks by `tripping' dams during flood-time ... [98??] ... Recently the creek-beds have been thoroughly scoured ... owing to the `driving' of kauri timber by lumbermen. <i>(1912,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.15 (N.S.) 34</i>) [NOTES: (Bell and Fraser, Waihi - Tairua)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [[?f. drive v2.]]

>  <b>EDMUND BOWLER</b> Hard work today driving (droving) owing to long grass. [The `droving' is Mayhew's insertion] <i>(Jan 1, 1858,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 1 Edmund Bowler's Diary in 1949 Mayhew Tuapeka 12]

>  I wanted a [?] driving sheep, but he would not agree to that. <i>(1861,McLean Papers,20/8</i>) [NOTES: (Hawkes Bay)]

>  Pattison and I have the driving of 1500 Wethers from this run to town ... and I have the driving of a mob down and back ... in a few weeks I hope. <i>(Dec 2, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,155,1950 ed.</i>)

>  ... I had not time to call on Tripp ... so he has given his driving to O'Callaghan, a stranger instead of me. <i>(Feb 8, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,165,1950 ed.</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEVY</b> Next, the forest trees up to approximately 30 in. in diameter were felled by a system of driving, the trees being notched on the upper and lower side of the drive, which commenced at the top of the slope and proceeded downwards. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,241,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> The bushmen developed an expert knowledge of driving, or directing one tree against another, or a body of trees, which swept a great weight in one irresistible drive of crashing and thunderous timber. <i>(1952,Faring South,179</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> [also driving-out]

>  Driving : The term used to describe the process of tunnelling. <i>(Sep 1999,Ohinemuri Regional History Journal,43 : 14</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> Immediately north of the [Waikaka] township a rich layer in the fault-involved older quartz drifts, ... was worked from the surface ... This layer is locally known as the `back lead'. On or near it a shaft was sunk ... but was not utilised for driving-out. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,4</i>) [NOTES: 1914 P.G. Morgan report Geological Survey Branch 147 in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 4]

>  <b>McARA</b> The term driving generally referred to tunnelling in any direction but in some circumstances, where reefs were present, it meant tunnelling along the line of the reef or parallel to it. The term drifting was used more specifically for driving on a reef, while the term crosscutting referred totunnelling across the reef up to an angle of say forty-five degrees; however it was common to talk of `driving a crosscut'. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi,184</i>)

### driving creek
 
 <i>n.</i> a creek used for driving kauri logs [(Northland (kauri) bushman's)]

>  Stripped of their branches they could be floated down the driving creeks with the ordinary logs, and if `broken down' or cut into suitable boards for wainscoting and furniture, would be highly appreciated for their striking beauty. <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/186</i>) [NOTES: (Firth)]

>  My son ... took a leading spur on the right bank of what had been a driving-creek for kauri logs ... <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/38</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> In a steep driving creek, a branch of the Kapowai, the sub-contractor had built a dam in very much of a hurry. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,215</i>)

### driving dam
 
 <i>n.</i> a dam built to assist driving logs by sudden release of water; a trip-dam.

>  When the natural supply of water is not sufficient to float these [logs] they are forced along by driving-dams or hauled out of the bush, either by bullocks or steam-engines. <i>(1904,TrNZI,36/464</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We waded downstream, passing the confluences with Pukearuhe Stream and Stoney Creek, which, with Spragg Stream, probably had driving dams in the old days. <i>(Nov 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 60/50</i>)

>  Inventive methods of transportation were devised to get logs to the mills. One of the most ingenious involved the use of timber dams, better known as driving dams... When enough water had built up, the dam would be tripped and the logs carried down the stream to the sawmill on the resulting flood. <i>(Sep 1991,Historic Places,27</i>)

### driving dog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a heading dog.

>  A driving dog is a heading dog, and therefore he should always look after the lead of the sheep, the tail usually looks after itself. <i>(May 1933,NZ Farmer,317</i>)

### driving while black
 
 <i>phr.</i> African New Zealanders' sense of being picked on by the police

>  Wellington's African youth experience frequent discrimination - including being picked on by police for "driving while black", a study has found. ... Some reported being frequently pulled over by police for no particular reason, dubbed the "DWB" or "driving while black" phenomenon, the students found. <i>(Oct 8, 2005,Dominion Post,A10</i>)

### driving-out
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McKAY</b> Gold was got at the furthest depthreached, but not enough to pay forworking, under circumstances that wouldhave compelled the "driving-out" of thewash. <i>(1895,Report on Geol. of SW Nelson,101</i>)

### droid queen
 
 <i>n.</i> a steroid-using female athlete

>  Brett Naylor.. used to coach Olympic swimmer Anna Simcic. He remembers her commnents about certain athletes: "'Ahh, that's a droid queen', she used to say." <i>(Jul 8, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

### drong
 
 <i>n.</i> drongo [1983 Oct 14 p.c. Assoc.-Prof. C. Corne (Auckland) Drong - v. drongo.]

### drongo
 
 <i>adj.</i> daft, stupid. [ca1961 June Victoria University first-year English Student's Essay She's drongo, eh? [=`She's daft']]



 
 <i>n.</i> a dumbcluck, oaf; stupid or clumsy fool; ?clown. [(WW2 and later) ca1944-5 first heard by HWO (perhaps introduced by returning servicemen) I have a feeling it was around long before that, probably introduced by the Oz radio serial "Dad & Dave" [DEH]]

>  That Herman, the EEC-approved drongo, is on about three times as much... <i>(Oct 24, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  "We are full this year, we don't even have a place for you in the drongo class"... "So my father went to Avondale College where they took on lots of "drongos" and they were very friendly to this one. <i>(1997,John Banks : A Biography,36</i>)

>  <b>Gee</b> He smiled at Mother when he came back. 'Dad's a bit of a drongo,' he said. <i>(2005,Blindsight,50</i>)

>  And why don't we say to young Maori girls, 'Listen, don't go with any drongo.' <i>(Mar 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  Some drongo's beer spills on your tweeds/ As you're drinking, as you're drinking in the pubs./ <i>(1951,Moa on Lambton Quay,21</i>) [NOTES: (James Ryan)]

>  ... bookmakers, moneylenders, parasites, newspaper proprietors, spielers, chisellers, drones, drongoes and spivs. <i>(Apr 1951,Here & Now,9</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the [slang] expressions listed by children in two schools are: `Up the shoot, going eyes out, bullswool, go jump in the lake, ... shivery dick, shiver me timbers, ... you're telling I, got it on the brain, like smoke in a wheelbarrow' (Kaitaia), `Sheila, crow, bud, frig it, grub, holy moses, blast it, mole, balls to you, shut your face, ... nerts to you, go bite your back, have a roll, fluff off, scram, ants in your pants, rag bag, ... you're bats, dronk, drongo, dill, dip, dippy, ya big lug, greetings gruesome, who dented your beak. (South Wellington). <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/677</i>)

>  His eyes are six pick heads apart: He is an idiot, a clot, a nitwit, a drongo. <i>(Dec 28, 1954,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: F.M. O'Brien Invercargill]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He took it all like the good sport he turned out to be. Not like this drongo. Him and his damned blankets. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,96</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> `You may think I'm a bit of a drongo,' said Ivan surprisingly. `Letting that big Pommy bastard horn in on me and Mollie. ... If I turfed that Pongo bastard out of the house she'd go with him.' <i>(1985,Horse,35</i>) [NOTES: c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Name and the Game) 35]

>  <b>CASEY</b> If you gave that drongo fifty bucks he'd still think you were holding out on him. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,251</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> A weak personality; a fool.`It's no use sending that drongo - he doesn't listen to what you tell him and half an hour after he wants to know what he ought to be doing.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],291</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> [They] were out in the alley, leaning on each other ... and vowing eternal friendship in the face of all drongos, no-hopers, wowsers and bush carpenters and all those poufs who had promised to the big game and then backed out ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,171</i>)

>  drongo ... n. bird; the spangled drongo; drongo (Sl.) R.A.A.F. recruit;n. and adj. (Sl.) useless,incompetent person. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... even though a lot of the menfolk in town considered him a bit of a drongo ... he was really quite a gay spark among the ladies. <i>(1973,NZ Jack,84</i>)

>  <b>CLEARY</b> ... clip all this long hair off and make skinheads of these drongos, ... <i>(1975,Pocketful of Years,23</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> `Drongos,' Max hisses. `Wart-heads. Dingbats. Halfwits.' <i>(1976,Send Somebody Nice,32</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> A DRIP. This refers to people, not plumbing. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,35 ed.</i>)

>  drongo: idiot <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  drongo: stupid person ... : a despised person <i>(1981,Auckland Secondary Teachers College</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  drongo ... 2. Austral. sl. a slow-witted person. [C19: < Malagasy] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,341</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 230; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 148]]

>  `I feel a right drongo.' `Whadarja? That's what it's for.' <i>(1983,Landfall,278</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> You're sure we wouldn't be just two lousy drongoes and get in everyone's way?' <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,43</i>)

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> The words flew, and enriched Celia's [an English visitor's] vocabulary. `Duck shoving' was the local name for buck-passing between government departments. A `Queen Street farmer' was a city slicker who owned land or stock for tax-fiddle reasons. `Skite' and `drongo' were terms ofabuse. A `cow-cockie' was a dairy farmer and inferior for some reason to a `runholder' concerned only with sheep. A `piker' was a faint-hearted person who gave up the fight too soon, ... <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,26</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> Then there's those two drongoes. ... What's the bloody matter with them? Me I'd be into the flax bushes like a shot, ... <i>(1986,Gemina,23</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> drongo slow-witted person; probably early 1920s Australian recehorse of that name which never won, the horse most likely getting its name for the bird; eg `Gabe's a complete drongo when it comes to driving in rush-hour traffic, though otherwise he's competent enough.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,39</i>)

>  Suppose a friend says to me, `You know that drongo who was on the chain with me at the works last summer? Just a little joker he was, remember? A real serious ankle-biter. Hailed from somewhere down the Wire Wrapper. A right useless bastard. Couldn't see the road to the dunny if it had red flags on it. Well, it seems that he's shacked up with some pluty Remmers sheila and she's about to drop her bundle.' ... A non-New Zealander will probably require a translation, ... My friend is communicating to me the information that a slow-witted, incompetent person of diminutive stature, who was reared in the Wairarapa district and workedfor a time in the killing gang of an abattoir, is now cohabiting with a wealthy woman from the affluent Auckland suburb of Remuera. This woman is soon to give birth. <i>(1989,Pacific Way,10</i>) [NOTES: (Sharp New Zildish)]

### drongoistic
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  So your average <i>Terminator 2</i>-loving, popcorn-munching escapist can happily hunker down in the dark to watch <i>Black Hawk Down</i>.. or any amount of drongoistic nonsense with the blessing of the censors, but this little number might actually ... what? Upset us? <i>(May 25, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

### dronk
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the [slang] expressions listed by children in two schools are: `Up the shoot, going eyes out, bullswool, go jump in the lake, ... shivery dick, shiver me timbers, ... you're bats, dronk, drongo, dill, dip, dippy. Wellington). <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/677</i>)

### drooping
 
 <i>n.</i> as a distinguishing epithet in names of plants, see club-rush; fern, bristle, filmy; grass, fescue; pine, spleenwort.

### drop
 
 <i>v.</i> Transf. f. an orig. reference to farm animals, esp. sheep, giving birth: cf. OED 14. [in various colloquial uses.]

>  First lamb dropped among Dr Williams' Sheep. <i>(Feb 25, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>WODZICKI</b> Goats ...in Hawkes Bay drop their kids between May and August. 1950 <i> Ibid.</i>. 217 [Sambur deer] calves are dropped in November and December. <i>(1950,Introduced Mammals of NZ,168</i>)

>  The nannies should start dropping about 10th October. <i>(Sep 1986,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 1986 Sept Farming Programme, National Radio]



 
 <i>v.</i> In male informal use, to give birth to a baby (see also to drop one's bundle) (See [also drop one's bundle](../A#also-drop-one's-bundle))

>  <b>HARVEY</b> Well, we started talking about this and that and ended up on Nuts's sister Violet, the one who was having this baby. `When's she goin' to drop it?' Jim Dickson asked Nuts. `Drop what?' `The kid.' Snigger, snigger. `She's not goin' to drop it,' said Nuts angrily. `It's goin' to be born.' `Same thing,' answered this know-all. `What do you know about babies, mate?' asked Nuts. <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,71</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to cut hay.

>  <b>NELSON</b> to drop - to cut hay, as in "Bob's away dropping." <i>(1999,Eugene Nelson Glossary,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (poison drop)

>  <b>BURDON</b> What bears this out is the result of follow-up trapping by the four guys who have worked every major ridge and spur in the drop zone for about 12 months following the drop. <i>(1996,Hunting for DOC,53</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  I think he's dropped a lot in quartz refining, and I don't think his estate will yield very much. <i>(Dec 23, 1890,Otago Witness,17</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to cut down, fell

>  <b>FRANCES</b> `We're dropping the trees on Marsden's place - which we should have started this morning. ...' <i>(1964,Johnny Rapana,27</i>)

>  fromDROP as to drop a tree - to fell a tree - was missed by Turner but is good Aust as well as NZ colloquialism.Similarly too, KNOCK a crop down is to cut it.`THROW DOWN' is still a good colloquialism for reaping a crop. See the last line of Donne, Hymn to God My God ... <i>(Jul 1966,Letter</i>) [NOTES: J. Davey (Whangarei)]

>  [Footrot Flats] PUSH OFF YOUNG PEW! IT'LL TAKE A LOT MORE THAN ONE SCRAWNY LITTLE MAGPIE TO STOP ME DROPPING THIS TREE MATE. <i>(Mar 5, 1981,Evening Post,28</i>)

>  These scrubcutters are supposed to be cuttin' the barbery [sic] but they're droppin' the kanuka as well. <i>(Nov 7, 1983,Evening Post,36</i>) [NOTES: (Footrot Flats strip)]



 
 <i>n.</i> the number of farm animals (esp. lambs) born on a farm in a season. [(farming)]

>  Lamb losses were staggering. One farmer had lost 1850 lambs out of 2100- 90 percent of his drop. <i>(Sep 20, 1977,Dominion,1/9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a delivery of sly-grog liquor.

>  <b>McCLENAGHAN</b> I'll take care of the bill until you're able to yourselves, which should be when you've made your first two drops. <i>(1976,Travelling Man,21</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a brew or selection of liquor

>  When Montana boss Peter Hubscher invited me, earlier this month, to a tasting of the company's flagship red, he added: "It's not a bad drop." <i>(Jun 15, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,5</i>)

>  <b>AGNEW</b> They've got a good drop at the Park Hotel. I can recommend it.' <i>(1974,Loner, The,180</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the number of lambs produced on a property.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Every few days, depending on the drop of lambs and the paddocks available, the unlambed ewes are moved on. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,49</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> The gen. Eng. sense `to fell to ground by a blow or shot' (OED 1726) is often used as I'll drop you as a threatening catchphrase or exclamation of annoyance.

>  <b>COLIN GORDON</b> A sharp comment of annoyance is I'll drop you!; and the reply is You couldn't drop a fart!: or I'll hammer you down flatter'n a tin tack! <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c.Colin Gordon]

>  <b>McGILL</b> drop to hit somebody with the intention of flattening them; eg `One more crack out of you and I'll drop ya.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,39</i>)

### drop across
 
 <i>v.</i> to run into; to meet unexpectedly and fortuitously; `come across'. [1950 (frequent) I dropped across him yesterday in the street]

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> I dropped across a job. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,30</i>) [NOTES: (`Swagger')]

### drop box
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The name of the operation, Tri, was an obscure play on words dreamed up by a rugby-loving detective boss, linked to the handy "drop boxes" in banks that were exploited during the scam... As "tri" means three, a "drop-kick" is worth three points... But the case became better known as the "drop-box scam". <i>(Jul 22, 1998,Dominion,9</i>)

### drop in for
 
 <i>v.</i> to come in for; be in for (punishment). See also drop a person in it [(obs.)]

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> If they were found out they would drop in for it. <i>(1895,Diary of Jonathan Roberts,33</i>)

### drop kick
 
 <i>n.</i> an obnoxious person [ORIGIN: rhyming slang, in Macquarie Dict. - may be more general?]

>  <b>Fiu</b> 'Why don't you leave him alone, you drop kick!' I yelled back. <i>(2006,Purple Heart,87</i>)

### drop off
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [ewes or cows] to abandon or neglect newborn offspring.

>  .. lamb survival rate is higher because they have more milk and (in the case of multiple births) are not dropping off lambs. <i>(Jul 2001,Lifestyle Farmer,23</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to come down from a peak or top. [(musterers') ca1952 Apr 21 Peter Newton (Letter) drop off ... opposite of pulling onto a top `climbing a peak.']

>  <b>STRONACH</b> We left one man for the bottom beat. Then Mac dropped off; then I did. Two men [53] remained for the top, and one for the other side. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,52</i>)

### drop on
 
 <i>v.</i> to find unexpectedly

>  ... I went in the other direction and dropped on the [strayed] horses about 3 miles from home so I drove them into the paddoc and mended the fence, ... <i>(Oct 28, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,64,1950 ed.</i>)

>  The most inexperienced man ... has ... the guarantee of making on the average more than `tucker', with every prospect of `dropping' upon one of those rich patches which are the object of every miner's ambition. <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources,25</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> And each steamer will bring up a cargo/ Of Victorian diggers, no flies;/ They're the boys that can drop on the metal,/ .../ They'll get all the gold from the river,/ ... <i>(1864,Songs of the War,6</i>)

### drop on the metal
 
 <i>phr.</i> to find gold

>  <b>THATCHER</b> And each steamer will bring up a cargo/ Of Victorian diggers, no flies;/ They're the boys that can drop on the metal,/ .../ They'll get all the gold from the river,/ ... <i>(1864,Songs of the War,6</i>)

### drop one's bundle
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> to give birth

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> If he doesn't hurry she'll be dropping her bundle at our place. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,25,1976 ed.</i>)

>  Suppose a friend says to me, `You know that drongo who was on the chain with me at the works last summer? Just a little joker he was, remember? A real serious ankle-biter. Hailed from somewhere down the Wire Wrapper. A right useless bastard. Couldn't see the road to the dunny if it had red flags on it. Well, it seems that he's shacked up with some pluty Remmers sheila and she's about to drop her bundle.' ... A non-New Zealander will probably require a translation, ... My friend is communicating to me the information that a slow-witted, incompetent person of diminutive stature, who was reared in the Wairarapa district and worked for a time in the killing gang of an abattoir, is now cohabiting with a wealthy woman from the affluent Auckland suburb of Remuera. This woman is soon to give birth. <i>(Jan 1989,Pacific Way,10</i>) [NOTES: (Sharp New Zildish)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>phr.</i> lose one's cool, panic

>  The fullback "dropped his bundle" completely. <i>(Sep 10, 1947,NZ Observer,27</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> 'Feefi's gonna drop his bundle, Dad, I can feel it.' <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,42</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> He absolutely dropped his bundle, and, to make matters worse, I had started off with a duck's breakfast, but I saw a cookhouse and decided to give it a pop for a binder. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> I found that once I left the security of my saddle and my reliable Jimmy, my confidence immediately disappeared. However, I could not drop my bundle, so into the jungle I went [after the ounded beast]. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,115,1949 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> From thence on Mabel [after losing the tennis set] began to drop her bundle, and as the crowd frantically applauded[,] the popular Nancy ... looked the part of a veritable `Venus'. Jim fought valiantly, served magnificently, ... but still Mabel wilted. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,173</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To stop trying after experiencing a difficulty. .;`Come on! Cheer up! Don't drop your bundle. Look at it this way, it could ha' been a worse, now, couldn't it? Anyway, I've got some good news for you ...' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],296</i>)

>  <i>bundle ... 7. drop one's bundle. Austral. sl.</i> to panic or give up hope. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,144</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 94]]

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> drop his bundle become agitated <i>(1984,Blackball 08,243</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>drop one's bundle </i> to despair or give birth; eg `Sam's dropped his bundle ever since that triple bogey on the 18th when he was four clear.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,40</i>)

### drop sheet
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a tarpaulin or large sheet.

>  <b>KERR</b> In case you think this is nothing, they had to shear them on a drop sheet near the marking yards with blades. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,90</i>)

### drop someone in it
 
 <i>phr.</i> to land someone in a difficult situation

>  <b>McGILL</b> drop in it put someone in trouble; eg `You really dropped me in it with the wife telling her I was the one wanting another round.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,40</i>)

### drop the block
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [early hay-baling] to lever a slicer to cut hay into bales.

>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> Then someone had to drop the block that separated the bales which was a dangerous operation as it often sailed twenty feet into the air. <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,148</i>)

### drop the handkerchief
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  `Do you play with the girls at all?' `Sometimes we play Drop the Handkerchief. The girls can join in. But most of the time they play Eely Ily Oh, or Queenie, or some cissy game like that...'[sic] <i>(1978,Elver, The,61</i>)

### drop up and see you
 
 <i>phr.</i> [According to Laurie, it's "drop down & see you" in the UK]

### dropper
 
 <i>n.</i> a light, vertical fence post between the main fixed posts, attached to the wires only to keep them firm and evenlyspaced.Contrast -->standard, -->waratah.

>  In the erection of a wire fence it isusual to put the posts in the ground at acomfortable distance apart, droppers beingused as intermediate attachments tothe wire to keep them from opening orcontracting so that animals are preventedfrom forcing their way between the wire. <i>(May 29, 1912,Otago Witness,33</i>)

>  She grabbed something between a dropper and a post and I a Koradi. <i>(Feb 26, 1927,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,39</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> When all the wires are strained tightly the battens or droppers, which are split to about two inches square, are stapled at spaces varying from two to three feet. <i>(1965,Open Country,55</i>)

>  <b>Meredith</b> I ought to have mentioned that the first thing to do is to put in the `straining-posts,' and then run a wire ... to act as a line to erect posts by. ... Between the posts the wires are stapled to `droppers,' consisting of about three by one battens. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,68,1935 ed.</i>)

>  All hands in the carpenters shop. Made a mess of cutting dropper slips. ... 14th. ... I made droppers all day. <i>(Jul 13, 1904,Chudleigh Diary,423,1950 ed.</i>)

>  DROPPER. Abbreviation of FENCE DROPPER; BATTEN <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The `droppers' were first slung into place [on the pack-horse]. <i>(1949,High Country Days,39</i>)

>  The new [Heinemann's] dictionary carries: ... mall, ... M.O.W. and o.n.o. ... dropper (for fencing) ... and garage sale. <i>(Mar 28, 1979,Otago Daily Times,21</i>) [NOTES: (G. Griffiths)]

>  Dropper: Same as batten ... Used in parts of South Island and Australia. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,27</i>)

>  dropper ... NZ & Austral. batten in a fence. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,230</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 148]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> dropper fence batten. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,40</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> It was a viciously strained ten-wire, with close standards; and every other wire was a barb. Cosidy, who had prayed for slotted droppers, collapsed in the saddle. <i>(1904,Sons o' Men,93</i>) [NOTES: Check spelling of Cosidy]



 
 <i>n.</i> tubes which hang down and carry the milk, or pulsation tubes [(dairy-farming)]

>  Milking-machines: Droppers are the two small pipes to which the tubes with suction cups are attached. They hang or `drop' into the bail from the main pipeline. The operator is free to attend another bail while the cow is milking. <i>(1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: G.W. Turner]

>  Tubes which hang down and carry the milk, or pulsation tubes <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,40</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a batten on a flood-gate.

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> Afterwards it was quite simple to retie the sections back on the droppers. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,241</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the flexible worm which hangs down from above the shearer and carries the driving mechanism of the machine-shears. [??chk]

>  <b>BOWEN</b> ... when the comb digs in it makes the sheep kick and the dropper moves around and loses its true swing. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Cf pikau, pocket @n. [(station, high-country farming)]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Potatoes and vegetables were in sacks known as `droppers' which would hang from the hooks on each side of the pack saddles. <i>(1949,High Country Days,38</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> One who delivers sly-grog supplies in a dry district, either by lorry or from a train; a sly-grog wholesaler [(obs.) ca1920 at least: probably earlier, P.S. Arden]

>  <b>EDMOND</b> A dropper is a person who sells sly grog. They were called droppers up there [in Ohakune, a dry district]. <i>(1992,Autobiography of My Father,69</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> She does catering, he said. And she sells it. You know what I mean, Dave - sly grog. One time when the police raided her she was doing the washing, Johnny said. And they reckoned they'd got her this time, because they'd found out a dropper had been through the town just a few nights before on his lorry. So they reckoned she'd be well stocked up. <i>Ibid.</i> 255 [The police] reckon they'd got her [slygrogging] this time, because they'd found out a dropper had been through the town just a few nights before on his lorry. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,247,(1974 ed Rhodes) ed.</i>)

>  A gang of the boys often beat up the local `dropper' in Raurimu, who can't say anything or else he'll get had up for `dropping.' The police usually allow only one `dropper' in the town and clamp down on the others. <i>(1952,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: (? informant: King Country)]

>  <b>McCLENAGHAN</b> ... They could bring in their own liquor ... and there was no reason [20] why they should when they could rely on the droppers. <i>(1976,Travelling Man,19</i>)

>  <b>EDMOND</b> ... there was [ca1950s] a chap called Paddy the Pig, a dropper ... he told him he had to pay [pounds] 5 for the last inch in a bottle of whiskey. <i>(1984,High Country Weather,22</i>)

>  The man who enters a bording(?) or `temperance' house is immediately accused of having rolled a beer barrel through the maze of the Licensing Act. He is suspected of designs of all descriptions. He is dubbed `a dropper', or `blind tiger.' <i>(Oct 26, 1912,NZ Truth,383/6/5</i>)

>  We want [pounds] 2 a week extra for the `looker-out' and the `dropper.' <i>(Apr 5, 1951,Dominion</i>) [NOTES: [Arbitration Court evidence] [Hotelworkers]]

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> ... have a swig for Christmas ... A man isn't a big time dropper for nothing. <i>(1974,Predicament,61,1981 ed.</i>)

>  A person who sells or hands over sly-grog, or afterhours grog: there was an article in Christchurch ?Star-Sun ?ca1946-7 `Spotters and Droppers' <i>(1972,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1972 p.c. Jack Columbus (Blenheim)]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a tube carrying a driveshaft that powers a shearing handpiece.

>  <b>BOWEN</b> A shearer will shear less sheep in a day if he is cutting them, as when the comb digs in it makes the sheep kick and the dropper moves around. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,4</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> I now have a collection of antiques .. old handpieces, combs, cutters, droppers. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,41</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a part of a milking system.

>  The droppers are very free-swinging and follow whatever position the milker moves into with the cups ... young cows can crowd up along the line and get out of position with the dropper. <i>(Dec 31, 1964,NZ Farmer,10</i>)

>  Each claw has its own pulsation system so that it's no longer necessary to have a separate air line and separate droppers to each cluster. <i>(Nov 1975,NZ Farmer,13</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Droppers: Tubes which hang down and cary the milk or pulsation tubes. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,40</i>)

>  Your complete conversion pack includes rubber bends, new milk and air tubes and droppers. <i>(Mar 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,36</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large bag, billy or pot on a packsaddle.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> dropper: open-mouthed pannier for loose goods such as carrots, potatoes etc which hangs on the side of a pack saddle. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,27</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mainly S. I.] a fence batten.

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Between the posts the wires are stapled to 'droppers' consisting of about three by one battens. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,68</i>) [NOTES: (1935)]

>  After sheep dipping, the dip is used for soaking the droppers in. These droppers, or battens, to which the wires are nailed between the strainer ... <i>(Aug 1900,NZ Farmer,331</i>)

>  The cheapest and most expensive dropper in the market. A perfect stay and post saver. <i>(Mar 9, 1907,Farmers' Union Advocate,15</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> .. no stapling is used, and each wire runs freely through all posts and droppers without risk of kinking. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,428</i>)

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> Battens, or droppers, are also expensive components of the traditional fence ... <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,192</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> Once the wires were strained and stapled it had the distinction of being the only properly constructed wire fence in the valley with rimu droppers perfectly spaced. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,42</i>)

>  <b>FISK</b> Ask a Northlander to throw you a dropper and you are likely to get a fist in your mush, while in the South Island a batten is what you do down to the hatches when the nor'wester starts blowing. <i>(1996,Practical Smallfarming,7</i>)

>  So I planted a dropper, a 2" x 2" of Black pine about three feet long under the dropping bench. <i>(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,263</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a hotel worker who handed out illegal after-hour take-away liquor; a sly-grog scout. [(to ?1968)]

>  It is possible to get as much liquor as one wants in Masterton, and while much of it (privately and legitimately brought in) is of good quality, much more (surreptitiously dispensed at sly-groggeries and by what are locally termed `droppers' at enhanced prices) is atrocious and harmful liquor. ...A Masterton gentleman ... told our representative that the knew of twenty sly-groggeries in Masterton, and double that number of `droppers.' It can be imagined what kind of poison they dispense. <i>(Oct 28, 1911,NZ Truth,331/4/6</i>)

### dropper-holder
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a battener or implement that holds a batten/dropper upright and steady for a fencer to attach it to wires.

>  The save-a-man dropper-holder means 1 man, unaided, fixes 60 droppers an hour. <i>(Jan 18, 1951,NZ Farmer,26</i>)

### droppie
 
 <i>n.</i> shortened form of "dropkick" or "dropped goal" [(rugby union)]

>  Those who wore the number eight jersey before him for so long used to say as much, and ended up scoring droppies that won tests. <i>(Sep 2000,North & South,33</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Down slams the scrum, Wales hook, and Cleaver has a droppy that spins just wide of the post. <i>(1970,On the Ball,138</i>)

>  Droppie undoes Hutt [headline] A 40 metre dropped goal by Porirua City centre DonBaker enabled his side to score a grand 11-10 last minute rugby league victory over Upper Hutt... <i>(Apr 6, 1981,Dominion,20</i>)

>  <b>HOWITT</b> `I'll show him' I said. I scored a try, kicked a droppie and played a blinder. <i>(1975,NZ Rugby Greats,17</i>)

### dropping
 
 <i>n.</i> the action of a dropper n.2 [(obs.)]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> When I first visited the farm he had told me stories about King Country drinking, that was to say, `sly-grogging' and `dropping'; ... <i>(1973,Once Is Enough,81</i>)

>  <b>McCLENAGHAN</b> ... `You think we could a little dropping?' he said. ... The sale of liquor was prohibited in the King Country ... that didn't mean people couldn't drink. <i>(1976,Travelling Man,19</i>)

### dropping-board
 
 <i>n.</i> in a fowl-house, the board under the perches which catches the fowl droppings.

>  <b>HARVEY</b> And when it rained [3] his slippers got all caked up - just like the chooks' feet when hehadn't scraped down the dropping-board for a time. <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,2</i>)

### droppo
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There is Spit Wilson, a 16-year-old streetwise "droppo" who becomes a courier for the mysterious Mr Blyss. <i>(Sep 26, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

### dropsies
 
 <i>n.</i> tendency to drop a ball

>  Umaga suffered the most co-ordinating with new five-eighth combinations, got a case of the dropsies in the test against England and would have found more space playing on a postage stamp. <i>(Jul 13, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B3</i>)

>  Black Caps catch dose of dropsies <i>(Oct 25, 2005,Dominion Post,C14</i>)

>  [Hdg] Bad case of the dropsies has Fleming stumped. New Zealand captain Stephen Fleming is baffled by their case of the dropsies against Pakistan at Jade Stadium on Saturday. <i>(Jan 12, 2004,Dominion Post,D10</i>)

### drought master
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  These "drought masters" are bred in a harsh environment at Otamauri on the Napier-Taihape Rd. <i>(Jun 20, 2003,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

### droughtlander
 
 <i>n.</i> an Australian [obs.]

>  At the dinner to the English cricketers in Melbourne a jovial `drought-lander' with some knowledge of English and fond of music sat beside a dashing batsman of the visiting team. <i>(Dec 7, 1907,NZ Truth,129/3/3</i>)

### drove
 
 <i>n.</i> The word is in use in NZ, but earlier drive (q.v.) would poss. be preferred.] [?More cits or omit or have one entry at verb.???]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> ... this noteworthy drove. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,52</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Pa. tense droved; =drive (travelling stock).

>  Counsel: `You drive for a living?' Witness: `No, I'm a drover.' Counsel: `Well, you drive sheep for a living?' Witness: `No; I drove.' Counsel: `Do you mean you used to drive?' Witness:`No, I drove now.' Counsel: `H'mph! What does your brother do?' Witness: `He's got a timber contract.' Counsel: `Oh, he's a bush faller.' Witness: `He's a bush feller.' <i>(Apr 23, 1910,NZ Truth,252/1/6</i>)

>  drove v.t. to drive animals, as a mob of cattle, etc.; - v.i. to act as a driver of animals. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  see cit. drover. <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>MUIE</b> There must have been great loss and expense in droving the stock overland. <i>(1960,Word for Word,38</i>)

>  <b>RENNIE</b> During the 1700s ... when great mobs were droved between Scotland and England the specifically Scottish Collie is believed to have come down into England. But the name Borderwas never heard of until after 1900 when it came to denote an area [Scottish or English] in which a series of sheepdog trials were held ... <i>(1985,Working Dogs,7</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to drive farm stock on hoof.

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> 'The way to drove cattle is,' he said afterwards, 'a step and a bite ...' <i>(1949,Early NZ Families,16</i>)

>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> Margaret met her husband while droving sheep. <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,156</i>)

>  <b>DOMINY</b> Then men were still droving thousands of sheep off the hillsides ... <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,176</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the driving of sheep or cattle on foot or hoof, or the stock being driven on foot or hoof.

>  <b>C W RICHMOND</b> You can tell a drove of sheep a long way off by the cloud of dust which looks, in the valley exactly like smoke. <i>(Mar 28, 1867,Letter,238</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers II]

>  A short time ago, a drove of full-mouthed ewes from Napier, in rather poor condition, were sold upon arrival at 7s to 9s each. <i>(Aug 1888,NZ Farmer,291</i>)

>  <b>SILK</b> The Puhoi settlers soon became accustomed to see large droves driven through the settlement; with the result that on the 29th September, 1877, the first cattle sale was held at Puhoi. <i>(1923,History of Puhoi,84</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> Year after year huge droves of sheep used to pass Tauroa for shearing at Waikuku. <i>(1958,Sturdy Sons,171</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> They were not the most civilised cattle on the road .. so the drove was not without incident. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,45</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> Instead of those early droves to Cromwell, taking up to ten days, we can now have a truck and trailer drive into the yards ... <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,102</i>)

>  .. there are still people who like to buy in autumn and winter, graze their purchases on the highways and by-ways and have them home as spring approaches. Two such droves are in progress. <i>(Jul 13, 1995,NZ Farmer,21</i>)

>  <b>PULLEN</b> The night paddock was at the bottom end of Arohana Station which isn't too big a drove, but by early afternoon it was raining. <i>(1996,On the Road Again,37</i>)

### drove boss
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a head drover in a large mob, usually droving long-distance.

>  <b>GORDON</b> Not far into the mob a traveller would come across the first of three drovers. Usually he would be sitting on a horse at a vantage point quietly watching was happening ahead. Generally this would be the 'drove boss.' <i>(1990,Matawhero, Elsewhere and Other Things,32</i>)

### drove-track
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a path by which stock are driven.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> .. sometimes an old drove-track can be picked up by its tall manuka at a distance of miles ... <i>(1921,Tutira,183</i>)

### drover
 
 <i>n.</i> one who drives sheep or cattle for a living. cf stock-driver, stockman.

>  Two routes were successfully burnt through dense vegetation by determined stock drovers, both converging on the Acheron Valley. <i>(Dec 22, 2003,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  "The Bald Eagle" - Haast cattle drover and horseman Kevin Nolan, 82 when he died, was one of the last links to pioneer days in the South Westland region. <i>(May 8, 2003,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  Yes, we're all going to hell in a drover's hat, thanks to environmental exploitation, blind patriotism and.. the media. <i>(May 1, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

>  ...Haast cattle drover and horseman Kevin Nolan, 87 when he died, was one of the last links to pioneer days in the South Westland region. <i>(May 8, 2003,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  At Oeo a party of drovers were refreshing themselves, their dogs and their stockwhips. <i>(Jan 1, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  She knew the drover with the blue eyes only as Jim. <i>(Nov 30, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,96</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Cattle have been driven from Oihoa (or Go-ashore), ... to Akaroa in about eight hours; ... my informant was one of the drovers. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,343</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Nov Capt. Smith's report in 1848 Wakefield Handbook for NZ 343]

>  ... the hills at [the run's] south-west extremity present no serious obstacle to the drover ... <i>(Mar 8, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  <b>E.J. WAKEFIELD</b> Their drovers were forced to abandon them in a labyrinth of mountains. <i>(1851,Letter,12</i>) [NOTES: 1851 E.J. Wakefield Letter to Grey 12]

>  <b>FITTON</b> The vulgar delusion that, in order to be a colonist in earnest, it is expedient to dress like a drover, ... is now quite out of fashion. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),278</i>)

>  At Lyttelton. ... Took the sheep over the hill myself with 2 drovers <i>(Mar 2, 1904,Chudleigh Diary,420,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Drover: One who drives stock for a living. In Canterbury they were usually spoken of as sheep- or cattle-drivers until about 1900. There is a newly formed verb, to drove, trans and intrans, coming into use. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]; stock-driver, C.R., p.104) <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The drovers camped that night and the next day arrived at Basstina's station. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,112</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> In my boyhood [1880s] we called a man driving sheep a drover, and a man driving cattle a stock-driver or stock-rider or stockman. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,12</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> DROVERS: Men who drive sheep or cattle on the road. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,110</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> After camping the night there, we had to cross Kerr's Hill which was covered with tutu, a poisonous plant for sheep, and the curse of drovers. ... Odd sheep started to get `tooted' and instead of pushing on the men started bleeding the sheep. This was done by cutting a veinunder the eye. It was supposed to help the sheep, although I never thought it did any good. <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,19</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> Starting in the extreme north these drover buyers would buy any cattle they found for sale, mostly store steers ... <i>(1970,Way Up North,55</i>)

### drover's dog
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  You could argue that most MPs are only elected because they stand for a viable party: the old "a drover's dog would win Remuera" theory. <i>(May 1, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  Labour lost it [sc. the 1990 election] bigtime, and James B was simply the drover's dog. <i>(Apr 3, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

### droving
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the moving of sheep and other stock on hoof between properties or to railheads, saleyards or wharves.

>  <b>PLUMMER</b> Towards the end of 1931, after I had been at Puketiti for just on four years, I decided to leave and go droving. <i>(1994,From Drover to Director,43</i>)

>  Droving's no easy job on East Cape. Long distance droving has certainly changed. <i>(Jul 13, 1995,NZ Farmer,21</i>)

### droving party
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a group of drovers, dogs, horses and their drove.

>  <b>PASCOE</b> Droving parties used Browning's route to take sheep from Canterbury to Hokitika, an undertaking of more magnitude than could be realised until it was experienced. <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,122</i>)

### droving team
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) two or more drovers who work together when moving very large mobs of sheep or cattle.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Mr and Mrs Swinn claim to be the only man and wife droving team in the country ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,94</i>)

### droving trail
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Ancient trees continue to give shelter, and you can still trace the old droving trail up the adjacent river flat. <i>(May 1996,NZ Historic Places,58/12</i>)

### drown the miller
 
 <i>phr.</i> to add too much water to liquor, or too much milk or water in tea.

>  <b>STEWARD</b> ... in mixing my negus I've quite `drowned the miller' ... <i>(1867,Carmina Varia,10</i>)

### drowned sailors' purses
 
 <i>n.</i> a seaweed (cf. mermaids' beads)

>  <b>HYDE</b> ... the seaweed known as Drowned Sailors' Purses. <i>(1937,Wednesday's Children,57</i>)

### DRSL
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Dispute Resolution Services Ltd

>  Cases are heard by Dispute Resolution Services Ltd - nicknamed Drizzle (DRSL) - when claimants feel they have been unfairly treated. <i>(Nov 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A12</i>)

### drum
 
 <i>n.</i> a rolled swag n1. [(obs. station)]

>  Got underway, drum and all. <i>(Jan 20, 1898,Otago Witness,62</i>)

>  <b>ROSS</b> `... Time's up!' is called, the `drum' (the local for swag) hoisted on, and the final ascent begins. <i>(1889,Lakes of Central Otago,44</i>) [NOTES: [Mount Earnslaw Station]]

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> For the strange gipsy fancy came on me - I longed for the mountain and plain;/ My passport the drum of the whaler, and the old savage freedom again. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,24</i>) [NOTES: `The Hard Case'[Mount Earnslaw Station]]

>  <b>McKEE WRIGHT</b> I longed for the mountain and plain;/ My passport the drum of the whaler, and the old savage freedom again. <i>(1900,Wisps of Tussock,51</i>)

>  <b>PICARD</b> You may look down on a man who gives his swag, drum, knot, or parcel of bags a shove round the land ... <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,7</i>)

>  The `drummer,' starting from the man who carried a drum, became the man who carried a swag and (then or thereafter) the loaded man (as a commercial traveller loaded with samples). To `carry the load' is another synonym; and the essential point throughout the symbolry was evidently the load. <i>(Jul 7, 1911,Maoriland Worker,18/14/2</i>)

>  drum n. a bundle of clothes, etc., wrapped in a blanket; a swag. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  Drum. Swag, obviously from the shape. Hence drummer. <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> 2. And when passing Jimmy Holland's,/ I felt a wee bit queer;/ I thought I'd throw the old drum down/ And have a pint of beer./ ... <i>(1956,Pioneer Recollections,55</i>) [NOTES: 1947-1948 Beattie Pioneer Recollections (1956) 55]

>  drum n. (Coll.) swag;hump a drum, carry a swag. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a (bawdy) house.

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> drum house, lodgings. Also information <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,96</i>)

>  Rossini had been fined for keeping a `drum' in Auckland. <i>(Sep 8, 1906,NZ Truth,64/4/7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> usu. the drum, correct (esp. horseracing) information; the oil. Also give the drum. Also attrib.

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> [The race-course tout] says, `... wait till I get the drum.' I say, `What's this drum business?' He says: `It's the same as the oil, the griffin, and the low down.' <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,23</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To explain or describe. .in 4;`If you haven't operated a winch before, just ask Harry.He's an an old hand at it, he'll give you the drum. <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],408</i>) [NOTES: (1962-63)1974 Syder and Hodgetts Aust and NZ English [TS] 408]

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> drum house, lodgings. Also information <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,96</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Taylor gave me the usual drum on the rules of the block ... <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,61</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] Drum 1518 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to give (misleading) information to. [(obs.)]

>  The unsuspecting lad fell in and drummed everybody that the horse was ?oomey. <i>(Apr 18, 1908,NZ Truth,148/2/6</i>)

>  As to sheep, some of the mob have drummed me they'll bite if they're disturbed during nesting season, don't have it on your mind that I'm afraid of being bitten by a savage sheep when taking the housemaid out for a walk on Sunday afternoons. <i>(May 6, 1911,NZ Truth,306/4/6</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> of kakapos, to make their peculiar noise..

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> On one occasion the noise of the kakapos drumming on the northern side of the Sound was so loud that he had to put in at Cooper Island for the night. <i>(1968,Early Fiordland,126</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> steal from a dwelling [criminals' Cf drum n3.]

>  drum (v.) steal from a dwelling <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,96</i>)

### drum fish
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [?drummer 3](../?#?drummer-3))

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> All the coves of this bay teem with fish of various kinds, such as cuttle-fish, large breams, ... small grey breams, small and large barracootas, flying gurnards, horse-mackarel, dog-fish, soles, dabs, mullets, drums, scorpenas or rock-fish, cole-fish, the beautiful fish called chimera, and shags. [In the Stout Collection copy, Victoria University Library, an early 19th C. or late 18th C.hand has added: `*Baracootas, a fish remarkably smooth about 7 or 8 foot long [dagger] FlyingGurnards, a flying fish, of a remarkable fine Gold Colour. [double dagger] Chimeras, a fish of Silver Colour - [triple dagger] Drum fish, so call'd from the noise they make, - ' <i>(Jan 15, 1770,Journal,114</i>)

### drummer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  KYPHOSUS SYDNEYANUS Gunther. <i>Drummer</i>. ... Distribution: North Island coasts. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/16</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE DRUMMER <i>Kyphosus sydneyanus<</i> (Gunther) <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,101</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Drummer <i>Segutilum sydneyanum</i> (Gunther) (Syn. <i>Pachymetopon</i> and <i>Pimelepterus drewii</i> Hector) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,407</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 407]

>  <b>WILSON</b> DRUMMER Inhabitant of more northern, rocky waters down to about 100 feet (30.48 metres) the Drummer, or Silver Drummer as he is sometimes known, is characterised by his stout shape and small mouth and head. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,60</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> DRUMMER [<i>Kyphosus sydneyanus<</i>] ... Silver drummer is an alternative name. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,42</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> DRUMMER ... <i>Other names: Kyphosus sydneyanus</i>; silver drummer. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,240</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family KYPHOSIDAE. We have just one kyphosid, the oval drummer, <i>Kyphosus sydneyanus</i>..., a small-mouthed fish with small soft scales covering head, body and fins; it has an outer row of cutting teeth and fine teeth on the jaws and roof of the mouth (vomer). About thirty inches long, ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,221</i>)

>  <i>drummer ... NZ & Austral. </i>... NZ greyish-green oval fish. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,231</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] drummer 1505-14 DRUMMERS 1491-1523 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the slowest (often the learner) shearer in a gang [(station)]

>  The `drummer,' starting from the man who carried a drum, became the man who carried a swag and (then or thereafter) the loaded man (as a commercial traveller loaded with samples). To `carry the load' is another synonym; and the essential point throughout the symbolry was evidently the load. ... and a `drummer' is the man with the lowest tally - perhaps because he is likeliest to take and keep the track with his `load.' The general applications of these fresh significations may be doubted. <i>(Jul 7, 1911,Maoriland Worker,18/14/2</i>)

>  Drummer.-The slowest shearer in a shed. <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Hewett, the learner, and `' of the gang with seventeen to his credit had also shown an improvement. <i>(1949,High Country Days,55</i>)
