>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Drummer. 8. Slowest shearer in a shed: New Zealand shearers': since ca. 1890. Niall Alexander letter of Oct. 22, 1939. Perhaps ex sense 1. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1037</i>)

>  RINGER. The fastest shearer in the gang. Fast men are also known as `guns,' and the slowest man in the shed as the `drummer.' <i>(1949,High Country Days,197,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Drummer: The slowest shearer in a shed. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,366</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> DRUMMER: The shearer with the lowest aggregate tally of shorn sheep. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,110</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> DRUMMER. The slowest shearer on the board. He shears at the bottom of the board, the furthest away from the wool table. <i>(1956,Wool Away!,156</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> ... I was only the drummer (slowest shearer) of that particular gang, ... [1925 - North Canterbury] <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,255</i>)

>  Drummer: Slowest shearer on the board who shears at the bottom of the board furthest away from the wool table. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,54</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Fred, being the ganger, was on No. 1 stand. Mick was No. 2, Thornton No. 3, and I, being a learner, was on the traditional `drummer's' stand, at the far end of the board. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,73</i>)

>  drummer: The name given to the shearer with the lowest daily tally. He is not necessarily a bad or lazy shearer, but simply slower than the rest, and in a group of good shearers he could be quite competent, but not as good as the others. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/24</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a swagger [(rare in NZ)]

>  Drum. Swag, obviously from the shape. Hence drummer. <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> drummer swagman or tramp. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,40</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a swagger.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> drummer: a swagger. This obviously comes from the shape of his swag-roll. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,29</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a slowest, most novice shearer in a gang.

>  <b>MEEK</b> drummer - the shearer with the lowest aggregate tally of shorn sheep. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,110</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> Jack O'Rourke, the ringer, quietly and grimly ordered the drummer to leave the boy alone. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,122</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> drummer: the slowest shearer in the shed. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,29</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Right down the other end of the board, sweating and grunting is the "drummer", the youngest and slowest member of the gang. Still learning the game and strengthening his wrists, the drummer's not the sort of bloke that goes in for a 'catch.' <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,ii 1</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Despite the popularity of the faster machines blade shearing is still taught at special wool board shearing courses and in the last half of each year gangs are still hard at it in the Southern high country - chasing the ringer, ripping bootlaces, going for a catch and shoving an extra cobbler into the drummer's pen. <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BLUEFISH </i> (Blue drummer, korokoropounamu) <i>Girella cyanea </i>... As the name suggests their body colour is a uniform bright blue and all the fins are a similar colour. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,229,1984 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] drummer, oval 1505 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### drunk as a skunk/drunk as a wheelbarrow
 
 <i>phr.</i> very drunk

>  Drunk as a skunk, with his whiskers skewed,/ <i>(Mar 1953,Here & Now,14</i>) [NOTES: (A.Vogt)]

>  There's old timers we knew, and some who shot through A hard case, drag the chain, done a bunk, As busy as a blow fly, and bugalugs - from the boo-ai The rouseabout, as drunk as a skunk. <i>(Dec 2004,NZ Memories,p18</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Drunk as a skunk he was. b. drunk as a wheelbarrow. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,182</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Further up the creek beyond the site of the old still a pretty waterfall tumbles through the native bush. Adam's ale is the only tipple on tap there today. No one will get as drunk as a wheelbarrow on that. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,41</i>)

### drunk as chooks
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  "This lot were as drunk as chooks, " Mr Georgantis said. <i>(Nov 9, 1998,Dominion,3</i>)

>  <b>HUGHES</b> Pissed as proverbial chooks, they had got stuck into the top shelf early at their own party and now they roared into the car park shrieking and howling like weasels on speed. <i>(1999,Shooting from the Lip,214</i>)

### drunk's express
 
 <i>n.</i> a common term in NZ cities in days of 6 o'clock closing for a tram, bus or train which left the terminal shortly after the hotel-bars closed at 6.00 pm. [ca1920 p.c. Prof. P.S. Ardern Auckland: a 6 o'clock tram, then bus. ca1950 Wellington, the trains leaving soon after 6 p.m. for the Hutt or Paekakariki.] (See [shicker express](sa-sho.md#shicker-express))

### drunkie
 
 <i>n.</i> a drunk (person).

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> Near by [sic] a drunkie fell over and hit his head but got up painfully and wobbled on. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,68,1976 ed.</i>)

### dry
 
 <i>adj.</i> of a claim or workings, removed from water; occas., not liable to flooding or seepage. See also dry diggings.

>  The account of the Gully which he gives is, that fair prospects have been found there, and his opinion is that a party can make an ounce a man, as the sinking is very shallow, and the working dry. <i>(Sep 20, 1861,NZ Goldfields,13</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Sep 20 in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 13]



 
 <i>adj.</i> (with respect to alcohol) [Jim Cameron writes: NSOD gives the general sense, but the words (dry area) and several compounds have considerable historic significance in NZ from the 1890s almost up to the the present. DNZE does not record these - wet/dry area, district, vote; go wet, stay dry. NZPOD has no entry for wet in this sense but does for dry.]

>  [Caption] In the early days of its history the Corbans winery was located in a 'dry' electorate; the small white building in the background, situated across the railway lines in a 'wet' area was used for many years as the company's sale depot. <i>(Jun 1991,Cuisine,83</i>)

>  Mr Halford remembers when there was a post office, six churches, three hotels, three garages, two grocers, two tearooms, blacksmiths and wheelwrights, a bakery, bank, drapery, hardware, watchmaker and a town hall, where the ladies smuggled in liquor to the dances under their wide skirts in the days when the district was "dry". <i>(Jan 5, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,1</i>)

>  This is a "dry" town with no licensed hotels. <i>(Mar 3, 1938,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  John says they considered turning it into a restaurant because it is so far up the hill it is just outside the Tawa boundary - and therefore out of the "dry" liquor area. Tawa was a dry area until it went wet following the election. <i>(Jan 13, 2000,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  The hotel was closed when the district went "dry" in 1906, the bar reopening for business in 1998. <i>(Jun 2004,NZ Memories,No. 48/41</i>)

>  Paeroa is "dry". <i>(Sep 20, 1913,NZ Observer,4</i>)

>  From that date [1906] the town was legally "dry". <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(17) : 3</i>)

>  <b>HUTT</b> The east coast remained 'dry' until 1922 when special enabling legislation made a second poll possible. <i>(1999,Maori & Alcohol,67</i>)

>  Prior to the last general election Tawa was one of the only three dry suburbs in New Zealand. <i>(Oct 4, 2000,Capital Times,17</i>)

>  In 1864 came the proclamation that made the first part of the King Country - the Te Kuiti, Otorohanga and Kawhia districts - "dry". <i>(Sep 23, 1953,NZ Observer,6</i>)

>  The Horouta district went "dry" in 1911. <i>(Dec 14, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,23</i>)

>  It is said.. that in some "dry" places they will get up a smoke concert to celebrate a fellow having got over a severe stomach ache. <i>(Jan 8, 1910,NZ Truth,4</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  [Hdg] When to "Dry" Cows. <i>(May 7, 1892,Auckland Weekly News,34</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (of cows) not producing milk [in ODE]

>  There is a difference of opinion in regard to the time cows should become dry before calving. <i>(May 7, 1892,Auckland Weekly News,34</i>)

>  As soon as the cows are nearly dry, I am going for my holidays. <i>(Mar 11, 1924,Otago Witness,64</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a female animal that has not produced offspring after mating, either through non-conception or miscarriage, or a castrated male animal.

>  And while about 3% dries was the norm, this year it is up to 12%. 'We normally sell our dries but we've kept the better condition ones to help eliminate a cashflow problem later on when we would have to buy in ewes.' [drystock] <i>(Aug 12, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,15</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> of sheep, not too wet to prevent shearing [[Get cits from discards.]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> [1971 Boxing Day Kendrick Smithyman loquat: It was so dry there that even the rabbits were taking cut lunches.]

### dry as a sack of gum dust
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> But Andersen seemed neither drunk nor mad as he entered the store ... Then he approached the storekeeper and whispered something in his ear. `Not a taste,' said the latter aloud. `Dry as a sack of gum dust, I give you my word.' <i>(1895,Toll of the Bush,154</i>)

### dry as a wooden god
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  ... I went to sleep with the light on and woke up at one-thirty in the morning dry as a wooden god. <i>(Mar 3, 1950,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>) [NOTES: (P. Llewellyn)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ` ... let's get crackin on our own tank-up. Over to the bar at the high port. I'm dry as a wooden god.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,181</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> dry as a wooden god very dry or thirsty; popular NZ Navy WWII. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,40</i>)

### dry as the rim of a lime-burner's hat
 
 <i>phr.</i> of a person, very dry, thirsty (prob. an occas. use during WW1 from Eng dial of EDD dry as a lime-burner's clog. (Cumb); nf W3).

>  I'm as dry as the rim of a lime-burner's hat. <i>(Jun 7, 1918,NZEF Chronicles,205</i>)

### dry blow
 
 <i>v.</i> of gold

### dry country sheep
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a breed of sheep e.g. Corriedale that flourishes on dry pasture where it is not prone to footrot.

>  <b>GORDON</b> The biggest thing is that they can stand this climate a lot better than the Romneys. They're a dry country sheep. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,58</i>)

### dry cow
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to use dry-cow therapy for mastitis. (See [dry-cow therapy](dou-dyn.md#dry-cow-therapy))

>  We do not dry cow and have not had to use antibiotics to treat a cow since we started using homeopathics. <i>(Mar 13, 2001,Fencepost.com,Homeopathic.</i>)

### dry diggings
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The conglomerates accumulated on the slopes of the mountains are the proper field for the "dry diggings", while from the gravel and sand streams the gold is obtained by "wet diggings". <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),104</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER & PETERMAN</b> By the gradual wearing away of these rocks ... large masses of debris have been formed, and nature itself has executed an operation of gold-washing, by collecting the heavier particles and depositing them in the gullies of streams, or in the conglomerates covering the slopes of the hills. There are, therefore, two principal descriptions of diggings; either `river diggings', in the beds of streams, or `dry diggings', in the conglomerate and gravel accumulated on the slopes of the mountains. <i>(1864,Geology NZ,88</i>)

### dry dry
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a barren ewe or one that has not conceived a lamb in a season.

>  .. increasing lambing percentage by using recommended teachniques such as culling dry-dry and wet-dry ewes and shearing two-tooth before tupping. <i>(Dec 1964,NZ Journal of Agriculture,463</i>)

>  Dry dry ewes amounted to about four per cent, which is nothing much. <i>(Dec 9, 1976,NZ Farmer,45</i>)

>  Last season they recorded 7.2% ewe deaths over winter and lambing, wet dries were 7.8% dry dry 1.7% and 15% lamb deaths. <i>(Jun 1, 1994,NZ Farmer,22</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> 'Get the dry dries, the wet dries and the late wets' .. When Nimmo came by me, I asked him to translate. The first were fat barren ewes destined for harder territory, the second were those who lost their lambs destined for harder territory, and then he too was gone, but I figured 'late wets' were late lambers who'd want a wee spell before their lambs were docked. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country</i>)

### dry ewe
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ewe that is barren or has lost her lamb.

>  <b>MCKENNY</b> Another good plan is to shear all wethers, hoggets, and dry ewes first and to defer, somewhat, the shearing of the wet ewes. <i>(1927,Sheep-Farming, Fencing etc.,14</i>)

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> The dry shearing (hoggets, rams, wethers, and dry ewes) was done in November, and the wet ewes were shorn after weaning in February. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,23</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> October arrived and so did the shearing gang to do the dry shearing as it was called, or in other words, the hoggets and any wethers that may have been run through, and the dry ewes. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,24</i>)

### dry hash
 
 <i>n.</i> a baked (highly seasoned) dish made out of leftovers or oddments of meat, vegetable, breadcrumbs, without the addition of water; a dry kind of `shepherd's pie'. [DNZE says also dried hash, but no cit.]

>  <b>WALKER</b> ... moist and `dry' hashes. <i>(1902,Zealandia's Guerdon,57</i>)

>  [Miss Wilson] accomplished the impossible by abolishing Tuesday's abomination called Dry Hash [at Nelson Boy's College]. <i>(Dec 4, 1983,NZ Times,8</i>) [NOTES: [c1930s]]

>  There was a pretty hubbub in a toney Wellington hash-house last week ... the bachelors in these dry-hash establishments ... <i>(Oct 7, 1905,NZ Truth,16/4/7</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> `Dry hash, corned beef, then again the stew, ... <i>(1934,Journalese,209</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> `'Ere, take this. Not much, it ain't. Dry 'ash.' ... Dry hash, sailor. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,223</i>)

### dry horrors
 
 <i>n.</i> the D.T's.

>  There's hoodlums, hoodackys and hoodunnits Funny money, down the dunny, join the club The guy with dry horrors - drowning his sorrows little Johnny, Little horror, the Little grub. <i>(Dec 2004,NZ Memories,p18</i>)

>  Dry horrors.-State of a shepherd when back from a bust in town. <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  Dry Horrors: State of a shepherd when back from a bust in town. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,366</i>) [NOTES: Glossary]

### dry off
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere but first recorded in New Zealand] to cease the milking of dairy cows to prepare for calving.

>  Some practical dairymen have found that to resort to rather violent measures to dry off a cow that is a very persistent milker, lessens the cow's performance the next year. <i>(Dec 22, 1888,Egmont Star,12</i>)

>  What is the best method to use when drying off cows? <i>(Jun 20, 1922,NZ Journal of Agriculture,381</i>)

>  The milk sugar should be got rid of as quickly as possible when time came for drying the cow off and this was best accomplished by completing the drying-off in the minimum time. Treating the subject at length, Mr Wood said that a cow should not be 'dried off' as was commonly understood and practised, but should be left alone and allowed to do so naturally. <i>(Aug 9, 1924,Hawera Star,10</i>)

>  <b>TINSLEY</b> As in most other companies [dairy companies] the milk supply had all but stopped as cows were dried off for the winter months. <i>(1985,Cheese Punchers' Ball, The,163</i>)

>  Drying off cows early does not prevent this season's production target being achieved. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,10</i>)

>  <b>TINSLEY</b> If the trend reveals the cows are losing condition rapidly, dry-off thinner cows or take other remedial action. [dry-off] <i>(1993,Cheese Punchers' Ball, The,68</i>)

>  The cows were nearly all dried off. <i>(Jun 1951,Landfall,5(2) 18 : 112</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> The cows were being dried off for the winter, and he was occupying his spare time doing up fences and cleaning out drains. <i>(1936,Follow the Call,144</i>)

>  It is generally believed that all sorts of devious things happen to a cow which is not "dried off" before being burned out. <i>(Nov 30, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,86</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### dry till
 
 <i>n.</i> a situation where the host or organising body of a function is paying for drinks etc. ["When I am invited to a function and told that there will be a dry till, I am never quite certain initially whether it means that I pay or the organisation does but I think the latter." [P.M.MacCallum]]

### Dry Valley
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McPHERSON</b> Transecting the Transantarctic Mountains west of McMurdo Sound are three ice and snow-free valleys collectively called the Dry Valleys. The valleys extend from the 2,750 m Polar Plateau in the west to within a short distance of the sea. Along with some smaller subsidiary valleys the system constitutes the largest ice-free area in Antarctica, a total of approximately 4,000 square km. <i>(1975,Footprints on a Frozen Continent,64,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McPHERSON</b> The Dry Valley Region is a geologist's paradise because of the excellent exposure of rock, without grass, trees, soil or snow, to hide it. <i>(1975,Footprints on a Frozen Continent,85,1979 ed.</i>)

>  Throughout the summer Vanda Station proved its value as a staging station for parties operating within the Dry Valley region... Apart from making measurements throughout the year of the climate of this unique "Dry Valley" area and of the "hot" lake near the station, the wintering-over party also carry out upper atmosphere physics and earthquake observations in conjunction with Scott Base. <i>(1969,AJHR,H34 : 65</i>)

>  <b>KNIGHT</b> Frighteningly, as this book was going to press, newspapers in New Zealand reported a significant rise in Lake Vanda, in the Dry Valley region of the Ross Dependency. <i>(1988,Icebound,113</i>)

>  There are three main Dry Valleys, the Victoria Valley in the north, the Taylor Valley in the south and the Wright Valley in the west. Together these three valley systems expose 4000 km2 of moraines, sediments and rocks - an oasis on the edge of the ice sheet. <i>(1978,NZ Antarctic Record,1(1) : 15</i>)

>  The Dry Valleys were originally discovered in 1903 by Scott, Evans and Lashly and since the rediscovery by geologists Peter Webb and Barrie McKelvey in 1957, the work of summer field parties, including geologists, chemists and physicists, led to the decision to winter at Vanda Station. <i>(Mar 1970,Antarctic,5(9) : 371</i>)

### dry, the
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used in Aust.] dryness, dry spell or drought.

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> We've had an awful lot of the dry. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,51</i>)

### dry-chucker-in
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BREMNER</b> A description of an unidentified sheepstation in 1881 recorded that a twenty-two man gang ... put through [the sheep-wash] 1250 sheep in a ... day. The `yarder-up' kept the pen nearest the wash full of sheep. Two `dry-chuckers-in' forced sheep through a swing door where they fell four feet into a 20-foot wooden trough full of hot soapy water. ... The trough was divided into four partitions with a `crutcher' at each. Four times each sheep was soaped, its back scrubbed with a crutch. ... At the end of the trough three `wet-chuckers-in' hauled frightened sheep out and sent them down three slides to the cold-water rinsing wells. Each well had two spouts of cold water and four hands called `spouters' who tilted the sheep, parting the wool <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,15</i>) [NOTES: 1881]

### dry-cow therapy
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a treatment of newly dried off cows with a long term slow acting antibiotic to prevent mastitis.

>  The objects of dry cow therapy are 1) to eliminate existing infections of the udder 2) to prevent new infections during the dry period. Dry cow therapy helps protect the cow against these new infections because antibiotics are present in the udder for many weeks. <i>(Feb 23, 1978,NZ Farmer,17</i>)

>  A warning against farmers expecting 100 per cent results from dry cow therapy can be a valuable tool against mastitis. The Wallaceville researchers said that dry cow therapy was unable to eliminate all infections or prevent reinfection or new infection during the dry period. <i>(Apr 29, 1980,NZ Dairy Exporter</i>)

>  Dairyfarmers should note those cows affected and consider greater use of dry-cow therapy at drying-off. <i>(Mar 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,03</i>)

>  The way dry-cow therapy is used is critical to success .. Do not milk the cows the last time they are brought to the shed, but give them dry-cow therapy. <i>(Mar 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,36</i>)

>  Dry cow therapy should be done immediately after a cow's last milking. <i>(Feb 7, 1990,NZ Farmer,19</i>)

>  The cell count average is low, all cows will be dry by sale day and will have been dry cow therapy treated. [dry cow therapy] <i>(May 19, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,18</i>)

### dry-fleeced
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] sheltered from dew or weather prior to shearing.

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> After tea Bill had to help pack the shed with another cut of dry-fleeced sheep for the morning run. <i>(1981,Sunrise on Hikurangi,49</i>)

### dry-goat therapy
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) administration of antibiotics when a dairy goat is dried off. (See [dry-cow therapy](dou-dyn.md#dry-cow-therapy))

>  Whole-herd dry-goat therapy is likely to reduce BTSCC more than part-herd ... <i>(2002,Lifestyle Farmer,April/May 10</i>)

### dry-off
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the cessation of milking of dairy cows or goats.

>  On day of dry-off, draft and do dry-goat treatment ... <i>(2002,Lifestyle Farmer,April/May 10</i>)

### dry-skin
 
 <i>adj.</i> [Is this merely a descriptive term or is the punct. astray: = a dry skin, itching for growth ...'Write Alpers.]

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> All that day the heat was terrible. The wind blew close to the ground - rooted among the tussock grass - slithered along the road, so that the white pumice dust swirled in our faces - settled and sifted over us and was like a dry-skin itching for growth on our bodies. <i>(1912,Stories,109,1984 Alpers ed.</i>)

### dryblowing
 
 <i>n.</i> a process where a draught of air is used to separate esp. gold particles from attendant rubbish.

>  <b>BAKER</b> The finding of gold brought ... nuggeting, dryblowing ... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,28</i>)

>  dry-blowing n. gold-mining term, separating gold by placing pounded material in one dish, pouring it slowly from a height into another, and allowing the wind or a bellows to blow the dross away. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

### drying green
 
 <i>n.</i> an outdoor paddock fitted so that fibre material can be spread or hung out ro dry or bleach.

>  <b>CARR</b> A number of stations ... run a scour ... From five to seven men are generally employed ... at the wash, and the balance on the drying green. ... The work on the green [at the port scour] is practically the same as on a sheep station. <i>(1913,Country Work,18</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> [The flax fibre] is carted ... to the drying green, generally called the `paddock', where it is spread out to dry and bleach. ... It is [then] taken in hand by the `scutcher', who feeds it into a revolving drum ... which beats the refuse out. <i>(1913,Country Work,44</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a building for drying sealskins. [(sealers')]

### drying off
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  While long lactation is an important factor for high production per cow, consulting officers occasionally advise early drying off to build up reserves of feed for the winter. <i>(Feb 1960,NZ Dairy Exporter,52</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [dairy cows] the ceasing of lactation to bear calves.

>  In general the drying off process should be commenced at least two months before calving. <i>(Jun 20, 1922,NZ Journal of Agriculture,381</i>)

>  The milk sugar should be got rid of as quickly as possible when time came for drying the cow off and this was best accomplished by completing the drying-off in the minimum time. <i>(Aug 9, 1924,Hawera Star,10</i>)

>  They always aim to have a paddock or two of grass on the wintering area of the farm closed up and spelled ready for the herd at drying off. <i>(1950,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,18</i>)

>  Treatment of a dairy herd should be routine at drying-off time as this corresponds well with their greatest danger period from lice. <i>(Oct 1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture,68</i>)

>  Could it be that poor cow condition at drying-off had a big part in the spring problem? [drying-off; drying-off time] <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,10</i>)

### drying paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a paddock where fibrous flax hemp, straw and wool are dried.

>  <b>CHINN</b> We had on hand, ready for shipping, sixty bales of dressed hemp, besides a quantity of fibre in the drying paddock. <i>(1963,Packtrack to Highway,121</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Out of the tanks, a machine feeds the retted straw (and it absorbs three times its own weight of water) on to the drying paddocks. <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,292</i>)

>  <b>BREMNER</b> In front behind the wooden fence is the draining stage from which wool was carried in baskets to the drying paddock. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,38</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> After the fibre had been taken to the drying paddocks, spread on the ground, turned (sometimes twice), and in the wintertime hung on the fences to allow drying to take place ... <i>(1990,Redan, Valley of Farming and Flaxmilling,178</i>)

>  <b>BRITTAIN</b> .. the wool was cleaned of grease and dirt in steam boilers, rinsed, spun dry and manhandled onto scrim sheets. These were carted in open drays to the drying paddocks. [drying green, drying field] <i>(2000,Lake Coleridge,84</i>)

### Drysdale
 
 <i>n.</i> a NZ breed of sheep yielding a coarse wool used in carpet manufacture [after Dr F.W.Dry, 1891-1979, agricultural geneticist].

>  <b>BROOKING</b> Much of the credit for producing the Drysdale breed must go to its original ancestor the Neilson ram lamb named "Pa Neilson", which... had such a hairy coat that Dry had to invent another category which he named the "N"-grade in honour of its owner. <i>(1977,Massey - Its Early Years,87</i>)

>  He cites the example of the Drysdale sheep, a wool breed developed in the 1950 for the carpet industry, that has now virtually disappeared from New Zealand farms. <i>(Oct 25, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

>  The man who developed the Drysdale sheep breed, Dr Francis William Dry has died in Palmerston North. He was [87 years old.] Dr Dye was born in Driffield, Yorkshire, and educated at Bridlington School and Leeds University from which he graduated in 1925 as a Doctor of Science. He came to New Zealand in 1929 to be a lecturer at the newly-founded Massey Agricultural College. Shortly after his arrival Dr Dry discovered a ram lamb in a flock near Palmerston North which had an extremely hairy fleece due to an abundance of halo-hairs. Dr Dry's successful efforts to isolate the gene in Romney led to the development of the Drysdale. Dr Dry devoted nearly 40 years studying the genetic makeup of the fleece of the sheep and published many papers. He retired from Massey University in 1956 as Professor of Animal Genetics but continued his interest in the Drysdale till his death. <i>(Jul 17, 1979,Dominion,5/1</i>)

>  Drysdale n. NZ breed of sheep developed from Romneys and Cheviots yielding wool which when blended with crossbred wool is suitable for carpet making. [F.W. Dry, agricultural scientist] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,231</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 149]]

>  Drysdales are brighter, too. That was one of the best of the series, the year they used drysdales. <i>(May 24, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred sheep, developed in the 1960s from a hairy-fibred Romney and Cheviot-cross, esp. for carpet production. [after Dr F W Dry (1891-1979) geneticist and hair biologist]

>  The Drysdale .. produces the best carpet wool in the world. <i>(Dec 1970,NZ Journal of Agriculture,69</i>)

>  Drysdales will give an increase in wool weight per sheep per year, and as good or better lambing percentages. <i>(Nov 13, 1975,NZ Farmer,60</i>)

>  <b>WARMAN</b> The Drysdale is descended from one of nature's genetic freaks that became the subject of pure scientific research. <i>(1991,What Sheep is that?,28</i>)

>  <b>MEADOWS</b> The Drysdale was developed during the 1960s into a commercial breed, and its medullated wool fibre is now a vital component in New Zealand carpet blends. <i>(1997,Sheep Breeds of New Zealand,48</i>)

>  <b>MEADOWS</b> Dual purpose sheep: sheep from which production has an equal emphasis on wool and meat. <i>(1997,Sheep Breeds of New Zealand,89</i>)

### dual-purpose
 
 <i>adj.</i> of sheep, bred for both wool and meat production. [OMIT covered by OED2 dual 3, of cattle breeds.] [farmers']

>  <b>BOWEN</b> ... Merinos, run on hard, dry country, are much lighter to handle than `dual purpose', or mutton breeds run on high-producing lands... The Corriedale is a very popular dual- [132] purpose breed, with a good heavy fleece of quality wool, and an excellent carcass, which makes the Corriedale ewe ideal for producing fat lambs. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,131</i>)

### dub
 
 <i>n.</i> Often pl. Cheap clay marbles, esp. ones put down to be fired at; a marble game or action where marbles are laid down, usu. in a ring, to be fired at. Compare also dub in (marbles). [cf also: [(children's) ca1918-20 Auckland p.c. a male informant 66 years old (c1978) to Professor Graeme Kennedy. A common term in Auckland in the 1920s]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. [175] Here is Little Ring as played in Waitara, Taranaki, in 1899: A team would consist of six to eight boys [order of shooting determined] ... An oval ring was decribed in the earth by means of a stick, and each player would `dub in' one marble ... A line was drawn some six or eight feet away, as the firing line, and the first player sang out `Fire!' as he fired the first shot.] <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> It is important to distinguish these `Action-Terms' of marbles from the innumerable merely descriptive marble terms which have no effect on the course of the action - terms such asRinger, In, Smug, Dead Stick, Skun, Skinned, Sported off, Scat, Cannons, Dubs, Kills, Funs, Keeps, Pink. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/770</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Here are some [marble] terms from Taranaki around 1880-1890: firsts, lay-up, stakes, tips, sticking-taw, nothings, anythings, fade-it, knuckledown stiff, dubs, finking [sic, ?funking], sips, spanning, placing; and from Dunedin School in 1949: knee high, manyies, tracks, screws, nuts, sleets, kills, forces, dig, dubs, cheats. (child marble-players') <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,269</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a double-decker tramcar [(obs.)]

>  ... so that our next-to-eldest can get away at 7.45 to catch the `8 o'clock dub.' This double-decker tram must have been on the tracks for close on fifty years. <i>(Mar 1954,Parson's Packet,3</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [ca1938 Havelock schoolchildren. Also Taranaki (DEH)]

>  <b>ADSETT</b> No luck tonight, I'll have to dub you home to keep me hands in practice. Never touched the handle-bars once last time. <i>(1963,Magpie Sings, A,177</i>)

>  dub v.t. (bb.) ... N.Z. colloq. = double-bank. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,231</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 149]]

>  NICKY: I was dubbing my little brother down the footpath and this van comes out from behind these bushes. <i>(Jul 20, 1987,Evening Post,18</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> a cockfighting term

>  <b>SEDDON</b> Cock fighting was a favoured sport on the goldfields and some very gallant birds were bred for this old English pastime. At Marsden, a secluded mining township in the bush some distance from Kumara, many cock fights were fought and a lot of money changed hands over the fate of these fights called `mains.' One of the cockers gave my father a beautiful white cock, his comb dubbed, trimmed and fighting fit. <i>(1986,Seddons, The,16</i>) [NOTES: c1880s in Seddon The Seddons (1968) 16]



 
 <i>n.</i> short for double-bank; horseback ride behind the rider or on the bar of a bicycle [Common usage in 1930s ca1938 Havelock schoolchildren. -->ca1940 Wellington (R. Mason) -->ca1951 Professor A.B. Cochrane (Wellington)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> doub/dub/dubb short for `doubling', in context of giving someone a lift on your bike; Australians prefer `double-bank' or `double-dink'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,39</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (graffiti term)

>  Leaving the ranks of bored adolescents getting quick thrills from quick tags (toys, as the amateurs are called), she has gone on to the more esteemed throwup or bomb (a tag in fat letters with the outline coloured in), then the dub (a throwup with an outer line and bubbles), and finally the piece, short for masterpiece (a dub with filled in letters and a painted background). <i>(Jan 20, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> In this [West Coast version of Duckstones] the duckstone belonged to the He. As soon as he put his duckstone on the big stone the game commenced and the others began to fire their own `dubs' at the duckstone. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,167</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> In my hurry into tea [at the hotel] I planted them [the watches] all in a small drawer at the side of the old-fashioned duchesse, ... <i>(1943,Pure Gold,17</i>)

### dub fern
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> The uphill land produces scarcely anything but very dwarf Tea trees dub fern and a great quantity of a plant something like what we call in England Cats tail grass which indicats [sic] the ground to be inclined to wet <i>(Mar 22, 1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Mar 22 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 362]

### dub in
 
 <i>v.</i> contribute var. of dob in [CHECK], to put in a share of esp.money into a pool. [[But this is surely merely OED2 dub v&sup4. Make a contribution 1823-.] OMIT -->ca1945 Wellington]

>  It must have been that year that Johnnie and Ginge and I started the grand money-raising scheme, because dubbing-in for Miss Raynor's farewell present, and then the Christmas shopping, left us really hard-up for the rest of the long holidays. <i>(1969,NZ School Journal,4(3): 00</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Here is Little Ring as played in Waitara, Taranaki, in 1899: A team would consist of six to eightboys [order of shooting determined] ... An oval ring was decribed in the earth by means of a stick, and each player would `dub in' one marble ... A line was drawn some six or eight feet away, as the firing line, and the first player sang out `Fire!' as he fired the first shot. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

>  dub in vb. (tr.) N.Z. inf. to contribute to the cost of something: we'll all dub in a dollar for the trip. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,343</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 149]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> dub in to contribute to a kitty or collection; eg `Are you dubbing in for her going away present?' Local variant of `dub up', pay up, mid-C19 English slang. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,40</i>)

### dubber
 
 <i>n.</i> [?sense and derivation: ?OED2 dub v1. 6 b. to trim or crop hedges, trees, etc: ? 10 to trim or work level with an adze: ??AND dubboA backblocker from Dubbo, but 1973 earliest: CHECK EDD. ?a misprint, but what of? Are these workers on the main trunk project?]

>  <b>MYERS</b> On clearings of bush, hundreds of `dubbers,' sturdy, tough pioneers, lived along the [main trunk] line in slab whares and canvas tents. <i>(1941,Valiant Love,9</i>)

### dubbing-in
 
 <i>n.</i> contribution

>  It must have been that year that Johnnie and Ginge and I started the grand money-raising scheme, because dubbing-in for Miss Raynor's farewell present, and then the Christmas shopping left us really hard-up for the rest of the long holidays. <i>(1969,NZ School Journal,4(3):</i>)

### duchess
 
 <i>v.</i> treat like a duchess

>  Some ... conscious of being "duchessed" by unaccustomed personal star treatment ... were cautious about just how far Clark would go to address urgent issues . . . <i>(Jan 20, 2005,NZ Herald,C1</i>)

### duchess(e)
 
 <i>n.</i> in NZ duchesse has developed meanings wider than the orig. and now seems applied not only to a dressing table with drawers, but to a low chest of drawers (?`lowboy') without a mirror; and occas. (?because the word is now obsolesc. or unfamiliar) to a `tallboy'. [check HNZD2]

>  [Advt] Modern Reflex Mirror Duchess, withCabriolet Legs... Now Only GBP4/10/-. <i>(Jan 19, 1938,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

>  [Advt] Harley and Co., Ltd., will submitto Public Auction.. Oak Bedroom Suitecomprising Duchesse, Wardrobe, Bedsteadand Pedestal. <i>(Apr 1, 1924,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

>  [Advt] Dingle Bros Reliable Furnishing Emporium. . . Duchess Suites, Combination Chests, Couches in endless variety. <i>(Jan 3, 1900,Taranaki Herald,4</i>)

>  [Advt] R.M. Corsey. . . Duchess Chests, Duchess Suites <i>(Jan 3, 1900,Taranaki Herald,5</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> The tweezers ... stayed in exactly the same place on the duchesse. <i>(1957,Owls Do Cry,54</i>) [NOTES: [OED2 I xii]]

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> Suddenly a woman hisses, `There's borer in the back of this duchess.' ... What is a duchess? What is borer? <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,35</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> Duchesse: The hereditary New Zealand title for a LOW CHEST OF DRAWERS WITH A MIRROR on it so it can double as a dressing table. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,35</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> After we was born we was put in a drawer in a duchesse <i>(1987,Oracles and Miracles,3</i>)

### duchess(e) chest
 
 <i>n.</i> = duchesse

>  Duchesse Chests.. from 42s to GBP10. <i>(Dec 20, 1907,Dominion,11</i>)

>  [Advt] 14 Days Sale. Furniture,Consisting of Duchess Chests,Washstands.. Chairs. <i>(May 23, 1918,Greymouth Evening Star,1</i>)

>  [Advt] Duchesse Chests, Walnut finish,bevelled mirror - 37/6, 42/6, 65/-. <i>(May 1, 1912,Otago Witness</i>)

>  Special line of oak Duchess Chests. <i>(Mar 10, 1919,Evening Post,4</i>)

### duchesse pair
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Advt] Duchesse Pair, Latest Design,Bevel Mirror, Brackets, Tile BackWashstand. <i>(Jan 14, 1905,NZ Herald,6</i>)

### duck
 
 <i>n.</i> [[Check ca1899 1957 Pascoe MED 235/7 [Birds South Westland] See also parera, putangitangi, whio]

>  Sea and water fowles of all sorts are however in no great plenty, those known in Europe are Ducks, Shags, Gannets and gulls all of which were eat(?) by us and found exceeding good, ... <i>(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/276,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Ducks, shags, black oyster-catchers, and some sorts of plovers were very numerous here [at Dusky Bay]. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/133</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> The Best kinds were Ducks, Curlews, a bird very much resembling a Wood Cock & Sea Pies; - Shags, Wood Hens, & Parrots were in great abundance ... the Wood Hen very much resembles the Common Barn door Fowl in England <i>(Nov 1791,Journal of Voyage in HMS 'Chatham',50</i>)

>  <b>PARKER</b> `There are eleven species of Native Ducks belonging to nine genera, all found elsewhere, except two--the little Flightless Duck of the Auckland Islands (genus <i>Nesonetta</i>) and the Blue Mountain Duck (<i>Hymenolaemus</i>). Among the most interesting of the non-endemic forms, are the Paradise Duck or Sheldrake (<i>Casarca variegata</i>), the Brown Duck (<i>Anas chlorotis</i>), the Shoveller or Spoonbill duck (<i>Rhynchaspis variegata</i>), and the Scaup or Black Teal (<i>Fuligula Novae-Zealandiae</i>).'] <i>(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,117</i>) [NOTES: [Morris]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Early, of various native spp.; now, also introduced spp., ?esp. the mallard gen sp

>  <b>FORSTER</b> In this fine place, we found a number of wild fowl, and particularly wild ducks ... from whence we gave it the name of Duck Cove. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/137</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> ... we dined off a wild duck and potatoes, as also some provisions we had brought with us. The duck Shunghee shot at the lake. <i>(1815,Letters & Journals,99,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRAIK</b> Among them are many sorts of wild ducks, large wood-pigeons, sea gulls, rails, parrots, and parroquets. <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,187</i>)

>  The River `Wiiaw' is very spacious in places, ... and abounds in fish, eels and wild-ducks of 3 or 4 different kinds. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,74,1986 ed.</i>)

>  I found [round Wellington] Teal, common Wild Duck, the paradise Duck, a variety of the Grebe, a kind of Water Hen the proper name of which I do not know. <i>(Jun 1840,Boultbee Journal,230,1966 ed.</i>)

>  We scarcely took a step that did not delight us; the soil good, the banks of the [Waipa] river high, interspersed with woods of kahikatea. ... Here the banks of the river were covered with wild-ducks. <i>(1842,GBPP,(No.569) 98</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Jan 4 Report of George Clarke, Protector of Aborigines in GBPP 1842 (No.569) 98]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> These (wild ducks of different sorts) are principally the black, the grey, the blue-winged, and the paradise duck, or `pa tangi tangi', as it is called by the natives. The last is nearly as large as a goose, and of beautiful plumage <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/57</i>)

>  Wild ducks, called `Paradise Ducks', nearly as large as a Goose, splendid eating. <i>(1848,Otago Journal,3/47</i>) [NOTES: (R. Donaldson)]

>  <b>POWER</b> A few curlews, whale-birds, sand-pipers, and wild ducks frequent the coast and river swamps, but are generally fishy, and unfit for the table. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,72</i>)

>  <b>1/80</b> [The Waikato] runs through ... deep fern fringed with raupo (a bullrush) ...[sic] and the magnificent toi-toi, a gigantic grass whose flower stalk is from 8 to 12 feet and has an exquisite feather of two feet long playing in every air. This grass is ... with the phormium tenax the most remarkable of the open country vegetable products. ... You see many nativedwellings & many canoes for they are a most locomotive people, few as ignorant of all but their own kainga as our British Hodge. There are troops of wild ducks & shags, some hawks of large size, bitterns & smaller waterfowl unknown to me. <i>(Mar 25, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1960</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  <b>BARKER</b> I don't think a minute passed without a brace of wild ducks flying past, - grey, blue, and Paradise. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,1870,181 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Several kinds of birds are indigenous to the woods and waters; among them the oyster-catcher, bittern, kingfisher, cormorant, quail, wild-duck, mocking or [16] parson bird, parrots, parroquets, woodhen, pigeon, etc. (the [sic] gigantic Moa bird is believed to be now quite extinct). <i>(1867,Twelve Years in Canterbury,15</i>)

>  <b>HAR T</b> At the back ... was a large raupo swamp, which was a rare resort for wild ducks and pukaki. <i>(1886,Stray Leaves,13</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The Sultan-hen of brilliant plumage (<i>Porphyris melanotus</i>) [sic], or the Pukeko of the natives; the New Zealand bittern or Matuku (<i>Botaurus melanotus</i>), and the wild-duck Parena [sic] (<i>Spatula rhynchotis</i>). <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),293fn</i>)

### duck fart
 
 <i>n.</i> The noise of a small stone plopping into water; occas. the stone itself. [(schoolboys') ca1938 Havelock schoolboys. ca1940 Palmerston North p.c. Rodney Simonsen.]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The hurling of stones straight up in the air so they would fall into the back-water with that satisfying plup known as a duck fart. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,51</i>)

### duck itch
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LELAND</b> Duck itch: a RASH that develops as a result of contact with a waterborne parasite that infests some of New Zealand's southern lakes. It has just left its snail host and is looking for a duck to grow up in. Look for warning signs. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,35</i>)

### duck shoot
 
 <i>n.</i> [[Cf also duck-shooting season. nf OED2: duckshooter in OED]]

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> `Used to be real live-wires when we were first married,' said Mrs Foden philosophically. `Never missed a tin-kettling or first-footing; euchre evenings, sales, duck shoots, dances, send-offs, fires. We always went. ...' <i>(1979,Supper Waltz Wilson,47</i>)

### duck shover
 
 <i>n.</i> someone who passes on blame

>  [Hdg] Blood on hands of duck shovers. <i>(Oct 13, 2004,Northern Courier,3</i>)

### duck shoving
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) touting for passengers in the street instead of wating one's turn on the rank. [(cabmen's obs.)]

>  The case was one of a series of cases of what was technically known as `duck shoving', a process of getting passengers which operated unfairly against the cabmen who stayed on the licensed stand and obeyed the by-law. <i>(Jan 25, 1896,Otago Daily Times,3</i>) [NOTES: [Morris]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> undecisiveness; passing the buck [[AND b. 1942.]]

>  The Government's Proposals for Resource Management Law Reform: a True Reform or a Case of Duck Shoving? <i>(Jun 1989,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,84/52</i>)

>  They were fed up with 'duck-shoving'. <i>(Dec 13, 1990,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  No duck shufflin', yer cow. <i>(Nov 23, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,96</i>)

>  We have been the perfect vehicle for duck-shoving from one department to another. We have been shunted from the Health Department to the Social Welfare Department to the Education Department. Each in turn has declined responsibility for us. <i>(Oct 20, 1972,Press, The,1/3</i>)

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> The words flew, and enriched Celia's [an English visitor's] vocabulary. `Duck shoving' was the local name for buck-passing between government departments. A `Queen Street farmer' was a city slicker who owned land or stock for tax-fiddle reasons. <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,26</i>)

### duck's breakfast
 
 <i>n.</i> a drink of water and nothing to eat (cf. Irishman's dinner, dog's breakfast).

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> He absolutely dropped his bundle, and, to make matters worse, I had started off with a duck'sbreakfast, but I saw a cookhouse and decided to give it a pop for a binder. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>) [NOTES: 1935 A.E. Strong WW1 scene in Partridge Slang Today 287]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Duck's breakfast. A drink of water with nothing to eat: esp. New Zealanders': C. 20. Cf. Irishman's dinner. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,246</i>)

>  ... `ducks' breakfast,' `to float up to,' `to blow up to,' `to sleep in the Star Hotel' need no explanation. <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>McGILL</b> duck's breakfast or dinner a drink of water to WWI soldiers. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,40</i>)

### duck's disease
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I have been teased for the way I stand - it's called duck's disease... Now that I have left school and I stand up for myself and no-one has anything to say about duck's disease, I am getting on with my life. <i>(Aug 2000,Tearaway,5</i>)

>  I have been teased for the way I stand - it's called duck's disease... Now that I have left school and I stand up for myself and on-one has anything to say about duck's disease, I am getting on with my life. <i>(Aug 2000,Tearaway,5</i>)

 <i>n.</i> ?bankruptcy; a severe loss. [a duck's egg is zero]

 <i>n.</i> frilled shag, <i>Phalacrocorax melanolencus</i>, Vieill. [(Kenepuru Sound, Marlborough: obs.)]

>  The frilled shag is, owing to its cautious habits, called the `duck-scarer' by the sportsmen living in the above-mentioned sound [Kenepuru]. <i>(1896,TrNZI,28/367</i>)

### duck-shooting season
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It was a week or so before the opening of the duck-shooting season. <i>(Dec 1, 1928,NZ Free Lance,17</i>)

### duck-shove
 
 <i>v.</i> to avoid blame or responsibility

>  Mr Dunne is strongly critical of all the national and local politicians, roading agencies and other planning bodies "who constantly dither, duck shove and otherwise prevaricate" while motorists continue to die needlessly on the notorious Centennial Highway. <i>(Oct 13, 2004,Northern Courier,3</i>)

>  As the leader of the [New Zealand] Opposition complained, the Government has `dodged and duckshoved' the issue. <i>(Nov 4, 1960,Spectator (London),677</i>) [NOTES: [OED2]]

>  duck-shove NZ & Austral. sl.. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,232</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 149]]

>  duckshove to cheat or pass responsibility on to someone else, perceived as a typical bureaucratic trick; eg `The public is always complaining of duckshoving in that department.' Originally cabbies jumping the queue in mid-C19 Melbourne cab ranks. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,McGILL,40 ed.</i>)

>  And then there was the bit where she duck-shoved in Parliament about whether she had ever seen, let alone written the letter of apology. <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

 <i>n.</i> A spec. use of duckboard - a slatted timber path laid down in the trenches.]
