### duckstone
 
 <i>n.</i> [(children's) Often pl.]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Others wanted [to play] `Prisoners' Base' or `Chivvy', `Brewer', `Egg-in-the hat', `Duck-stone', `Red Rover', or `Kingy-seeny'. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,84</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Duckstones and Tops were the throwing games of greatest historical importance. Others were Pegknife, Ducks and Drakes, Snowballing, stone and ball throwing. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/739</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> At school, however, there was little place for Duck Stones, with its dangerous stone-throwing. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,217</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> Some of the simple games played by the [West Coast] children were rounders, shinty, duckstone, tiggy touchwood, kiss in the ring, ... cockfighting, marbles, fly the garter and leapfrog. <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,38</i>) [NOTES: [c1870s] 1953 Patrick Magee (aged 86) Growing Up at the Diggings in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 38]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> A game that was based directly upon children's interest in throwing stones was Duckstones (K-16). ... In the simplest form, the players put one stone on top of a larger stone and then attempted to knock the top one off by throwing other stones at it. The player who knocked off the most stones won. ... (South Clutha, 1875; Stillwater, Nelson, 1885; Wakefield, Nelson, 1875). <i>Ibid.</i> 167 In this [West Coast version of Duckstones] the duckstone belonged to the He. As soon as he put his duckstone on the big stone the game commenced and the others began to fire their own `dubs' at the duckstone. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,166</i>)

### duckweed
 
 <i>n.</i> any of a variety of New Zealand aquatic plants.

>  (And) <i>L. minor </i> <i>(1907,AJHR,C-8</i>) [NOTES: Report on Botanical Survey of Kapiti Island (]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>L. minor </i> <i>(1908,AJHR,C-14</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Survey Waipoua Kauri Forest in AJHR]

>  (And) <i>(1916,Dunedin Field Club Plant Catalogues</i>)

>  <i>duckweed [s] Lemna sp. </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,23</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> From having formerly been the principal abode of the Rotu-rua natives, ... it was always well cultivated, and ... plantain, duckweed, and others, which in such cases generally spring up, vary agreeably the usually brown tint of the lower native vegetation. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/393</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>Lemna</i> ... A very curious genus, known as Duckweed in England, and found in most parts of the temperate world. ... <i>Lemna</i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 238-239</i>)

>  Duckweed. <i>Lemna</i> a. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),764</i>)

>  Duck weed.<i> Lemna minor</i>, Linn. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/50</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> (And) <i>L. minor </i> <i>(1906,Introductory Classbook of Botany</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>L[Lemna] minor, Linn. ... Duckweed. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,745</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Nertera depressa</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Nertera depressa </i> <i>(1908,AJHR,C-14</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Survey Waipoua Kauri Forest]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Anacharis canadensis (Elodea canadensis). </i>. [ca1954 Wairarapa (R. Mason) `Apparently well established in the district. The plant is nothing like the true duckweeds (<i>Lemna</i> spp)]



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> starwort, also called purple-backed duckweed. [(R. Mason) In North Auckland]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> STARWORT (<i>Callitriche verna</i> [spp.]), sometimes called drain weed or even duckweed, is not uncommon in still and slow-running water. ... [It is native to New Zealand.] <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,129,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>duckweed [=] purple-backed duckweed: starwort: watermeal [s] ... duckweed, purple-backed [s] Spirodela oligorrhiza</i> Kurz. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,23</i>)

### dud
 
 <i>v.</i> swindle, cheat (out of money)

>  Some of that vitriol came from Australian fund managers who got dudded in the Lion Nathan deal, but rarely invest in New Zealand companies anyway because the playing field is tilted in favour of the locals. <i>(May 7, 1998,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  The Pacifika concept may be dead but the feeling that Cullen Sports tried to dud the Warriors and the New Zealand Rugby League and the fans won't fade quite so fast. <i>(Nov 20, 2005,Sunday Star Times,B14</i>)

>  "Shoddy dropping" and "dudding" are lawful occupations at present. <i>(Sep 23, 1953,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  Dudded: It is what some absolutely useless bastard does to you when they get their claws on your shekels. <i>(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,277</i>)

### dud up
 
 <i>v.</i> to fabricate evidence to secure a conviction

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Dud up. (v) Fabricate evidence to secure a conviction <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)

### dudapper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  While presenting a programme on Radio Sport recently, I asked listeners what a "dudapper" was... Eventually, one caller came up with the correct answer : a dudapper was once the term used to describe a delivery that bounced twice before reaching the batsman. Shortly after, an elderly gentleman rang in to say that when he was a youngster playing school cricket in England, a team-mate by the name of Nipper Dingle made a specialty of such a delivery. I distinctly remember," said the caller, " our master taking us for practices and saying, 'I've never seen a boy who could bowl a dudapper like you, Nipper Dingle.'" <i>(Mar 18, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

### dudeen
 
 <i>n.</i> a (clay) pipe. (The word as doodee /du|di:/ was occas. used by old people in Havelock in my childhood for `pipe': `he won't go anywhere without his old doodee.')

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> We no sooner brought up [at Lyttelton] than ... our little craft was crowded with persons, eager for news, smoking their short dudees. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,12</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... we were roused by a roaring in our ears, and had only time, the one to snatch his pocket-book ..., the other his black dudheen ... before the hut was a raging furnace. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,95</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> [The Irishman] smoked a short dodeen pipe. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,32</i>)

>  <b>SENIOR</b> [He] knocks the ashes out of his dudheen. <i>(1880,Trout and Travel,100</i>)

>  <b>A TRAMP</b> The boss of [the Cathedral] is Bishop Julius ... who smokes a `dudeen' and reads shilling shockers. <i>(1891,Casual Ramblings,141</i>)

### duds
 
 <i>n.</i> a woman's breasts [(pl.) ca1941 St Patrick's College, Silverstream]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Telling her he hadn't seen such lovely duds outside a garage calendar. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,165</i>)

### duff
 
 <i>n.</i> boiled or steamed suet pudding, often with dried fruit in it; a plum pudding. [(mainly early outdoor and farming usage)]

>  "Got a duff?"... "Oh, yes. I made two,filled ours with thrums and sent one tomother." <i>(Apr 2, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,71</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> ... our delicate ... stomachs received the most racy and highly esteemed dainties peculiar to the cuddy table, and which when you all come out you will soon discover, such as `Duff & Treacle', Boiled Pudding, Plum Duff, ... and bread in particular. <i>(1851,Biscuit and Butter,78,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> I. left at home with strict injunctions to make a duff - <i>(Sep 8, 1867,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,115</i>)

>  <b>HODDER</b> I chopped suet and plums to make a `duff' for dinner. <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,10</i>)

>  We had a plum duff boiling. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,393</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Barrington West Coast Prosp Party in 1959 Taylor Early Travelles 393]

>  <b>BAKER</b> ... `duff' - that's colonial for pudding, ma'am. <i>(1872,Xmas Cake in Four Quarters, A,Chap III</i>)

>  <b>TONKIN</b> On Christmas Day ... if they [Dunedin gaol prisoners] do not absolutely have plum pudding they have a plain of [sic] `duff', with a few currants in it, which to them is doubtless as acceptable as the richest of plum puddings <i>(1980,Dunedin Gaol,15</i>) [NOTES: >c1875 Otago Guardian in 1980 Tonkin Dunedin Gaol 15]

>  <b>DUNCAN</b> If we drew their attentions to ... the inevitable currant pudding on Sunday, their only answer was `Take the whole blooming `duff.' <i>(1888,Wakatipians,28</i>)

>  <b>BROOKES</b> The largest duff I have ever seen ... <i>(1892,Frontier Life,111</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> Anyone who can bake, make a decent `brownie' (as the plain cake is called) and a plum duff is fit for a shearers' cook. <i>(1913,Country Work,17</i>)

>  Duff: A very plain plum pudding, which was the invariable second course at Sunday dinner on stations. (G.P.W.) [A Northern English variant of,`dough' not recorded before the nine-teeth century.--A.W.] <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>HYDE</b> The duff boiled for three hours, and the rattle of tin plates was a newly invented song of the flax-cutters. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,48</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Take P.O.W. for example. Everyone had bashed up a duff for himself at one stage or another. <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,140</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Duff - `steamed pudding' <i>(1947,Wayleggo</i>) [NOTES: Gloss]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>(1949,High Country Days,195,1950 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Gloss.)]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Duff. A plain boiled pudding that was standard fare for shearers in the early days. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)

>  <b>FRANCIS</b> The [station] midday meal was more often a cut lunch eaten on the job. The main meal, `tea', was basically mutton, potatoes, onions, ... and, for afters, stewed dried fruit, plum duff or riceand prunes. <i>(1983,Wildlife Ranger,2</i>)

>  Who will race to Divvy Base and fetch a Christmas duff? <i>(Dec 22, 1941,NZEF Times,I/26 : 17</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a steamed pudding renowned of station cooks and packies.

>  <b>MONEY</b> The duff was made, the potatoes were boiled .. with plenty of sow thistles or Maori cabbage to give it a flavour ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,68</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> This is Sunday, a fact I'm made strongly to feel, By the presence of duff at our middle-day meal. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,9</i>)

>  The soil appears fairly good but here and there a few stones are visible, not very many, but scattered all through like the plums in some station duffs. <i>(1894,NZ Farmer,52</i>)

>  The packer, who is in charge of the packhorses, acts as camp cook. In the evening there is a huge meal, with a 'duff' if the packer has had time to build one. <i>(Aug 1, 1936,NZ Dairy Exporter,69</i>)

>  A good 'duff' is good indeed and a slice kept for lunch next day is a very sustaining food on the hill but a bad duff is terrible. <i>(Oct 27, 1938,NZ Farmer,10</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> .. the packie gets to work on a full-scale dinner of mutton, spuds, vegetables and duff ... <i>(1951,NZ High Country,32</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> Jack [the station cook] was soaking his feet in the dish that he mixed the duffs in! <i>(1990,Between the Rivers,192</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Jack was a wonderful cook and made the best station duff. [station duff] <i>(1966,Over the River,204</i>)

### duff (up)
 
 <i>v.</i> to make pregnant

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> He duffed one of the maids up at his old man's house and she died having an abortion, ... <i>(1974,Predicament,72,1981 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MAUGHAM</b> Still he had changed all that, moving into practice and duffing Elizabeth. <i>(1975,Good and Faithful Servants,7</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> When Ma-Mac finds out I've duffed her daughter there'll be a bloody massacre in the village! <i>(1981,Fish Factory,65</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> duff to make pregnant, developed from British phrase `up the duff', to be pregnant; eg `You know why he married her, don't you? Because he duffed her, of course.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,41</i>)

### duffer
 
 <i>adj.</i> . [goldmining]

>  <b>WAITE</b> Those first disappointed arrivals chose to call the expedition a duffer rush. <i>(1869,Discovery West Coast Goldfields,15</i>)

>  ... more than ordinarily rich the last six months seems to have been in `duffer rushes.' <i>(1870,AJHR,D-40-4</i>) [NOTES: Report Goldfields West Coast and Auckland]

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> ... you remember the duffer rush at Bruce Bay ... <i>(1875,Cobb's Box,8</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> Duffer rushes collapsed so quickly that storekeepers could find themselves in the back-of-beyond with a fully-stocked shanty and no sales to pay for the cost of packing-in and packing-out. <i>(1962,West Coast Gold Rushes,281,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> He was among the earliest at Hokitika, Grey, ... Jackson's Bay (a duffer's rush), etc. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,151</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  If the cows are themselves duffers, and are mated to a mongrel bull, the heifer calves cannot turn our very good ... <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,28</i>)

>  The following tables very well illustrate the value of culling out those cows that the system of testing reveals to be `duffers.' <i>(Jan 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,382</i>)

>  <b>POWDRELL</b> Practical farmers sometimes put two calves on to one duffer cow, or to a run cow for five or six weeks ... <i>Ibid.</i> 45 ... first he must weed out the `duffers' ... <i>(1920,Dairy Farming in NZ,42</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MAY</b> Another duffer of a different kind took place in July. Vague reports that the Commercial Banking Company and the New Zealand Banking Corporation had suspended payments caused a run on the unsuspecting Bank of New Zealand at Okarito township, and also at the Ross. <i>(1962,West Coast Gold Rushes,186,1967 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>PFAFF</b> I sunk a shaft on the Larrikin's Terrace there, but it proved a duffer. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,56</i>)

>  All hands.. are gone or going to theCreek.. which has often been pronounced"a duffer". Here.. a party got into a"pot-hole" and had got for two days' worksixteen ounces of gold. <i>(Mar 4, 1869,Westport Times,2</i>)

>  New rushes are spoken of every day, and the township which has sprung up is full of life and excitement caused by the crowds of diggers, packers, and storekeepers, all anxiously awaiting the latest intelligence to be off for "pastures new" on the Golden Grey. Affairs will not take their usual course if we do not have numbers coming back to the Hokitika, loudly declaring the whole thing a "duffer"and a "sell." <i>(Aug 3, 1865,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  They turned out a lot of "duffers". <i>(May 3, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  I think I should have preferred the old claim at the Thames, duffer as it was. <i>(Jan 1, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  The verdict of the miners ... regarding the Taieri rush is this, `that it is, as a gold-diggings, essentially a `duffer.' <i>(1864,Saturday Review,50</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... our claim proved a `duffer'. No less than fifteen shafts did we sink ..., but found a prospect of nothing sufficient to encourage us in working it. <i>Ibid.</i> 121 [Prospective diggers] had only arrived to find the whole thing what is called in digging parlance a `duffer' - i.e., that there was comparatively `nothing' to repay them for even the trouble of digging a single hole. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,99</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> He was working like a nigger, sinking duffers here and there. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,19</i>) [NOTES: [`Prospector']]

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> This visit was commemorated by miners who took up `Hochstetter' and `Haast' claims, which, as we have seen, unlike their namesakes, proved `duffers'. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,26 fn</i>)

>  duffer 1. Useless or stupid person. 2. In Australia and New Zealand, a worked-out orunprofitable mine. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),133,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> DUFFER: applied to a rush, a shaft, or a claim generally. A failure. Synonyms were `shicer',`stringer' or, in the case of a false rush, `store-keepers' rush'. A claim which was exhausted was said to be `duffered out'. At Ross in 1866 putting down a duffer shaft was termed `sinking for the Queen'. Duffers (Creek) is a common West Coast place-name. <i>(1962,West Coast Gold Rushes,526,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> A duffer: No gold. ...CLAIMS Worthless claims or ground were called schicers or duffers. Those yielding up to [pounds]3 per week per man were known as `tucker' claims; up to [pounds]8 per week were known as `wages' claims; above [pounds]8 were known as `pilers'; the richest of all were known as `Home-ward-bounders.' <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,200</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary of Mining Terms)]

>  <b>NOLAN</b> Duffer: a mine, claim or gold-rush which proves worthless. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,112</i>) [NOTES: (gloss)]

>  duffer ... 3. Austral. sl. a. a mine that proves unproductive. b. a person who steals cattle. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,344</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 232]]

>  ... the place was `rushed' by about 500 men, who speedily deserted it, and declared the Waitahuna to be a `duffer.' <i>(1863,AJHR,D6-3</i>) [NOTES: also in 1887 Handbook NZ Mines 5]

>  <b>HOPEFUL</b> If a man isn't `up to the mark' he is called `a regular shicer!' This phrase originated from the gold diggings. Supposing a man is digging, but without success, andhe therefore abandons his hole, it is called a `shicer' - that is, the ground was called a `shicer' or duffer, thus the origin. <i>(1887,Taken In,135</i>)

>  <b>BLAIR</b> He puddled and cradled, and digged all he knew / When the claim proved a regular duffer. <i>(1889,Lay of the Old Identities, The,48</i>)

>  It was soon found to be a "duffer" rush. <i>(Nov 11, 1924,Otago Witness,66</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> a washout

>  ... declaring the whole thing a `duffer' and a `sell.' <i>(1865,Evening Post,1/152/2</i>)

### duffer out
 
 <i>v.</i>



 
 <i>v.</i> of a mine, claim, to prove a failure; to become unproductive, peter out. [goldmining]

>  DIGGER'S FAREWELL... tells of 1874 whengold was "duffering out" on the coast,and hundreds of disheartened diggers weremaking their way back from the Grey Riverto the Palmer River in Australia. <i>(1955,Shanties by the Way</i>) [NOTES: Sleeve notes]

>  <b>O'REGAN</b> Your claim to duffered out at last. <i>(1894,Diggers' Story, The,15</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> A good bit of ground duffers out. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,59</i>)

>  ... since the house had been abandoned many years ago when the Owen reefs duffered out. <i>(1907,TrNZI,39/395</i>) [NOTES: (Townson)]

>  <b>MAY</b> While the energetic army of Waimea sluicers washed the terraces through the boxes and into the gully-beds, the Auckland Lead on the beach was all but duffered out. <i>(1962,West Coast Gold Rushes,198,1967 ed.</i>)

### duffer/duffer cow
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dry or unthrifty cow.

>  Possibly the reason why we have so many duffer cows in our herds is partly to be found in our lack of opportunity to think. <i>(Jun 1910,NZ Farmer,556</i>)

>  The following tables very well illustrate the value of culling out those cows that the system of testing reveals to be "duffers." <i>(May 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,382</i>)

>  It was necessary to warn the inexperienced buyer who, guided by the limitations of his pocket, might secure what on present values, was a medium or low-priced animal, and finding that he had bought a 'duffer' would drop the breed. <i>(Jul 3, 1920,Hawera Star,7</i>)

### duffered
 
 <i>adj.</i> exhausted; ended.

>  <b>O'REGAN</b> Life's claim is almost duffered. <i>(1894,Voices of Wave and Tree,10</i>)

### duffered out
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>HEINZ</b> Duffered out: All the gold has been mined. <i>(1947,Bright Fine Gold,200</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> DUFFER: applied to a rush, a shaft, or a claim generally. A failure. Synonyms were `shicer', `stringer' or, in the case of a false rush, `store-keepers' rush'. A claim which was exhausted was said to be `duffered out'. At Ross in 1866 putting down a duffer shaft was termed `sinking for the Queen'. Duffers (Creek) is a common West Coast place-name. <i>(1962,West Coast Gold Rushes,526,1967 ed.</i>)

### duffing
 
 <i>adj.</i> of a claim, find etc., proving unproductive. [goldmining] (See [also duffer aj](../A#also-duffer-aj))

>  <b>'H'</b> Then came the thought of how the boys would chaff him about this `duffing claim.' ... There is no crime so great as to make a `duffing find' intentionally. <i>(1898,Grain of Gold, The,39</i>)

### duffing find
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [duffing](dou-dyn.md#duffing))

>  <b>"H"</b> There is no crime so great as to make a "duffing find"<i> intentionally.</i> <i>(1898,Grain of Gold, The,39</i>)

### dug-out
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAYTON</b> As they are all what is known as "dugouts", or of one solid trunk of "totara", it may be imagined that the weight is very considerable. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,245</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> I counted not less than twenty-four of us ... the `dug-out' had to carry. [fn] `Dug-out' is the name of these canoes among the backwoodsmen of North America. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),293</i>)

### duggles
 
 <i>n.</i> the marbles you put in the centre of the ring, =dingles. Compare dubs. [nf dicts] [?is this supposed to be diggles or dingles?: [(marble players') ca1957-63 Horowhenua p.c. ?]

### duke
 
 <i>n.</i> [(child marble-players')]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

### Duke of Yorks
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) makeshift socks or boot-liners [used mainly by bachelors and bushmen in the 1800s]. [cf. Prince Alberts, Prince Edwards]

>  <b>WILSON</b> They [bushmen] did not wear socks: they cut an old tent into squares and pushed their feet, with the squares around them, into boots - they called these 'Duke of Yorks' and said that changing them daily they protected the feet better than socks. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,6</i>)

### dum
 
 <i>v.</i> to face [(criminals')]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> DUM (v) (?) Face. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)

### dumar
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Two of us dumared out together and every time one of us moved upwards the rope would start to move up and down for the other. <i>(Jun 18, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

### dumfarthing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He was told he could not own a farm ... he closed it ... And who benefited? Not a soul. But the regulations were carried out to the uttermost dumfarthing. <i>(Nov 1978,Public Service Journal,Vol 65/No.10 14/1</i>)

### dummied
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land ] owned in the name of one person but farmed by another. [or (in citation below) passive of verb dummy?]

>  <b>CHANDLER</b> No doubt many of these areas shortly became 'dummied' by a more successful neighbour getting possession of the area of a less successful one; the latter becoming an employee of the former, though ostensibly working his own block. <i>(1996,Land of the Mountain and the Flood,42</i>)

### dummy
 
 <i>n.</i> solitary confinement [Compare boob, cooler, digger. ca1941 Prison slang: Riverside Community]

>  Down below.. is the solitary block. This is the dreaded "dummy" where the prison J.P. sends recalcitrants for a spell on bread and water. <i>(Jan 15, 1947,NZ Observer,9</i>)

>  He [sc. Paddy Webb] described the punishment known as "dummy" - solitary confinement in a dark cell on hard rations. He had seen men go into "dummy" in fairly good health and come out physical wrecks. In "dummy" they got three small blankets, and had to sleep on the floor. <i>(Jul 31, 1917,Press, The,8</i>)

>  Mr Webb added that if he had been found out in this dishonest behaviour, he would have been put in the 'dummy' - solitary confinement. <i>(Aug 14, 1917,Wanganui Herald,4</i>)

>  The young prisoner [in the Terrace Gaol] got so bad with boils that he was unable to work, and was placed in `The Dummy,' a building which the prisoner described to `Truth' as an old chicken house. Whilst in `The Dummy' he received no attention whatever, except that his meals were brought to him. <i>(Jun 5, 1909,NZ Truth,206/5/5</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> He was taken before the Governor and sentenced to three days in the Dummy. It is always dusk in the Dummy, which lies underground. ... At six in the morning the door is unlocked, and the prisoner gets his clothes, all except boots. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,62</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> The aggressor in this case was promptly led off and incarcerated in the `dummy' [for attacking a warder]. <i>Ibid.</i> 109 Someone else had seen a bloodstained towel coming from the `dummy'. <i>(1952,In Prison,107</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> When one of the prisoners was put in the dummy for misbehaviour, Jim O'Brien ... wanted to bring the jail out on strike. <i>(1965,Rhetoric at the Red Dawn,127</i>)

>  <b>McKENZIE</b> The punishment block, known as the `pound' or the `dummy', was a prison within the [Mt Eden] prison. In the basement, through a steel grille and a wooden door, it was a dimly lit and silent annexe leading at its far end to the tiny execution yard. <i>Ibid.</i> 96 dummy punishment block. See pound and back <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,14</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Dummy. (n) Detention unit. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)

>  Though some of the [prison] conditions ... have changed - in that prisons have become more hygienic, bread and water is no longer the basic diet in dummy, nor is the Bible the only punishment book - ... the isolation of solitary confinement still has the whiff of the oubliette. <i>(Oct 13, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> dummy punishment cell in prison. - practice of taking up land allegedly for yourself, but onbehalf of another, usually a friend or relative acting for a squatter. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,41</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(Mount Eden prison).]

>  They took him down to the dummy yard where the flogging framework was set up and, Joe said, half the senior staff in The Hill was there. <i>(Nov 1950,Here & Now,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> In NZ from the time land laws were applied, one paid to acquire the title to a parcel of Crown land on behalf of another who was not legally entitled to do so; one who acquires title to land in name only, on behalf of another.

>  Mr. Browning held the shares in question.. and transferred them to Mr. Martin Sholl, sharebroker, who transferred them to William Lestrange, a well-known "dummy." <i>(Apr 26, 1873,Auckland Weekly News</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> He can go and be a Dummy on the Otago runs. <i>(1884,Southern Parables,17</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> And yet the talk is evermore, `The people want the land!'/ .../ The greedy few will clamour loud and clamour to the end;/ A dummy grabbing what he can is not the people's friend./ And Heaven's curse is on him still in all his schemes for gain;/ ... <i>Ibid.</i> 58 It's the pig-shooting dummy I've `set',/ And I'm willing to say it again. <i>(1897,Southern Parables,44</i>) [NOTES: (`Arlinton')]

>  Dummy, n. in the early days of settlement, a person who put in a claim for land for the use ofanother. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  Dummy: A man who takes a run in his name for the benefit of another; hence to dummy and dummyism. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  Dummy. 5. In Australia and New Zealand, a man who selects land on behalf of another. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),133,8th ed.</i>)

>  dummy n. third person or medium at a sale of Crown land, who, swearing he is selecting land for himself, buys it, and, when the deal is completed drops out, allowing the second party to aquire the land for a fee. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> It would appear that a syndicate of directors and senior officials of the Farmers' Coop Association were the prime movers in the purchase [in 1917] and that the Lockheads were [114] really `dummies' whose initial contributions towards the purchase [of the station] were soon lost. <i>(1969,Molesworth,113</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog-training device consisting of a sheepskin draped over a wire netting frame on a pulley line.

>  <b>WHYTE</b> a dummy is a sheepskin draped over a wire netting frame on a pulley line. <i>(1927,Sheep-Dog, The,13</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> A form of dummy sheep, constructed from a 'benzine box' upon which pram wheels and a sheepskin are attached - shaping it like a sheep. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,40</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a partition separating two cows in the double bail of a milking shed.

>  All that is required is a water-tap fitted in front of the 'dummy' with rubber tubing attached long enough to reach to the cow's udder. <i>(Jun 20, 1938,NZ Journal of Agriculture,452</i>)

>  A modification of the dummy and race has come into favour in certain districts during recent years. <i>(Jun 15, 1939,NZ Journal of Agriculture,489</i>)

>  The use of galvanised iron piping or iron piping for erection of internal dummies and partitions. <i>(Apr 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,378</i>)

>  The bails and dummies in this shed are all clear of the floor, suspended from the ceiling. <i>(Apr 26, 1951,NZ Farmer,6</i>)

>  The McGoughans have mounted these units in pairs within the bale [sic] dummies. Small canvas curtains slightly forward of the units are interesting additions. <i>(Nov 13, 1975,NZ Farmer,15</i>)

>  I recall the early sheds with only head bales [sic] and the walk-through with the dummy bale [sic] between cows. [dummy bail] <i>(Aug 10, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,Supp 14</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to select Crown land as a dummy. [Hist.]

>  <b>REEVES</b> The attempts often ingeniously made to evade these [land] restrictions by getting the land in the name of relatives, servants, or agents are called `dummyism', and may be punished by imprisonment - never inflicted - by fines, and by forfeiture of the land `dummied.' <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,356</i>)

>  Dummy: A man who takes a run in his name for the benefit of another; hence to dummy and dummyism. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>378/3</b> The use of galvanised iron piping or iron piping for erection of internal dummies and partitions. <i>(Apr 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture</i>)

>  (caption) A small dairy with suspended dummy and bucket milking machine for one cow. <i>(May 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,479</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> fishing rig [(?game fishermen's)]

>  <b>GREY</b> The place looked fishy, and here the boatmen began trolling with hand-lines for bait. They used a small gig, dark in color, shaped like a canoe, which they called a dummy. I rigged up a light tackle and put over a spoon, ... <i>(1926,Angler's Eldorado,33</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to purchase land in name only for another to use, in order that a holding can be legally extended.

>  The Dummying Question. At the dummyism inquiry today ... <i>(Jun 8, 1883,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  If two or three of a family select adjoining blocks a ring fence can be run around the whole allotment. One of the first and most obvious objectives is that the system would afford an easy method of dummyism. Members of a family might be used to "dummy" for their rich relatives. <i>(Aug 6, 1900,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  Like all liberal provisions, however, they soon opened the way to land sharks who simply dummied the sections until they could find a purchaser at a greatly advanced price, and then applied for a transfer. <i>(Dec 1904,NZ Farmer,1006</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a land purchaser who buys land for the convenience of another to accumulate.

>  Dummies were employed to occupy the land for a few years, and as they were permitted to transfer their leases after a three years' residence ... <i>(Jun 16, 1880,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  <b>MCKEE WRIGHT</b> The greedy few will clamour land and clamour to the end; A dummy grabbing what he can is not the people's friend. <i>(1898,Station Ballads,46</i>)

>  <b>MCKEE WRIGHT</b> Dummy - an individual masquerading as the owner of land usually in the interest of a large run-holder. <i>(1898,Station Ballads,63</i>)

>  In the latter [Hatuma situation] it is well-known that at least a dozen holders are dummies for a particular company but in their case they are holding on to the land and working it <i>(Dec 1904,NZ Farmer,1006</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> He'd think nothing of snooping into the cell of a man doing his dummy ... <i>Ibid.</i> 159Sometimes, during the strain of that long dummy I'd burst out in a stream of curses at a screw ... <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,57</i>)

### dummy cell
 
 <i>n.</i> see dummy 2

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> But the screws weren't taking any chances. When a three-striper came down they locked Barney up in a dummy cell and carted Alec up to the hospital. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,112</i>)

### dummy cow
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high-consuming, low-producing dairy cow.

>  If you cannot afford to replace your dummy cows you can at least feed your good cows better by getting rid of cows that are losing you money every day. <i>(Nov 27, 1920,Hawera Star,9</i>)

### dummying
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  <b>PINNEY</b> The upper Ahuriri and the hills ... were now legally tenanted. Any suggestion of dummying is no more <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,65</i>)

### dummyism
 
 <i>n.</i> the action or process of dummying [Hist. See cit.dummy v.]

>  I accused the honourable member of dummyism; I really mean't gridironing. <i>(Aug 21, 1890,Otago Witness,13</i>)

>  I accused the honourable gentleman of dummyism; I really meant gridironing. <i>(1890,NZ Parliamentary Debates,68/411</i>)

>  He said that he had not meant "dummyism," and that he had made a mistake, for he intended "gridironing," and wished that word in its place. <i>(1890,NZ Parliamentary Debates,68/413</i>)

>  [Hdg] The Alleged Dummyism Cases at Macraes. <i>(Jun 12, 1890,Otago Witness,19</i>)

>  <b>BAGNALL</b> I had no idea that the word dummyism was to be connected with my name., The reporter for the <i>Evening Post</i> hurrying up the stairs past me and asking whether the "Dummyism" enquiry had begun was the first intimation I received of the serious nature of this business. <i>(1976,Wairarapa : An Historical Excursion,286</i>) [NOTES: Quote from about 1890]

>  <b>REEVES</b> The Kakanui area was not entirely a stranger to a third practice of the large landholder, dummyism, that is land acquisition under cover of different names. It was ... nowhere in New Zealand practised with the same effrontery as in Australia. <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,356</i>)

>  <b>SCOTTER</b> The Kakanui area was not entirely a stranger to a third practice of the large landholder, dummyism, that is land acquisition under cover of different names. It was ... nowhere in New Zealand practised with the same effrontery as in Australia. <i>(1984,Run, Estate and Farm,14</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Where leaseholders were not permitted to `select' land, or where conditions of residence were enforced (?), `dummyism' was rife-that is, a leaseholder would pay some `swaggie' to act as the ostensible selector and fufil the requirements for him. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,156</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> ... as a member of the Otago Wastelands Board from 1878, he opposed `Dummyism' and similar abuses. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/235</i>)

>  dummyism n. the practice of acquiring land through a dummy, by misrepresentation. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

>  The Committee on Alleged Dummyism have brought up their report. <i>(Sep 3, 1883,Illustrated NZ News,10</i>)

>  The first case of alleged dummyism was ventilated at the Land Board on Wednesday. <i>(Dec 12, 1890,NZ Mail,24</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the use of others to buy land for oneself. [an unofficial situation of the 1800s and early 1900s where farmers used other purchasers to help them acquire land.]

>  The Otago Daily Times suggests that a Royal Commission should be appointed to investigate charges of dummyism in respect to land recently leased. <i>(Mar 9, 1883,Hawera Star,9</i>)

>  At the dummyism inquiry today, one man, named Kenneth Williamson, a shepherd in the employ of Messrs Gellibrand and Smith, and who was a purchaser at the land sale, said he had been "sounded" as to becoming a purchaser, and had been given to understand that unless he took up a particular section he must shift elsewhere. <i>(Jun 8, 1883,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  A gross case of dummyism is said to exist in Hawke's Bay and steps will probably be taken to bring it before the public. <i>(Dec 1892,NZ Farmer,493</i>)

>  If two or three families select adjoining blocks, a ring fence can be run around the whole allotment. One of the first and most obvious objectives is that the system would afford an easy method of dummyism. Members of a family might be used to "dummy" for their rich relatives. <i>(Aug 6, 1900,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  When they saw 420 applicants for one section, a new aspect of dummyism was coming into vogue. <i>(Mar 13, 1907,Hawera Star,5</i>)

>  The Otago Land Board has been inquiring into a case of alleged dummyism in respect of a run in the Tiger Hill district. <i>(Apr 1908,NZ Farmer,328</i>)

>  Dummy - a man who takes a run in his name for the benefit of another; hence to d[ummy] and dummyism. <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

### dump
 
 <i>n.</i> the rubbish tip

>  <b>RENeE</b> Usually on Sunday afternoons we're filling the car and its boot with garden rubbish because we're going to the dump, or we're actually going to the dump, or we're gardening because next weekend we are planning to go to the dump. <i>(1998,Yin & Tonic,10</i>)

>  <b>CRANNA</b> There are only four roads in and out of Puapiro - five if you include the road to the dump. <i>(1989,Visitors,107</i>)

>  Earlier this year we touched briefly on rubbish tips, with reference to a Board of Health survey into their usefulness... Correspondent John Ormond of Stratford unhesitatingly nominates Pahiatua's tip as the mnost scenically sited in the country. <i>(Dec 3, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),144</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> fecal excretion [?US]

>  I should point out that `ralph' is an onomatopoeia on vomiting, while a 'dump' refers to fecal excretion. A `Ngauruhoe special' is apparently a particularly eruptive `dump' which is typically associated with drinking Australian beer. <i>(1985,Sites,11/15</i>) [NOTES: (Hodges `Drinking Vernacular')]



 
 <i>v.</i> [Sunday Times letter suggests these are NZ uses of the word]

>  According to our TV newsreaders, snow no longer falls, it dumps and Jenny Shipley wasn't replaced by Bill English, she was dumped and the Bangladesh cricket team weren't beaten by the Black Caps - they were dumped. <i>(Jan 13, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a double-dumped bale of wool

>  <b>DAVIS</b> Below, our wharf gang was busy attaching the short ropes called snotters to a score of dumps that had just been tipped off the back of a truck. <i>Ibid.</i> 105 On wool, when three men work elbow to elbow on seven-hundred-pound dumps and one slip means a hook through your mate's hand... <i>(1964,Watersiders,42</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to press two normal bales of wool into the size of one.

>  Practically all wool is dumped prior to shipping, that is, the putting together of two bales into one and securing them by three strong hoop-iron bands. <i>(1919,Wool in NZ,32</i>)

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> The cleanest and best wool was dumped in the grease on the station and taken to Blenheim for shipment to London. <i>(1969,Molesworth,103</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> This press I was told was quite capable of dumping, i.e. pressing two normal bales of wool into the size of one. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,17</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wool bin in a woolshed.

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> Manu and I looked at each other simultaneously from the depths of two dumps of loose wool- those most luxurious of armchairs in which we had been sprawled with only our legs and the tops of our hats showing. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,50</i>)

### dump press
 
 <i>n.</i> a powerful hydraulic press used for dumping wool-bales. [also dumping press (woolstore) ca1949 Levin and Co.'s Wellington woolstore. The press was called a dumping press, hydraulically driven, and powered by a gas engine.] (See [also dumping press](../A#also-dumping-press))

>  It was customary, as at Waipiro Bay ..., to have a wool store and a dump press near the beach. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,10</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wool press, usually communal, situated near a coastal port.

>  It appears that in New Zealand a 10 to 20 per cent increase in density of single dumped bales .. could be obtained using existing dump presses with no more than one extra wire. <i>(Dec 1964,NZ Journal of Agriculture,466</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> It was customary, as at Waipiro Bay, Pourere, Burnview, Akitio, Castlepoint and White Rock to have a wool store and a dump press near the beach. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,10</i>)

### dumped
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>WHEELER</b> The dumped wool was taken by bullock wagon into the surf, transferred to surfboats, and rowed out to the ... steamer lying offshore. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,10</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of fleecewool] compressed and wrapped with wire.

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> The waggoners slept on the dumped wool under the waggon cover and there was very little room. Each wagon carried about thirty bales dumped. <i>(1969,Man of England Now,104</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> The dumped wool was taken by bullock wagon into the surf ... <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,10</i>)

### dumper
 
 <i>n.</i> wool-shed or wool-store hand who dumps wool. [(woolstore) ca1949 Used in Levin and Co.'s Wellington woolstore.]

>  [?-modn dump; or dump v1] A number of shearers and dumpers having secured a fiddler and fifer, paraded the town with green ribbon round their hats. <i>(Jan 18, 1868,Marlborough Express,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [[Not recorded in NZ.]]



 
 <i>n.</i> racecourse detective [(criminals')]

>  I recognise this bloke as a dumper, so I take it on the toe out the gate and up the frog and toad. dumper ... racecourse detective <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)

### dumping
 
 <i>n.</i> [1955 citation is of verb to dump]

>  <b>LYON</b> When shearers are striving for a tally, or a big output of shorn sheep, dumping is the act of pushing the lamb's head downward when seated on the boards, in such a way that the lamb'slungs are pushed down on his diaphragm causing great agony and often fatal injury ... . The idea is that by dumping the sides of the lamb are flattened and permit a larger flat surface of fleece to be exposed to the shears. <i>(1952,Faring South,194</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> There are several known styles of shearing. They range from the old blade method of stretching the sheep and shearing more round and round, to the modern method of dumping or crimping the sheep and following the blows right through more along the body of the sheep; ... <i>(1955,Wool Away!,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the process of compressing two bales of wool into the size of one. [1885 citation is of verb to dump?]

>  The Wanganui Wool Dumpers Partnership hadconcluded its first financial year ofoperations... The stockpile of unsoldwools in store will contribute to thedumping trade as it is sold. <i>(1991,Ann. Rep. Farmers' Co-op,5</i>)

>  [Hdg] The Wool-Dumping Charges... That the present charge for dumping wool be reduced at once from 2s to 1s 9d per bale. <i>(Nov 28, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> The dry wool was pressed at the scour and the bales taken to the woolshed for dumping ready for shipment. <i>(1969,Molesworth,103</i>) [NOTES: c1907-1914 Mowat diary in 1969 McCaskill Molesworth 103]

>  <b>PERRY</b> It is a mistake to think that seed (hatiwai[sic] or bidi-bidi) in wool is crushed to dust in the dumping, ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,138</i>)

>  A powerful hydraulic press forces two bales into the space formerly occupied by one, and they are bound with steel bands, or wires. (This process is called `double dumping' or `dumping'.) <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,67</i>)

### dumping press
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wool press, usually communal, situated near a port.

>  The Harbour Board has an up-to-date wool-dumping press and hydraulic pumps. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,42</i>)

### dumping shed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Wool waggons are daily to be seen onalmost every road bringing down wool fromthe various farms and station to thedumping sheds.. and from thence to thewool ships which roll at anchor in theBay. <i>(Dec 28, 1899,NZ Times,2</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a store for wool [often containing a dump press.]

>  <b>TAIT</b> The wool dumping shed at Waipiro Bay. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,8</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> The wool dumping shed. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,51</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The big "dumping" shed - some 3,000 square feet - which overlooks the beach a mile or so below the mouth of the river is still standing ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,88</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> There was a dumping shed on the beach from where wool was loaded on the wagons and then taken out to the schooner ... <i>(1973,Petticoat Pioneers,I 213</i>)

>  <b>FEARON</b> A wool dump shed was built on the foreshore at Flat Point for the bales to be stored awaiting for the arrival of a steamer. <i>(1980,Te Wharau,13</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> We had twelve pack saddles which belonged to Hore Hore and also used Wrey's and mine so with fourteen horses we set out to pack the wool out to the dumping shed. [dump shed] <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,150</i>)

### dumping-plant
 
 <i>n.</i> [(wool)]

>  A dumping-plant has been erected by the Harbour Board; and last year (1893) 5,703 bales of wool ... were shipped. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,494</i>)

### dumpter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Pay rise for drivers of dumpters [Headline] The Government Railways Industrial Tribunal hasgranted a rise of 1 1/2d and hour, bringing the rate to 8/5 1/2, for dumpter drivers employed by the New Zealand Railways. The award was made in a reserved decision on a claim by the Amalgamated Society of Railway Servants for dumpter operators to be included with plant operators (employees with approved certificates) and paid at the rate of 8/8 1/2 and hour. Dumpter operators have been paid as lorry drivers at 8/4 per hour. (wool) <i>(Nov 10, 1965,Dominion,3</i>)

### dumpy
 
 <i>n.</i> [OBS.]

>  The game of Dumpy `was played on a space about the size of a tennis court and divided into three sections. There were six (often more) players, two on each section. Each player had a whipand each pair had to stick to its own section. When the top was whipped over the back line, that gave a point to that team' Taranaki, 1880). <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,166</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an outdoor privy

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> ... a flush toilet that would not have disgraced any suburban home. `Thank heaven for that,' I said. `I had visions of tramping some lonely path to an old dumpty, perched over a big hole full of spiders and wiggly things. <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,101</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> The lavatory, or `dumpy' as we called it, was the usual enclosure about a deep hole, with a railway-red seat. <i>(1982,To the Is-Land,29</i>)

### Dunedin Sound
 
 <i>n.</i> A characteristic musical sound emphasising jangly guitar sounds, said to distinguish Dunedin pop groups from other New Zealand bands.

>  The Dunedin Sound. For many who made the music, it has been <i>enough already</i> for some time now. <i>(Oct 7, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  But the "Dunedin Sound" as such didn't really exist. That was a convenient tag that made it easy for people to lump us atogether and pretend there was a common sound, but right from the start the bands were different. <i>(Apr 1998,North & South,80</i>)

>  Some of the most memorable are TheChills, whose frosty guitar jangle summedup the label's "Dunedin Sound". <i>(Mar 25, 1996,Salient,30</i>)

>  [Hdg] Venerable New Zealand label Flying Nun has grown a new wing. The label which brought us the Dunedin sound has unveiled two new signings from... Auckland. <i>(Sep 30, 1999,Evening Post,23</i>)

>  The book is a personal reminiscence from the inside of "Sneaky Feelings and the Dunedin Sound." as it says in its subtitle. <i>(Oct 24, 1999,Sunday Star Times,F2</i>)

>  Matthew Bannister was in a band called Sneaky Feelings, formed in the early 80s and part of the so-called "Dunedin Sound." <i>(Dec 10, 1999,Dominion,5</i>)

>  Then the music of the Dunedin Sounds bands leaked south. <i>(Feb 5, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>) [NOTES: check whether "Sound" or "Sounds"]

>  The so-called "Dunedin sound: was deliberately glamourless, but Carter was the closest it came to producing a star, a pin-up, a ... teen idol. <i>(Mar 31, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)

>  "Do you consider that there is such a thing as a distinctive "New Zealand" sound?"... No, but I think there's a "Dunedin Sound"." <i>(Dec 15, 1981,In Touch (Wellington),6</i>)

>  "I spent about two hours.. discussing.. the Dunedin sound and Hunters and Collectors, things like that." <i>(Aug 1981,Rip It Up (Auckland),96/12</i>)

>  On musical terms, 'Dunedin Sound on 45' was a good idea which could have been done better or worse. <i>(Aug 1981,Rip It Up (Auckland),98/32</i>) [NOTES: Note apparent date anomaly wwith previous citation, needs checking]

>  I'm worried that the "Dunedin Sound" will begin to parody itself very soon. <i>(May 17, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),68</i>)

>  <b>DIX</b> Four Dunedin bands - the Chills, Sneaky feelings, Stones and Verlaines - contributed three songs each... The development of these four groups was the yardstick by which outsiders would follow the growth of the 'Dunedin Sound'. <i>(1988,Stranded in Paradise,287</i>)

>  Empty white-sand beaches, the very good university and the famous "Dunedin Sound" pub music attract people too. <i>(Nov 1991,North & South,74</i>)

>  Cited by many as the headlining band of a "new Dunedin Sound", Hellzapoppin' has entered the national chart at no 24. <i>(Apr 13, 1992,Salient,12</i>)

### Dunedin Sounders
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [Dunedin Sound](dou-dyn.md#dunedin-sound))

>  Which would be more camps than the Dunedin Sounders had when they confronted the industry nearly 20 years ago. <i>(Aug 12, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

### Dunedinite
 
 <i>n.</i> one born or resident in Dunedin

>  <b>W.T.SMITH</b> ... the 11 of All-England and the 22 of Otago, and as a matter of course the Dunedinites were beat. <i>(Feb 9, 1864,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Feb 9 W.T.Smith's Diary (South Otago) Hocken Library MS (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>INGLIS</b> The Dunedinites ... have rather arrogated to themselves the reputation of being preternaturally knowing, ... <i>(1887,Our NZ Cousins,224</i>)

>  <b>DAVIDSON</b> It was sometime in the sixties, when young Dunedinites were full of blood and deviltry ... <i>(1889,Stories of NZ Life,11</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> I have wandered some way from the amusements of the Dunedinites in the 'sixties. <i>(1925,Random Recollections,18</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> In early Dunedin large shoals of barracouta were easily caught and sold at sixpence or less for a fish ... No wonder the old Dunedinite of a former generation referred to them, those wide-jawed and projecting toothed long fish, as `Old Identities'. <i>(1952,Faring South,131</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> They found the evening a little chilly, and even the Dunedinite found the warm glow acceptable <i>(1961,North Cape to Bluff</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> I see him as a Dunedinite. <i>(1985,Horse,122</i>) [NOTES: 1967 Baxter The Man on the Horse in 1985 Horse 122]

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> Dunedinites think of Auckland as another country. Aucklanders advise South-bound trippers to take enough overcoats ... for the South Pole. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,33</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> I didn't ask the Dunedinites if they knew what was playing at the Court in Christchurch. <i>(1985,G'day Country,173</i>)

>  <b>SLIGO</b> Each brick ... was dense with the thickness of the Dunedinites' lives. <i>(1987,Final Things,172</i>)

>  A Dunedinite, Michael Abbott, is pitting himself against the worst that the wilds can throw at him ... <i>(Feb 14, 1989,Press, The,6</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> There is nothing, however, in the natural situation or general aspect of the townitself which recalls the "Modern Athens," although a Dunedinite might well exclaimwith Scott " mine own romantic town." <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,13</i>)

>  There is a modicum of truth in the Dunedinite's facetiousness. <i>(Mar 20, 1926,NZ Observer,2</i>)

>  The Dunedinites were supposed to attack at daylight, which was 5 a.m., but they didn't turn up till 5.30 a.m. <i>(Dec 1916,Roll Call,30</i>)

>  Flying Nun'ophiles and Dunedinites can rattle on at you for hours about how wonderful The Puddle are. <i>(Feb 27, 1993,Evening Post,25</i>)

>  An enraged Dunedinite is a fearsome creature to behold and is capable of inflicting severe damage. Over 100,000 of these beasts in a stampede would strike a chill into the bravest heart. <i>(Aug 13, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  You Dunedinites are well off; you can go on every day and see it [sc. the Exhibition]. <i>(Feb 27, 1890,Otago Witness,42</i>)

>  The four Dunedinites arrived just when the fish had gone off the feed. <i>(Dec 21, 1920,Otago Witness,41</i>)

>  The tact of the Dunedinite was not on a par with that of the world's most famous diplomats. <i>(May 21, 1910,NZ Truth,3</i>)

>  [Hdg] Dunedinite in Wales. <i>(May 4, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,69</i>)

>  The Dunedinites are gearing up to see Otago thump North Harbour. <i>(Sep 27, 1992,Dominion Sunday Times,3</i>)

### Dunedinitis
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Scottish settlement's contribution is"Fergy Forester", and he never let's usforget that fact. He suffering"Dunedinitis". <i>(Oct 16, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/4 : 83</i>)

### dung-duster
 
 <i>n.</i> a topdressing pilot

>  <b>GREEN</b> "Chris" Christoffells, the engineer at Rotorua, painted a typically English pub sign, <i>The Dung Duster's Arms</i>", and this hung in a prominent place behind the bar. <i>Ibid</i> 315 In the days when topdressing meant tiger moths many farmers were critical of how the fertiliser spread on the ground. They saw the superphosphate dust fall from the aircraft but it quickly settled on the grass and vanished from sight. A few pilots were astute enough to see the sprinkling of superphosphate on the cow dung in the paddocks. The farmers were called over to admire the dusting. So naturally topdressing pilots became "dung busters." <i>(1983,Topdressers, The,141</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an aerial top-dressing pilot.

>  <b>GEELEN</b> After each long and dusty day the James topdressing pilots returned to Rotorua aerodrome and quenched their thirst at the old Geyser Hotel, Whakarewarewa. "Chris" Christoffels, the engineer at Rotorua, painted a typically English pub sign, The Dung Duster's Arms, and this hung in a prominent place behind the bar. <i>(1983,Topdressers, The,141</i>)

>  <b>GEELEN</b> Dung Dusters Delight, with the compliments of James Aviation Limited. [miniature bottle of whisky presented to special customers] <i>(1983,Topdressers, The,309</i>)

>  <b>GEELEN</b> A few pilots were astute enough to see the sprinkling of superphosphate on the cow dung in the paddocks. The farmers were called over to admire the dusting. So naturally topdressing pilots became "dung dusters." <i>(1983,Topdressers, The,315</i>)

### dung-dusting
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) aerial topdressing

>  <b>BOOTH</b> They started [aerial work] with just this top-dressing - dung-dusting, the boys call it - ... <i>(1961,Long Night,109</i>)

### dung-out
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a clearance or clear-out

>  More intriguingly, this dungout suggests panic on the part of the producers - though about what, who could say? <i>(Dec 2, 2000,Dominion,25</i>)

>  Well, her famous dung-out speech, in which she urged tired old hands to make way for new talent, was code for a leadership spill if anyone was game. <i>(Oct 20, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  This [party] list is not a wholesaledungout, but rather a subtle revolution. <i>(Jun 16, 1996,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  Have a dungout of your advisers in a furtive way, issuing euphemistic explanations for getting rid of people, like 'disestablishing the position' and 'restructuring'. <i>(Dec 9, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  Even the historic dungout of centre-right councillors from the Auckland City Council is being read in two totally contradictory ways. <i>(Oct 23, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  We gave the house a real good dung out, but the children were still waking up. <i>(Nov 3, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,4</i>)

>  This means that, given half a chance, other ministers will have wholesale dung-outs of their least popular portfolio responsibilities. <i>(Aug 9, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  The Treaty of Waitangi is one of the biggest such barrels, with the slowest-moving fish - as evenMaori Affairs Minister Parekura Horomia seemed ready to concede yesterday, as Parliament considered his dung-out of the Waitangi Fisheries Commission. <i>(Aug 24, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  He'd look a big meanie, if he had an honest dung-out. <i>(Jan 26, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  Mr Lange is far from being alone inwishing for a dungout of those who aremore "centre" than "left". <i>(Jun 25, 1995,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

### dung-staining
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [pen-staining] the marking of wool by dags and dung prior to shearing.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Dung-staining of wool can be kept to a minimum by ordinary good management. Dung-staining of wool during yarding and penning for shearing is all too common. <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,94</i>)

### dunger
 
 <i>n.</i> [usually old dunger, aka dunga. This headword buildings, but see also dunger vehicles and machines]

>  She says memories born in the Ohariu house will remain including spending her first six months in what she describes as a "dunga", heavily pregnant up a ladder working on their "small house in a paddock". <i>(Mar 24, 2003,Norwester,2</i>)

>  Jarvis remembers the days when the local real estate agents would pop in for a drink after work and they'd crow, "I sold another old dunga today. My shout!"... Some of the old dungas must have been virtually afloat. <i>(Oct 29, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  <b>Richard Long</b> That would have added to the simmering resentment about his other Christchurch home truths: that it was "blindingly obvious" that some parts of the city were unrepairable and that some of the "old dunger" buildings needed to be demolished so the city centre could be rebuilt. There are some Cantabrians who, irrespective of cost and long-term safety standards, would painstakingly rebuild the old dungers brick by brick. <i>(Jun 28, 2011,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  "Quite frankly, people have died in the last earthquake trying to save old buildings. We're not going to do that any more. My absolutely strong position is that the old dungas, no matter what their connection, are going under the hammer." <i>(Mar 1, 2011,Dominion Post,A2</i>) [NOTES: Gerry Brownlee re Chch earthquake]

>  Newtown, Berhampore and Island Bay are the worst spots for old "dungers". <i>(Nov 18, 1992,Contact (Wgtn),1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> something old or unreliable, esp. an old car; something worn out or not of the first class; often collocated old dunger. [aka dunga; this headword vehicles and machines; see also old dunger buildings]

>  Judge P.J. Bate said Rawhiti might have made an initial mistake in purchasing a car which in common parlance might be called a `dunger', but he had gone about things in the wrong way. <i>(Mar 24, 1982,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  And Crump has written another book, using his `old dunger' of a typewriter which he describes as `a real antique' ... <i>(Nov 30, 1983,NZ Truth,23</i>)

>  `Those cars, doesn't matter whether its a brand new Mercedes, or an old dunger, are all somebody's pride and joy. <i>(Apr 7, 1983,Dominion,7</i>)

>  <b>D. HILDRETH</b> Very surprised at your not having heard the word `dunger', which has been familiar to me since secondary school - 15 `odd' years [sc. since 1968]. In my experience it's usually used of a car which is old, battered and, I think importantly, noisy. <i>(Oct 1983,Personal Communication</i>)

>  dunger: an old bicycle (pron. as in dung er); a common South Island term, almost of endearment, for one's bicycle <i>(1981,Rangiora High School 1884-1984</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> dunga, dunger trrouble, in phr. in the dunga; perhaps extension of the word `dung'. - dilapidated object, such as house, car or contraption, from engine going `dunga, dunga, dunga', the word serving to indicate the object is useless. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,41</i>)

>  There would be a few "old dungers" parked at stations around New Zealand, he said. <i>(Oct 17, 2003,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

>  What we learnt was: people often want to sell their car, and usually it's because they want the money; sometimes they don't sell it straight away and often they don't get as much money as they want; and sometimes you buy an old dunga cheaply and do it up. <i>(May 5, 1999,Capital Times,00</i>) [NOTES: Check page]

>  The judge then asked about the 18 "dunger" vehicles registered in Gray's name. <i>(May 2, 2003,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  It's not quite the Orient Express but the Tranzalpine is up-market by New Zealand Railway standards. Quite a change after some of the old, outdated dungers that New Zealanders put up with when they travelled by rail. <i>(Feb 1989,North & South,18</i>)

>  "I didn't want it to look like a dunga," he says. <i>(Oct 12, 1999,Independent Herald (Wellington),7</i>)

>  You were either one of us -caring, leftwing types who drove old dungers - or you were one of them... <i>(Jan 29, 2006,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),20</i>)

>  Held just before Christmas the lads prepare for demolition day finessing their beaten up dungers into race-ready machines by taking chainsaws to the tyres to get traction, and getting the whole family to apply the appropriate war paint to the chariot. <i>(Oct 28, 2005,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Mr Dewes has purchased "an old dunger" tractor, a rotary hoe and a potato digger from his own funds, but says the return on his investment has been more than achieved. <i>(May 2005,Kokiri Paetae,16</i>)

>  He is seen driving a dunger of a girl's car instead of the flash rental he showed off on the way to visit Boogie. <i>(Jun 5, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

>  The reason, quite patently, is that Tranz Rail always buys big dunger ferries that carry trains to span its main trunk line. <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  The All Black machine - and we're talking the off-field money-making one here, not the on-field dunger that has clearly misfired in recent times - is churning out the money like a printing press. <i>(Nov 3, 2000,Evening Post,32</i>)

>  She gingerly climbed on to the back of a sulky - if you could call it that. It was a real "dunger", made from rusty pipe and borrowed, like her ropey old harness. <i>(Nov 12, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B16</i>)

>  We had to take out a commercial motor vehicle policy on our old dunger of a station wagon because we used it to carry all the boys' equipment. <i>(May 1980,In Touch (Wellington),7</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (informal) a (big old) surfboard

>  In hindsight, the two boards I owned - the first an oversized "goat boat" or "dunger" in surfer-speak - seem secondary to the real, standard surfer equipment: the roof-racks. <i>(Feb 29, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),4</i>)

>  He learnt to swim at Te Mahia school and his Dad taught him to surf on "a big old dunger of a board". <i>(Aug 15, 2004,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,11</i>)

### dungery
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  There was much underdog-style rejoicing when the government decided to fatten up the Army, after its years of short-sheeted service overseas with dungery old utes and pre-war radios. <i>(Sep 8, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### dungy
 
 <i>adj.</i> covered or spattered with dung

>  So the fingers plucking at the sub-standard wool are high speed, precision instruments flashing in on some fluffy leg wool here or a piece of dungy crutching there and flicking it off to the appropriate bin. <i>(Jun 1996,Mana,No.13: 60</i>)

### dunite
 
 <i>n.</i> a mineral from Mt Dun in Nelson; a NZ mineral, a kind of olivine [See cit. 1883. [chk Hochstetter] (geologists' ca1946 p.c. Prof. R. Clarke (Victoria University) Probably the most well-known of NZ-named minerals.']

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> [Dun Mountain, Nelson] consists of a very peculiar kind of rock, of a yellowish-green colour when recently broken, but turning rusty-brown on the surface when decomposing. The mass of the rock is olivine ...; it is distinguished from serpentine for which it was formerly taken, especially by its greater hardness, and its crystalline structure. I have called it Dunite. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),474</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> Chrome ore. This ore, which is a mixture of chromic iron and alumina, is chiefly associated with magnesian rock, resembling olivine in composition, named Dunite by Dr. Hochstetter.' <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,56</i>)

>  This mineral is a massive variety of olivine through which grains of chromite are scattered. A specimen from the Dun Mountain, Nelson, was first described by Dr V. Hochstetter (New Zealand), 1863, Eng ed, p475) ... <i>(1883,TrNZI,15/392</i>) [NOTES: (S.H. Cox)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Dunite ... an ore in New Zealand, so called from Dun mountain, near Nelson. <i>(1898,Austral English,132</i>)

>  The less important [is] ... near the celebrated Milford Sound, in Fiordland. This locality is remarkable owing to the presence with the platinum of that peculiar nickel-iron mineral awaruite. In appearance it is not unlike platinum, and was mistaken by the miners for that mineral. ... Both the metal and the awaruite occur in a district composed ... of serpentine and dunite. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/451</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> His [Dr. Ferdinand R. Von Hockstetter's] name `dunite' for the olivine rock of Dun Mountain is firmly established. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/92</i>)

>  dunite n. N.Z. iron ore. [from Dun Mountains, near Nelson N.Z.]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

>  A large Sydney firm is at present interesting itself in the deposits of dunite, a mineral containing copper, on Dun Mountain in the Nelson district. <i>(May 19, 1939,Taranaki Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>CHAPPLE</b> Above Nelson in 1851 the Austrian geologist Ferdinand Hochstetter held a piece of heavy red rock in his hand and named it dunite. <i>(2002,Te Araroa: the New Zealand Trail,1,71 ed.</i>)

>  They described their finds and gave them names based on the local geography - dunite from Dun Mountain, and the semi-precious Rodingite from the Roding River. <i>(1990,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,16</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a children's game

West Coast: Dunk and Davey, Spang Weazling; <i>Ibid.</i> 264 Other games are Humpty Dumpty (known as Dunk and Davey in the 1920s), Kick the Tin, and Hot Rice. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,231</i>)

### dunny
 
 <i>n.</i> lavatory, toilet, loo [ca1910 at least W.H.B. Orsman vide the `Old King Cole' rhyme extant when he was a schoolboy. A version of the rhyme had been applied earlier to one Blackburn, the Havelock nightcartman. Old King Cole was a merry old soul/ And a merry old soul was he;/ He called for a light in the middle of the night/ To go to the W.C./ The wind blew round the dunny door,/ The candle took a fit,/ Old King Cole fell down the hole/ And came up covered in shit.]

>  <b>GEE</b> I wrote a poem about him squatting on the dunny ceiling watching us shit. <i>(1992,Going West,153</i>)

>  <b>CURNOW</b> How small/ Are the terrible doors; how sad the dunny/ And the things you drew on the wall. <i>(1962,Small Room with Large Windows, A,4</i>) [NOTES: 1941 Curnow `Country School' in 1962 A Small Room with Large Windows 4]

>  The outside dunny, coarse grass and our chance to sleep in the tent. <i>(Dec 1996,North & South,104</i>)

>  <b>IRELAND</b> In the 1930s there were still outside dunnies in many suburbs of Auckland, some with weekly nightsoil collections, and indoor lavatories were just beginning to replace them <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,58</i>)

>  Hosking, machine-gun voiced, calls adunny a dunny. <i>(May 11, 1996,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  Admittedly, he had suffered under an era of editors who said things such as, "When Muldoon goes to the dunny, I want you down there, looking up!" <i>(Nov 21, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  You can't avoid having a working relationship with the wildlife, whether it's frogs down the dunny, geckos in the rafters,.. or green ants visiting in the night. <i>(Jan 9, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

>  If the dunnies at parliament could only talk, what tales they'd tell - of those who never wash their hands; forget to flush; of the furtive puffing of leafy substances. <i>(Mar 19, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A11</i>)

>  At least they could hold forth for hours at a time on the subject of the things they went without, the first fridge they ever saw, when dunnies went indoors, ingenious ways with left-over bottle tops and that lovely Lord Cobham, the former governor-general whose speeches they so adored. <i>(Jul 7, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A11</i>)

>  While other airlines are dropping from fortune's skies like bluebottles in a sprayed dunny, Qantas alone is flying high. <i>(Oct 20, 2001,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  I said 'Follow your nose,' and of course he ended up in the dunnies didn't he. <i>(Jun 23, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  Loose forwards and other crafty bastards will ensure they are in the dunny when the money is collected and miraculously re-appear when the beer arrives. <i>(Sep 10, 2003,Capital Times,17</i>)

>  Who, by then, will be able to tell whether TVNZ was heading down the dunny anyway? <i>(Feb 26, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  The great mystery is how they persuade these people to go on national television and have their most intimate business outside the dunny and the bedroom so ruthlessly exposed. <i>(May 27, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),71</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> She delighted in giving cheek to the boys and taking refuge in the girls' dunny. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,iii 19</i>)

>  Three models contemplating the rigours of life on the road while posed against the wall of a public dunny in 1963 is another classic. <i>(Mar 29, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  The name is apt enough, for the decayed little <i>kainga</i> sits near the tip of a long flat stretching out southward from the peninsula; ... <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 23</i>)

>  Hide went to the dunny, at the same time as a union official and, according to Hide, the official asked - presumably because the urinal was extremely small - whether Hide minded if he followed him in. <i>(Mar 22, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Dragons, yellow scaly ones, from an old black-covered Whitcombe and Tombs book, lurked to devour small boys at the lavatory or "codge". Why codge instead of dunny I have never known. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,11</i>)

>  The country's most disgusting long drops, most beautiful dunnies and the people who use them are to feature in a new television documentary. <i>(Oct 2, 2000,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  ...the thought of Jeanette Fitzsimmons involved in a climactic consummation doesn't bear thinking about, does it? Such an act might shake the self-composting Coromandel dunny to its very foundations. <i>(Aug 17, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Please don't burn our dunny down / Father has promised to pay... / If you burn our dunny down / We'll all have to read in the yard. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,197</i>) [NOTES: c 1936]

>  Helen Wilson plays dunny patron one. <i>(Aug 4, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F7</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> There were the sad little dunnies with bracken growing high around them. <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,273</i>)

>  [Hdg] Men & Gentlemen. An Investigation into the Condition of the Auckland Public Dunny. <i>(Oct 1982,Metro,99</i>)

>  New Zealand is still the land of the milk-can dunny. <i>(Sep 28, 1992,Salient,32</i>)

>  Number eight (4mm thick) wire formed kitchen implements, latched a million dunny doors, and dried the nation's laundry. <i>(Aug 28, 1992,Dominion,23</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> I always scrub the dunny after I've been, that's why the seat's always up. <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,103</i>)

>  A trestle or two across the road; an outdoor dunny as a sentry box; and vigilant Whangamomonian guards demanding $2 for the passport they're about to issue me with. <i>(Nov 20, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  Poor Nats. For five years it's been their boast that only they could give New Zealand high-level access to Washington, that only Bolger would get to use the White House dunny. <i>(Mar 26, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  On the Beehive's seventh floor, province of senior Cabinet ministers. the toilets have been declared "MPs only", forcing staff to use dunnies on lower floors. <i>(Apr 3, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  But is the party really down the dunny, or just lurking in the S-bend ready to spring back? <i>(May 23, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Thus, to go beyond the several examples already given in the book, cack and dunnekin survive in New Zealand as well as Pitcairnese (though in New Zealand, as in Australia, the shorter dunny is much commoner than dunnekin now), but presumably these are independent survivals. <i>(1968,Anglia,86/359</i>)

>  dunny: toilet <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  dunny: loo <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> From veneration inside, the catalogue in time passed outside, to hang from a nail in the dunny, now but a grey gaunt skeleton a little further up the hillside but still commanding the same commendable view. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,35</i>)

>  <b>LYNN</b> One local builder ... gave instructions to one of his old carpenters to build the dunney for a job, ... The old Privy ... WC ... Lavatory ... Dunney ... Netty ... <i>(1982,Under the Lynnwood Tree,125</i>)

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> There were no flush toilets, only the `Old Dunny' in the backyard, both in town and country with the only difference that a vehicle called a night cart called in town in latter years, ... <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,65</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The dunny was still in the same place, ... but was now hidden ... by a prolific banana-passionfruit bearing vine. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,140</i>)

>  dunny n. NZ & Austral. sl. lavatory; an outside earth closet. [abbr. of Eng. cant dunnakin] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,233</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 346; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 150]]

>  <b>SLIGO</b> She heard the door to the [outside] dunny open and shut. <i>(1987,Final Things,179</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> For a different visual encounter we had only to visit the lavatory (our family called it the `lav', others preferred the terms dunny or outhouse) outside ... <i>(1987,Swagger on Our Doorstep,43</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> dunny toilet, a shortened form of English wotrd `dunnyken', a night cart, from `danna', shit. Among many calls upon the word in c/ps: - swings like a dunny door cricket term for a free-swinging and perhaps inept batsman. ANZ. Refer `bangs like a dunny door'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,41</i>)

>  dunny (outside toilet) <i>Ibid.</i> 6 I hated it there. My first time I hid in the dunny for about an hour. But she usually gave me a good feed - savs or snarlers, and sometimes a whole chip of strawberries. And fizzy drink instead of cordial. <i>(1988,University Entrance English,3</i>)

>  Dunny N.Z outdoor toilet <i>(1989,Chosen Place,104</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> `What're dunny holes?' ... `Dunny holes are loos. They used to have them in the olden days,' ... <i>(1986,Shooting Through,42</i>)

>  <b>GEE</b> When she caught me hiding in the hedge by the dunny where he was trumpeting away like a Salvation Army band she slapped my face. <i>(1972,In My Father's Den,19</i>)

 <i>n.</i> the housing for an outdoor privy

 <i>n.</i>

>  Chatterton passed a hand over his own stiff, grey hair. A dunny brush, he'd heard it called. <i>(Jul 25, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),49</i>) [NOTES: (Marshall A Day With Yestermen)Also in 1989 Marshall Divided World 12]

 <i>n.</i> toilet paper
