### dunny rose
 
 <i>n.</i> a trailing rose grown to hide or beautify an outdoor dunny.

>  <b>FRAME</b> They had a nice trellis-work too, with dunny roses growing up it. <i>(1951,Lagoon, The,55,1961 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [My Cousin's]]

>  <b>FRAME</b> I drew aside the curtains by `Dad's seat' and looked out along the path to the cypress tree and the old dunny with its dunny roses spilling in a mass of white buds over the corrugated tin-hat roof. <i>(1985,Envoy from Mirror City,167</i>)

>  Let alone the joy when it's your go at last and you can "swing high as the dunny roses"... <i>(Sep 17, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),108</i>)

>  Dunny rose, / Macrocarpa hedge, / and the orchards / where my girl-mother / picked apples / through hot summers / have gone. <i>(Feb 7, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> Along the path from the lean-to, the 'dunny' beside the cypress tree and soon known as 'the cypress', was covered with the small fragrant roses known to us as 'dunny roses', and was without a door, while the dunny seat, long and wide like a beach dunny, was set over a deep hole half-filled with old 'kiki' (our word for faeces) in varying shades of brown, topped with faded pieces of newspaper, the <i>Oamaru Mail</i> and the <i>Otago Daily Times</i>. <i>(1984,Angel at My Table, An,51</i>)

### dunnyhouse
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [dunny](dou-dyn.md#dunny))

>  ... its window revealing a red-painted dunnyhouse under the macrocarpas. <i>(1983,Landfall,147/124</i>)

### durbrain
 
 <i>n.</i> a silly or useless person; a fool [?related to smoking derbs and supposed effect on brain]. [1984 17 F E59 Pakuranga Coll. 1 DURBRAIN [a silly person] white buds over the corrugated tin-hat roof. Cf also dunny b. attrib.]

### durry
 
 <i>v.</i> to smoke (illicitly) [(schoolboys') 1960 18C M B11 Nelson Boys C. 21 DURRY [TO SMOKE A CYGARETTE]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a roll-your-own cigarette [1987 Dr C. Corne p.c. Shakespeare Tavern, Auckland a rolled cigarette, a derb 1984 16 F E30 Pakuranga Coll. 33 DURREY [CIGARETTE] -->1984 16 M E81 Pakuranga Coll. 33 DURRY [M3] -->1984 17 M E112Pakuranga Coll. 33 DURRY [SMOKING] -->1984 17 F E54 Pakuranga Coll. 33 DURRY: HAVE A DURRY [M2] -->1984 17 M E107Pakuranga Coll. 33 DURRY: HAVE A DURREE -->1984 17 M E115Pakuranga Coll. 33 DURRYING [F2M1]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> He had worked out of Christchurch ... he said as he [120] carefully rolled a durry. <i>(1985,G'day Country,119</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> durry cigarette, usually roll-your-own, the making often called durries, perhaps associated with Bull Durham brand tobacco. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,41</i>)

>  <b>MARRINER</b> 'Keep the durries; I reckon you'll be needing them more than me.' <i>(2001,Stonedogs,332</i>)

>  There was I, watching King of the Hill, blowing smoke rings and feeling lower than a snake's belly on account of recently falling prey to the durry again, and there on the screen were Hank and wide Peggy, admonishing son Bobby who'd just been caught smoking his first cigarette. <i>(Oct 24, 1998,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  Simple rules for survival: no pimping (telling tales), no stealing another's durries (smokes), no chatting up another guy's girlfriend, and no pooftahs. <i>(Apr 2000,North & South,130</i>)

>  His brother George VI was a decent enough stick who, with the aid of a strong wife (the centenarian Queen mother), and several packs of durries a day, got us safely through World War 2. <i>(May 9, 2001,Capital Times,2</i>)

>  "Got a durry?" <i>(Aug 14, 2000,Salient,No. 17 : 19</i>)

### dusky
 
 <i>n.</i> short for dusky dolphin q.v.

>  [I] am suddenly the focus of attention for three of the little duskys who are swimming aroundand under me... At one stage we see a group of four duskys mating. <i>(Jan 17, 1994,Evening Post,11</i>)

### dusky beech
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Nothofagus fusca</i>

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Nothofagus fusca</i> Dusky beech <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

### dusky dolphin
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Lagenorhynchus obscurus</i>

>  The inquisitive dusky dolphins would come to investigate the divers and play with them. <i>(Jul 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 58/33</i>)

>  Without searching, we were surrounded by hundreds of dusky dolphins. <i>(Jul 11, 2004,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,10</i>)

>  The dusky dolphin (<i>Lagenorhynchus obscurus</i>) is perhaps as plentiful round the coast of New Zealand as the common porpoise. It can be distinguished from it by the falcate dorsal fin and the more pointed snout. It is black above and white below, with an oblique white band on the flanks. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/140</i>) [NOTES: (Oliver)]

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> DUSKY DOLPHIN ... <i>Other names: Lagenorhynchus obscurus. </i> <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,304</i>)

>  To the south and further out are the larger and more acrobatic Dusky dolphins. <i>(Feb 1982,North & South,10</i>)

### dusky plover
 
 <i>n.</i> (<i>Charadrius obscurus</i>) New Zealand dotterel, tuturuwaka

>  <b>COLENSO</b> Here ... the Dusky Plover (<i>Charadrius obscurus</i>) and the Southern Godwit ... were in large flocks. The natives call the former, Tuturuwaka [check ?-wata]. [1959 ed fn] Dusky Plover or New Zealand Dotterel ... <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Colenso Excurs Northern Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 44]

>  61. <i>Charadrius obscurus</i>, Gml. Dusky, Red-breasted, or Mountain Plover, Big Dottrel [si], Tuturiwatu, Paturiwhata, Tituriwhatu-pukunui. - This excellent gamebird formerly bred on the Canterbury Plains ...; since farms and settlements have occupied the waste lands of the plains, for the most part it has retired to the mountains of the back country. <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

 <i>n.</i> = sooty shearwater? (Puffinus griseus, the muttonbird)

### dust
 
 <i>n.</i> the dust, miners' phthisis or silicosis.

>  <b>McARA</b> Dust: Quartz dust was a major menace to the health of miners. It blocked up the microscopic air-sacs in the lungs, producing hardening of the tissues and impairing the function of the lungs. Eventually, sometimes after many years, death resulted. The disease miners' phthisis was commonly called `the dust.' It gradually came to an end after the indroduction of axial-water-feed drills. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),320</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> gunpowder [(whalers' ?technical jargon)]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Dust `gunpowder' <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/318</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> gold-dust

>  The gold is in dust and nuggets. ... The implement most effectual in economising labour is the `Long Tom', or trough strainer, cradles are but of little comparative service. <i>(Dec 10, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/30/183</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy's Report)]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> The dust consisted of flaky gold, of a pale lemon colour, largely intermixed with auriferous quartz, the separation having been effected only by washing in the simplest manner. <i>Ibid.</i> 109 These parties also brought up from the same spot various other specimens; one parcel of which, consisting of large rich dust and flakes, and exceedingly auriferous quartz (perhaps as rich, gaudy specimens as have yet been seen), was sold by auction by the undersigned yesterday. <i>(1853,Auckland,106</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Dec 13 Gold Circular in 1853 Swainson Auckland 106]

>  <b>HYDE</b> Hadn't so much as a pick, he hadn't, and when he came down again he was carrying his dust in a sugar-sack on his back. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,54</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a kauri-gum grade, gum-dust

>  The chief factor influencing sales and prices of kauri-gum in recent years has been the rquest for the poorer qualities, called `chips' and `dust'. <i>(Aug 1916,Chambers Edinburgh Journal,542/1</i>) [NOTES: [OED2 chip]]

>  <b>STALLWORTHY</b> At one time the dust, and scrapings, of the gum were not saleable ... Since then there has always been a price of some sort for all dust and scrapings. <i>(1916,Early Northern Wairoa,54</i>)

>  ... it is no doubt also responsible for the dark coloured varnishes produced from the chip grades. [fn] The terms dust, seeds, chips, nubs, &c., are the names given to certain of the lower kauri gum grades according to the relative sizes of the pieces. From `dust' the size of the fragments increases in the order given, as does also the purity of the grade. Chalk, or `chalky gum,' is a particular grade recovered from swampy country and is characterized by its white or chalky appearance and large water content. <i>(1936,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,17/371</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> An increasing demand for poorer grades of gum [after 1900], used in making linoleum, made it profitable to search for smaller gum: `nuts', `chips', `seeds', and `dust'. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/207</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> The [1920 Kauri Gum Industry] report stated that during the preceding few years the proportion of low-grade gum, chips and dust (scrapings) had increased in volume ... to about two-thirds of the total gum exports. <i>(1972,Gumdiggers, The,82</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> The scrapings [sc. of gum] were kept and sold as 'dust', the bigger pieces were known as 'nuts' and the biggest as 'bold'. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,110</i>)

>  In order of decreasing size, the pieces of gum were known as nuggets, nubs, peas, chips, and dust. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,41</i>) [NOTES: check whether "nuts" or "nubs"]

### dusted
 
 <i>adj.</i> suffering from silicosis [miners']

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The greatest occupational hazard of mining was the dust. ... Drilling was all done dry in those days. The air was laden with particles of flint and quartz, ground to the consistency of flour. ... Once a man was `dusted', there was no cure. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,5</i>)

### duster
 
 <i>n.</i> a thief

>  Then they started their series of thefts. ...I notice that there has been a lot of `dusters' working over there recently, but these men are the first we have got of them. <i>(Nov 2, 1912,NZ Truth,384/6/2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an aerial topdresser

>  Country Calendar Bruce has been a duster for years. <i>(May 5, 1982,TV1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a blackboard eraser

>  <b>LELAND</b> Duster. When you were a kid did your teacher ever give you the job of taking the BLACKBOARD ERASERS outside and beating them together creating great interesting clouds of chalk dust? Things are no different in New Zealand, except they are called dusters. This term is also used for dustcloths. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,36</i>)

### dustie
 
 <i>n.</i> rubbish collector, dustman

>  Ferocious dogs, dangerous sliver of glass ... are obstacles faced every day by men of the Wellington rubbish carts. But anyone who thinks these rubbish men (or dustmen or `dusties' as they're sometimes called) don't earn their money, should spend a morning with a crew. <i>(May 17, 1975,Evening Post,93</i>)

>  DUSTIES SEEK SUPPORT. Wellington City Council refuse collectors are petitioning the public ... <i>(Feb 2, 1982,Dominion,3</i>)

>  Wellington dusties returned to work yesterday morning after reaching agreement with the city council on a dispute that stopped rubbish collections for two days. <i>(Jul 9, 1986,Dominion,4</i>)

>  `Excuse me,' Susan broke in, `would somebody mind telling me what a garbo is?' `Sorry Susie. What they say in Oz. A dustie,' Nicholas explained. <i>(1987,Islands,38/133</i>) [NOTES: (Beveridge)]

>  I'd seen you all at the pub I had taken to frequenting, the local Maori crim, bus drivers', dusties' pub. <i>(1987,Metro Fiction,6</i>) [NOTES: (Keogh, Chiaroscuro)]

>  No one told me that the dusties collectedthe rubbish around the back. Early. Andno one told the dusties that a whiterobed flailer was not a kehua, a ghost. <i>(Jun 8, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  Dusties will stop collecting rubbish from the back door of the house from 1 October. <i>(Jul 8, 1993,City Voice,1</i>)

>  [Hdg] Former dustie switches to sales... After a brief spell studying mechanical engineering at night school, then driving trucks.. [he] looked round for something more challenging. <i>(Sep 7, 2000,Evening Post,Real Estate Suppl. 21</i>)

>  Everyone left their full cans at the edge of the footpath for the men we called the 'dusties'. <i>(Aug 2005,NZ Memories,55 / 29</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [(Boer War spec or local)]

 <i>n.</i> <i>Senecio cineraria</i> [Wild, exotic but fully naturalised as per nzflora.landcareresearch.co.nz]

### Dutch oven
 
 <i>n.</i> a camp oven

 <i>n.</i> a Dutch treat (See [also Parnell shout](../A#also-parnell-shout),[Yankee shout](y-yc.md#yankee-shout))

### Dutchie
 
 <i>n.</i> A Dutchman, also a German or other middle European [as DNZE, but in SOED (Dutchy) so not a NZism]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Mr. Jock Anderson, ..[116]. , arranged to met [sic] a new-Dutchman, ... As he came onto a small bluff he called out. The Dutchie responded ... <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,115</i>)

### Dutchman
 
 <i>n.</i> In early NZ applied as elsewhere to any North European, esp. to a sailor. Also as an appellation or nickname (Dutch Charlie, Dutch Harry, etc.). Compare Scandy.

>  Many expressed regret when the `blasted Dutchman' succeeded in clearing his name. The tendency to call every foreigner `a Dutchman' is well known and amazing. <i>(May 29, 1909,NZ Truth,205/5/2</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Take `Dutch Harry' for instance. His name was Henry Wickson (or Wixon), and it was said he was an Austrian and came from Memel. ... To the whalers any foreigner was Dutch so that is how he got his nickname.] <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,47</i>)

>  <b>EADDY</b> Quite a number of the trading scows were manned wholly with Scandinavians. They were very clannish, these people, and should a `Dutchman' as they were always dubbed, be appointed master of any scow, generally speaking, that vessel would eventually be manned entirely by `Dutchmen.' <i>(1939,Neath Swaying Spars,26</i>)

### Duvaucel's gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) NZ's largest remaining gecko, now limited to island populations. Long-lived, sometimes exceeding 40 years. [<i>Hoplodactylus duvaucelii</i>]

>  [Caption] Among the 14 or so species of reptile on Little Barrier are Duvaucel's gecko (at up to 25 cm, including the tail, one of the largest geckos in the world), the very rare chevron skink (found only on Little and Great Barrier Islands), and the Pacific gecko... <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/13</i>)

>  Conservator Rob McCallum said the extermination of rats would lead to a dramatic return of some wildlife, including tuatara. lizards, insects, giant wet and Duvaucel's gecko, and a threatened burrowing seabird, the Cook's petrel. <i>(Jun 11, 2004,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

>  <b>Jenny Jones</b> Named after an early French naturalist in 1836, Duvaucel's gecko is a brown gecko. It is the largest New Zealand species. <i>(2002,Lizards,28</i>)

>  Indeed, rare species such as Pycroft's petrel, Duvaucel's gecko and the New Zealand native cucumber are all showing signs of regaining their foothold on these islands. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,10</i>)

Sometimes they join together to form intricate chandeliers, curtains and "dwarf beards" - waterfalls of white rock frozen in mid-air. <i>(Apr 1997,NZ Geographic,No. 34/34</i>)

These plants were the island yam (Dioscorea esculenta), the bottle gourd, the taro, a dwarf type of cabbage tree and the kumara. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,3</i>)

### dwarf mistletoe
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Environment) one of three dwarf or leafless semi-parasitic indigenous mistletoes <i>Korthalsella salicornioides</i>, <i> Korthalsella lindsayi</i>, or <i> Korthalsella clavata </i>. (See [all entries for mistletoe](../A#all-entries-for-mistletoe),[Maori mistletoe](../M#maori-mistletoe),[white mistletoe](../W#white-mistletoe),[etc](../E#etc))

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Korthalsella salicornioides</i> DWARF MISTLETOE ... Tiny green to yellow-green, jointed leafless parasite forming tufts or bunches on twigs of manuka. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,62</i>)

>  Dwarf mistletoes are found mostly on coprosma, kanuka and manuka. They often go unnoticed because they are so small, but they are distributed throughout the country. <i>(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 59/60</i>) [NOTES: Check ref.]

 <i>n.</i> <i>Montia verna</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> DWARF MONTIA (<i>Montia fontana </i>spp. <i>chondrosperma</i>). This European plant is widespread in New Zealand, and present in many pastures, ... <i>(1980,Weeds of Crops,40</i>)

### dwarf pine
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka dwarf-pine, pygmy pine; <i>Lepidothamnus laxifolius</i> previously <i>Dacryidium laxifolius</i>]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> The Dwarf-pine (<i>Dacrydium laxifolium</i>), which on the dry pumice soil has assumed the cushion-form. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,104</i>)

### DWB
 
 <i>abbr.</i> driving while black [ORIGIN: acronym] (See [driving while black](dou-dyn.md#driving-while-black))

### dwell time
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Capacity on the same piece of port land varies depending on how high containers are stacked and how long each container "dwells" or stays at a port. It inceases if containers are stacked higher or if dwell time is shorter. <i>(Oct 24, 2005,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

>  When Ms Elton joined CentrePort the average dwell time (time on the wharf) for a container was 15 days. <i>(Aug 20, 2001,NZ Infotech Weekly,15</i>)

### dwelling dancer
 
 <i>n.</i> [Compare stairdancer.]

>  <b>PARTIDGE</b> A burglar, esp. a transient lodger that pillages boarding-houses:Australian: since ca. 1910. Baker 1945. Cf. dancer, 6,of which it is prob. an elaboration - and an alteration insense. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,218</i>)

### DWF
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Distribution Workers Federation

 <i>n.</i>

>  Speaking of Norman Kirk, Bob Tizard said "At times he was perfectly lucid [in Cabinet]; at other times he would wander off into a fit of the dwindles". <i>(Aug 31, 1999,Radio NZ / National Radio,Kim Hill Show</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [eyebright](ens-eye.md#eyebright))

### dyer's weed
 
 <i>n.</i> mignonette

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> MIGNONETTE (Reseda luteola) is called wild mignonette, scentless mignonette, giant mignonette and dyer's weed. It is not uncommon in fields and waste places in both islands. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,96,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>dyer's weed, [=] wild mignonette [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,23</i>)

### dyke
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining; aka dike]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> In the detritus on the sides of the mountains, and in the beds of the stream, quartz occurs in detached blocks of various sizes, mixed with fragments of slate, granite and trap; but regulardykes of quartz are not generally met with, although they are reported to exist. <i>(1853,Auckland,91</i>)

>  DYKE.- A wall-like intrusion of igneous rock into a fissure. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,54</i>)

>  Near Carricktown, however, several dyke-like outcrops were found, locally known to the miners as `dykes'. They are not dykes, but outcrops of hard, bluish-grey, flinty or horny quartz, often much slickensided on one wall. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.5 (NS) 29</i>) [NOTES: (Park, Cromwell)]

>  One vertical dyke of solid whitish stone of moderate hardness, cuts the line of the tunnel at an oblique angle, and I have seen some indications of a second intersecting dyke. <i>(Mar 9, 1859,Lyttelton Times,3</i>)

>  Another feature analogous to this coal-field is, that in this hill is a dyke, apparently descending vertically through the strata, of a beautiful conglomerate of slate and quartz, and which I have never before seen at this elevation. <i>(Apr 10, 1863,Colonist, The</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a privy [aka dike]

>  A `john' means a cop, not a toilet, and the [38] latter is sometimes called (between men only) a `dyke' or `house of parliament'. <i>(1944,US War Dept. Short Guide to NZ,37</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> But the dyke's in the bathroom. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,74</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> I have heard it suggested that ... the vulgar dyke for `urinal' is used in Ireland, ... <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,11</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Everything from a light bulb to the wringer to the ball-cock in the dyke. <i>(1978,Glory and the Dream, The,196</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> The new guitar of sex I kept on twanging/ Inside the iron virgin/ Of the little smelly dyke, ... <i>(1979,Collected Poems,250,1962 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (1962) 1979 Baxter Collected Poems 250]

>  They make you put your head in a tuck locker. Or go in the Drying Room ... If you stay on the dike you can not [sic] be tested. <i>(1972,Islands,1/14</i>) [NOTES: Henderson (Freedom's Ramparts)]

>  A corrupt man can sit on a dike and watch [27] a tree beginning to blossom in the yard ... <i>(1972,Islands,1/26</i>) [NOTES: Baxter (Elegy for Boyle Crescent)]

### dynamite pill
 
 <i>n.</i> Cascara sagrada (three-grain) tablet takento ease constipation, Compare depth-charge. [ca1941 St Patrick's College, Silverstream. ca1941 Trentham Military camp.]

### dyno
 
 <i>n.</i> dynamite

>  dyno: dynamite <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: Slang Words in Use (Goldie Brown Collection)]
